### [1] [Audio Sentiment Analysis via Distillation and Cross-Modal Integration of Generated Multilingual Transcripts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06611)
*Andrei-George Durdun,Victor Constantinescu,Radu Tudor Ionescu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A multimodal sentiment analysis system combining audio with automatically transcribed and translated text via cross-modal transformers, with knowledge distillation to a lightweight audio-only model, achieving strong performance gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: It is unclear whether audio foundation models alone can fully capture sentiment, which depends on both vocal inflections and linguistic content. The authors investigate whether automatically generated textual information (transcripts and translations) can complement audio features for robust sentiment polarity classification.

Method: A cascaded cross-modal transformer architecture that fuses audio features with multiple text modalities: (1) ASR-generated transcripts, and (2) machine-translated versions in multiple languages. Cross-modal transformer blocks integrate modalities sequentially. Knowledge distillation transfers the multimodal teacher's capabilities into a unimodal (audio-only) student model, enabling inference-time efficiency.

Result: Automatically generated textual information significantly boosts multimodal sentiment polarity classification on a large-scale dataset. Ablations confirm both ASR transcripts and machine translations contribute positively. The distilled audio-only student model matches or approaches multimodal performance without computational overhead at inference.

Conclusion: Augmenting audio sentiment analysis with automatically generated multilingual text via cross-modal fusion and distillation yields a practical, high-performance system. The distilled audio-only model offers an efficient deployment path while retaining the benefits of multimodal training.

Abstract: Automatically recognizing the sentiment, positive or negative, from speech is a challenging task, requiring both the analysis of vocal inflections and the interpretation of uttered words. Recent solutions rely on audio foundation models to solve the task, but it remains unclear if such models can take all aspects into account. To this end, we propose a multimodal solution that integrates audio and text information via cross-modal transformers, where text transcripts are automatically generated via an automatic speech recognition (ASR) tool. Moreover, we create multiple text modalities by automatically translating the transcripts into multiple languages via machine translation tools. Audio and multilingual text features are combined via a cascaded architecture comprising cross-modal transformer blocks that integrate modalities one by one. We further distill knowledge from the multimodal model, called teacher, into a unimodal (audio only) model, called student. We conduct experiments on a large-scale dataset, demonstrating that the automatically generated textual information can bring significant performance boosts in multimodal sentiment polarity classification. Our ablation study confirms that both automatic transcripts and automatic translations are helpful. Moreover, we show that the audio-only model can be enhanced via distillation, boosting performance without any computational overhead during inference. To reproduce the reported results, we publicly release our code at https://github.com/andreidurdun/cross-modal-audio-sentiment.

</details>


### [2] [Healthier LLMs: Retrieval-Augmented Generation for Public Health Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06641)
*Felix Feldman,Joshua Harris,Timothy Laurence,Leo Loman,Ollie Higgins,Fan Grayson,Poonam Soma,Bethany Pace-Bonello,Michael Borowitz,Toby Nonnenmacher*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper extends PubHealthBench into a retrieval-augmented setting, systematically evaluating retrieval configurations and generation quality for public health QA. Hybrid retrieval consistently improves recall/ranking, and providing retrieved context boosts multiple-choice accuracy, enabling small open-weight LLMs to rival larger models. A rubric-based LLM-as-a-judge for free-form answers is introduced and validated against human annotations, with findings that retrieval is the primary lever for reliable, guidance-grounded public health QA.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs perform well on medical QA benchmarks but suffer from hallucinations and cannot keep pace with rapidly evolving public health guidance. RAG offers a solution by grounding responses in a maintained corpus, but its end-to-end performance depends heavily on retrieval configuration and on evaluation methods that go beyond multiple-choice formats—both underexplored in public health contexts.

Method: The authors extend the PubHealthBench dataset (7,929 UK government public health questions) into a RAG setting. They systematically compare dense, sparse, and hybrid retrieval across multiple embedding models and corpus variants (varying chunk length and topic). Multiple-choice accuracy is evaluated across diverse LLMs with and without retrieved context. For free-form evaluation, they introduce a rubric-based LLM-as-a-judge covering faithfulness, completeness, clarity, and factual consistency, validated against dual human annotations.

Result: Hybrid retrieval consistently improves recall and ranking quality, with chunk length and topic interacting with ranking performance. Retrieved context substantially increases multiple-choice accuracy, enabling smaller open-weight models to match or surpass larger models without retrieval. Gains are driven primarily by retrieval quality and careful context selection. LLM-as-a-judge agreement with humans is strongest for faithfulness and completeness, but weaker for factual consistency and clarity.

Conclusion: Retrieval is the primary lever for reliable public health QA. The study provides practical guidance for building and evaluating RAG systems grounded in official guidance and urges caution when interpreting LLM-judge scores for factual consistency and clarity at scale.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) achieve promising results on medical question answering benchmarks, yet their use in public health is constrained by hallucinations and the rapid evolution of official guidance. Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) mitigates these risks by grounding responses in an explicitly maintained corpus, but end-to-end performance depends critically on retrieval configuration and on evaluation beyond multiple-choice formats. We extend PubHealthBench, a question answering (QA) benchmark of 7,929 questions derived from UK Government public health guidance, into a retrieval-augmented setting and systematically evaluate retrieval and generation choices. We compare dense, sparse, and hybrid retrieval across multiple embedding models and corpus variants, and show that hybrid retrieval consistently improves recall and ranking quality, with chunk length and topic interacting with ranking performance. Providing retrieved context substantially increases multiple-choice accuracy across a diverse set of LLMs, enabling smaller open-weight models to match or outperform larger models used without retrieval, with gains primarily driven by retrieval quality and careful context selection. To assess realistic free-form answering, we introduce a rubric-based LLM-as-a-judge covering faithfulness, completeness, clarity, and factual consistency, and validate it against dual human annotations. Judge-human agreement is strongest for faithfulness and completeness, while factual consistency and clarity are less reliably reproduced, motivating caution when interpreting those dimensions at scale. Overall, our results highlight retrieval as a primary lever for reliable public health QA and provide practical guidance for building and evaluating RAG systems grounded in official guidance.

</details>


### [3] [Ad Headline Generation using Self-Critical Masked Language Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06818)
*Yashal Shakti Kanungo,Sumit Negi,Aruna Rajan*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper proposes a reinforcement learning-based method using Transformer masked language models to automatically generate high-quality product advertising headlines for E-commerce, jointly conditioned on multiple products, outperforming both existing methods and human-written headlines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Building high-quality, creative advertising headlines at scale for E-commerce websites is challenging, as it is difficult to consistently meet the platform's creative quality bar with manual human effort alone.

Method: The authors apply Reinforcement Learning (RL) Policy Gradient methods on top of Transformer-based Masked Language Models. The headline generation process is jointly conditioned on multiple products that a seller wishes to advertise, enabling programmatic, scalable headline creation.

Result: The proposed method outperforms existing Transformer and LSTM + RL baselines on both overlap metrics and quality audits. Notably, model-generated headlines surpass human-submitted headlines in grammar and creative quality as judged by audits.

Conclusion: RL-augmented Transformer models provide a viable, scalable, and high-quality solution for automated advertising headline generation in E-commerce, exceeding both prior automated methods and human performance on creative quality metrics.

Abstract: For any E-commerce website it is a nontrivial problem to build enduring advertisements that attract shoppers. It is hard to pass the creative quality bar of the website, especially at a large scale. We thus propose a programmatic solution to generate product advertising headlines using retail content. We propose a state of the art application of Reinforcement Learning (RL) Policy gradient methods on Transformer based Masked Language Models. Our method creates the advertising headline by jointly conditioning on multiple products that a seller wishes to advertise. We demonstrate that our method outperforms existing Transformer and LSTM + RL methods in overlap metrics and quality audits. We also show that our model-generated headlines outperform human submitted headlines in terms of both grammar and creative quality as determined by audits.

</details>


### [4] [Gradient-Based Speech-to-Text Alignment for Any ASR Model: From CTC to Speech LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06831)
*Albert Zeyer,Ralf Schlüter,Hermann Ney*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a generic, differentiable gradient-based method for speech-to-text word alignment that works across all ASR model families (CTC, transducer, AED, speech LLMs) without training or model modification. It uses per-token input gradients to construct frame-level saliency maps, then decodes word boundaries via dynamic programming. Evaluated on 16 models across TIMIT and Buckeye, the method is universally usable, compares favorably where native alignment is weak (e.g., streaming models), and operates on the fine input grid rather than the coarser encoder grid, at the cost of one backward pass per token.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Different ASR model families handle word alignment differently: CTC/transducer models produce alignments by construction, while AED and speech LLMs lack native alignment and rely on coarse attention-weight heuristics. Moreover, all existing alignment signals reside on the encoder frame grid, limiting temporal precision. The authors aim to develop a single, generic alignment method that works across all differentiable ASR models without requiring training, architectural changes, or dedicated alignment heads, while operating at the finer input-grid resolution.

Method: The method computes the gradient of each teacher-forced token's log-probability with respect to the raw input waveform or spectrogram frames. These gradients are reduced (via L2 norm or similar aggregation) to a per-frame saliency score. The resulting saliency matrix (frames × tokens) is then decoded into word boundaries using a single dynamic-programming pass (akin to forced alignment), yielding temporal segmentations directly on the input grid without any model modification, extra heads, or training.

Result: Evaluated on 16 models spanning CTC, transducer, AED, and speech LLM families on TIMIT (read speech) and Buckeye (spontaneous speech): (1) the gradient-based alignment is usable for every model tested; (2) it is generally somewhat behind strong native aligners (e.g., CTC) but outperforms native/attention-based alignment where those are weak, particularly for streaming models; (3) it operates on the input grid, offering finer temporal resolution than encoder-grid methods. The main drawback is computational cost: one backward pass per token.

Conclusion: Gradient-based alignment is a universal, training-free, model-agnostic approach applicable to any differentiable ASR model, including speech LLMs. While it does not consistently beat the best native aligners, it provides a reliable fallback where native alignment is poor or absent, and offers superior temporal resolution by working on the input grid. The cost of one backward pass per token is its primary limitation.

Abstract: Speech-to-text alignment means finding the temporal boundaries of each word in the audio. Some models provide such an alignment directly and others do not. Connectionist temporal classification (CTC) and transducer models have an alignment by construction, whereas attention-based encoder-decoders (AED) and speech large language models (LLMs) do not, and their word timings are usually read off the attention weights instead. All of these signals live on the encoder frame grid, which bounds their temporal precision. We study a generic gradient-based alignment that applies to any differentiable ASR model. We take the gradient of each teacher-forced token log probability with respect to the input, reduce it to a per-frame saliency, and decode the resulting matrix into word boundaries with a single dynamic-programming pass. The method needs no training, no model modification and no alignment heads, works across all model families including the speech LLMs, and aligns on the input grid rather than on the coarser encoder grid. We evaluate it on sixteen models from four families, on read (TIMIT) and spontaneous (Buckeye) speech, each against the model's own native or attention-based alignment. We find that the gradient yields a usable alignment for every model, that it is usually somewhat behind a strong native aligner but better where the native alignment is weak, as for the streaming models, and that its main disadvantage is the cost of one backward pass per token.

</details>


### [5] [LLMs Silently Correct African American English: Auditing and Mitigating Dialect Bias via Activation Steering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06845)
*Huan Wu,Ali Emami,Muhammad Furquan Hassan,Osaretin Igbinoba,Osakpolor Idusuyi,Osamede Igbinoba,Faiza Khan Khattak,Laleh Seyyed-Kalantari*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: LLMs systematically rewrite African American English (AAE) into Standard American English (SAE). The authors introduce cDGI for auditing dialect bias, identify syntactic triggers (e.g., negative concord), and propose activation steering as a training-free mitigation that reduces bias 5–20× more than prompting. They also release REAL-AAE, the largest real-AAE parallel corpus.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AAE is spoken by 30+ million people yet is routinely misinterpreted and "corrected" by LLMs into SAE. This constitutes a form of dialect bias that erases linguistic identity. The authors aim to systematically audit how and where this bias manifests in instruction-tuned models and to develop an effective, training-free mitigation strategy.

Method: (1) Auditing: Conditional Dialect Group Invariance (cDGI) isolates true model bias from translator-induced artifacts. Feature-level localization analysis identifies which AAE markers (syntactic, morphological, lexical) most strongly trigger bias across models (14B–70B). (2) Mitigation: Activation steering is applied to dialect bias for the first time — a training-free, test-time method that uses causal tracing to extract dialect directions and injects them into bias-relevant layers. (3) Resource: REAL-AAE corpus of 17,479 AAE/SAE/AAE_back triplets from natural tweets, validated by BERTScore (F1=0.95) and three native AAE speakers (83% semantic agreement).

Result: Six instruction-tuned LLMs systematically prefer SAE continuations given AAE context, effectively rewriting AAE into SAE. Syntactic constructions, especially negative concord ("ain't nobody"), are universal bias triggers across all models. Activation steering reduces dialect bias 5 to 20 times more effectively than prompting while preserving SAE fluency.

Conclusion: LLMs exhibit pervasive dialect bias against AAE, particularly triggered by syntactic features. Activation steering offers a promising, efficient mitigation that substantially outperforms prompting without requiring retraining. The REAL-AAE corpus provides a foundational resource for future dialect fairness research.

Abstract: African American English (AAE), a rule-governed dialect spoken by over 30 million people, is routinely misinterpreted and "corrected" by large language models (LLMs). Across six instruction-tuned LLMs (14B to 70B), we show that state-of-the-art models systematically prefer Standard American English (SAE) continuations even when the preceding context is in AAE, effectively rewriting AAE into SAE. We present an end-to-end framework to audit and mitigate this bias. For auditing, we introduce conditional Dialect Group Invariance (cDGI), which isolates true model bias from translator-induced artifacts, and a feature-level localization analysis that identifies which AAE markers most strongly trigger bias; we find that syntactic constructions, especially negative concord (e.g., "ain't nobody"), are universal triggers across all models. For mitigation, we introduce, to our knowledge, the first application of activation steering to dialect bias: a training-free, test-time method that extracts dialect directions via causal tracing and injects them into bias-relevant layers. Activation steering reduces bias 5 to 20 times more than prompting while preserving SAE fluency. To enable this work, we release REAL-AAE , the largest real-AAE parallel corpus to date: 17,479 AAE/SAE/ AAE_back triplets from natural tweets (2 to 6 times larger than prior real-AAE resources), validated automatically (BERTScore F1 = 0.95) and by three native AAE speakers (83.0% semantic agreement).

</details>


### [6] [Riemannian Geometry for Pre-trained Language Model Embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07047)
*Szczepan Konior,Alexandre Quemy,Przemysław Klocek,Grégoire Cattan,Bartłomiej Sobieski*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Riemannian Mean Pooling (RMP) aggregates token-level pullback metrics from a language model's Jacobian using the Fréchet mean on the SPD manifold, outperforming Euclidean pooling on linguistically structured benchmarks while correctly yielding chance performance on a lexical-artifact-free dataset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether sentence-level classification signal resides in the Riemannian geometry of contextual token embeddings, and to develop a geometrically principled pooling method that exploits this structure for improved interpretability and robustness.

Method: Per-token pullback Riemannian metrics are extracted from the analytical Jacobian of a learned encoder. These SPD matrices are then aggregated via the Fréchet mean on the SPD manifold. The resulting pooled representation is used for downstream classification (CoLA, CREAK, RTE, FEVER-Symmetric). Ablations compare trained vs. randomly initialized encoders and Riemannian vs. Euclidean pooling.

Result: RMP outperforms Euclidean mean pooling on CoLA, CREAK, and RTE. On FEVER-Symmetric (designed to lack annotation artifacts), RMP stays at chance level, indicating it does not exploit spurious lexical shortcuts. Randomly initialized encoder + Fréchet aggregation already beats Euclidean pooling on 2 of 3 signal-bearing datasets, isolating the gain to geometric aggregation. Trained encoder contributes additional signal specifically on CREAK, the most knowledge-intensive dataset.

Conclusion: The Riemannian geometry of token embeddings carries useful sentence-level signal. The geometric aggregation strategy (Fréchet mean on SPD) is the primary driver of improvement, with learned manifold structure providing additional benefits on knowledge-heavy tasks.

Abstract: Understanding the geometric structure of pre-trained language model embeddings matters for interpretability and safety. We ask whether sentence-level classification signal lives in the Riemannian geometry of contextual token embeddings, and probe it by extracting per-token pullback metrics from a learned encoder's analytical Jacobian and aggregating them with the Fréchet mean on the symmetric positive definite (SPD) manifold; we call this procedure Riemannian Mean Pooling (RMP). Across three datasets with non-trivial linguistic structure (CoLA, CREAK, RTE), RMP outperforms Euclidean mean pooling, while on FEVER-Symmetric, a benchmark constructed to remove annotation-driven lexical artifacts, the method correctly stays at chance. Ablations show that a randomly initialised encoder combined with Fréchet aggregation already beats Euclidean pooling on two of the three signal-bearing datasets, localising the source of the gain to the geometric aggregation rather than to learned manifold structure; the trained encoder contributes additional signal specifically on CREAK, the most knowledge-heavy of the three signal-bearing datasets.

</details>


### [7] [Evaluating RAG Metrics in Applied Contexts: An Experiment, Its Findings and Its Limitations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07302)
*Quentin Brabant*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Empirical study evaluating the relevance of RAG evaluation metrics from four libraries (Ragas, DeepEval, RAGChecker, Opik) by comparing them against human evaluator scores and standard metrics like recall, using a QA dataset built from business data, with correlation analysis and discussion of limitations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The increasing use of Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems creates a need for reliable, validated evaluation metrics. However, it remains unclear how well existing automated RAG metrics from different libraries correlate with human judgment and standard retrieval metrics. This study aims to fill that gap by empirically assessing metric relevance in a real-world business context.

Method: A QA dataset was constructed by human annotators from business data. A RAG system's generated responses and retrieved spans were then scored using metrics from four evaluation libraries: Ragas, DeepEval, RAGChecker, and Opik. These automated scores were compared against scores from two human evaluators and standard metrics such as recall. Correlation analysis was performed to assess alignment.

Result: The paper reports the outcomes of the correlation analysis between automated RAG metrics, human evaluator scores, and standard recall metrics. Specific numerical findings are not detailed in the abstract, but the analysis identifies which metrics align well (or poorly) with human judgment and standard retrieval measures, and highlights methodological limitations.

Conclusion: The study highlights limitations of the current methodology, compares it with approaches in existing literature, and proposes avenues for future research. It emphasizes the importance of validating RAG metrics against human evaluation in applied business settings. Originally published at the French-speaking EvalLLM workshop (Brabant, 2026).

Abstract: This paper reports an empirical study evaluating the relevance of several RAG metrics. The experiment is based on a question-answering dataset created by human annotators from business data. The generated responses and retrieved spans of a RAG system are scored using evaluation metrics from four libraries (Ragas, DeepEval, RAGChecker, Opik). These metrics are compared to scores given by two evaluators, as well as to standard metrics such as recall. An analysis of correlations is conducted. Finally, we highlight certain limitations of our methodology, compare it to those used in the literature, and suggest some avenues for future research. This paper is an English translation of a paper originally published in the French-speaking workshop EvalLLM (Brabant, 2026).

</details>


### [8] [Transformer-based segmentation of prosodic boundaries in Brazilian Portuguese](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07408)
*Rodrigo de Freitas Lima,Julio Cesar Galdino,Marcos Vinicius Treviso*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: SAMPA is a Whisper-based prosodic segmenter for Brazilian Portuguese that fine-tunes Whisper large-v3 to transcribe speech with explicit terminal boundary markers, achieving F1=0.731 on NURC-SP and F1=0.796 on out-of-distribution MuPe-Diversidades data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automatic prosodic segmentation for Brazilian Portuguese (BP) lags behind English, still relying on rule-based or traditional ML methods rather than modern deep learning. The authors aim to bring state-of-the-art deep learning prosodic segmentation to BP.

Method: Fine-tuning Whisper large-v3 on manually segmented recordings from the NURC-SP dataset, with the model trained to insert explicit terminal prosodic boundary markers directly into the transcription. Various training and test-time filtering configurations are evaluated, including out-of-distribution testing on MuPe-Diversidades. N-gram and acoustic-visual analyses are used for interpretability.

Result: Best models achieve F1=0.731 on the NURC-SP held-out test split and F1=0.796 on MuPe-Diversidades. Analyses confirm the model leverages morphosyntactic, semantic, and prosodic cues for boundary detection.

Conclusion: SAMPA demonstrates that fine-tuning a large pre-trained ASR model like Whisper is an effective approach for prosodic boundary detection in Brazilian Portuguese, with strong generalization to out-of-distribution data.

Abstract: Automatic prosodic segmentation identifies boundaries between speech units from acoustic and linguistic evidence. Although recent deep learning approaches have produced strong results for English, automatic segmentation for Brazilian Portuguese (BP) still relies mostly on rule-based or traditional machine-learning methods. This paper presents SAMPA, a Whisper-based segmenter that transcribes BP speech while inserting explicit markers for terminal prosodic boundaries. We fine-tune Whisper large-v3 on manually segmented recordings from the NURC-SP dataset and evaluate different training and test-time filtering configurations, including out-of-distribution testing on the MuPe-Diversidades dataset. SAMPA achieves competitive boundary-detection performance across settings, with the best models reaching F1=0.731 on the held-out test split and F1=0.796 on MuPe-Diversidades. Finally, through n-gram and acoustic-visual analyses, we show that our model follows morphosyntactic, semantic, and prosodic cues for detecting prosodic boundaries.

</details>


### [9] [DeLS-Spec: Decoupled Long-Short Contexts for Parallel Speculative Drafting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07409)
*Hong-Kai Zheng,Piji Li*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: DeLS-Spec proposes a decoupled long-short context speculative decoding method that combines a fixed DFlash block-parallel drafter (long-context expert) with a lightweight, independently trained local head (short-context expert) to introduce intra-block causal conditioning without requiring joint training or retraining from scratch.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Block-parallel drafters like DFlash lack explicit intra-block causal conditioning in position-wise predictions. Existing solutions (Domino, DSpark) introduce causality but require training draft models from scratch, limiting flexibility and increasing cost. There is a need for a modular, low-cost method to inject causality into off-the-shelf block-parallel drafters.

Method: DeLS-Spec decouples drafting into two components: (1) a frozen DFlash model serving as a long-context expert, and (2) a lightweight local head trained independently with a standard next-token prediction objective as a short-context expert. The local head is trained without joint optimization with either the target model or the DFlash backbone. At inference, long-context and short-context logits are combined. The local head is checkpoint-agnostic, enabling modular swapping.

Result: Experiments on Qwen3 models across math, code, and dialogue benchmarks demonstrate that DeLS-Spec consistently improves both speedup and average acceptance length over the DFlash baseline.

Conclusion: DeLS-Spec offers an effective, low-cost, and modular approach to enhancing block-parallel speculative decoding with intra-block causality, achieving consistent inference acceleration without the training overhead of prior causal block-parallel methods.

Abstract: Speculative decoding accelerates LLM inference by drafting multiple tokens and verifying them in parallel. Block-parallel drafters such as DFlash further improve drafting efficiency by predicting an entire block in one pass, but their position-wise predictions lack explicit intra-block causal conditioning. Recent methods such as Domino and DSpark attempt to introduce such causality into block-parallel drafting, but they require training the draft model from scratch, which limits their flexibility and increases training cost. We propose DeLS-Spec, a decoupled long-short context speculative decoding method. DeLS-Spec treats the fixed DFlash model as a long-context expert and introduces a lightweight local head as a short-context expert. The local head can be trained independently with a standard next-token prediction objective, without joint training with the target model or the DFlash backbone, leading to extremely low training cost. At inference time, DeLS-Spec combines long-context and short-context logits, and the local head is not tied to a specific DFlash checkpoint, making the method more modular and flexible. Experiments on Qwen3 models show that DeLS-Spec consistently improves speedup and average acceptance length over DFlash across math, code, and dialogue benchmarks.

</details>


### [10] [SynthAVE: Scalable Synthetic Labeling for E-Commerce with LLM-Arena Validation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07469)
*Andrea Scarinci,Virginia Negri,Brayan Impata,Suleiman Khan,Victor Martinez,Marcello Federico*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: SynthAVE is a large-scale, human-validated benchmark for multilingual e-commerce attribute extraction. It introduces a multi-LLM arena framework where 21 judge configurations vote to validate synthetic labels, achieving near-human agreement (Cohen's κ=0.92) at scale.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fine-tuning LLMs for e-commerce attribute extraction demands massive labeled data across thousands of product types, attributes, and languages. Human annotation at this combinatorial scale is prohibitively expensive. While synthetic label generation with LLMs offers a solution, industrial deployment lacks robust, scalable quality control mechanisms to validate those synthetic labels.

Method: The authors construct SynthAVE, a benchmark of 12,726 products (229 product types, 792 attributes, 4 languages: ES, FR, IT, DE). They propose a multi-LLM arena framework: each sample is independently evaluated by 21 judge configurations (7 model families × 3 prompt templates), and final labels are assigned via majority voting. Agreement is measured using Cohen's κ against human experts and Fleiss' κ for inter-model agreement.

Result: The majority vote ensemble achieves Cohen's κ=0.92 (95.2% raw agreement) with human experts. Individual judges exhibit substantial inter-model agreement (Fleiss' κ=0.76). This demonstrates that aggregating diverse, individually imperfect models yields highly reliable predictions, matching human review quality.

Conclusion: A multi-LLM arena with majority voting provides a cost-effective, scalable alternative to human validation for synthetic attribute extraction labels, maintaining quality parity with human review while dramatically reducing costs.

Abstract: Fine-tuning large language models (LLMs) for e-commerce attribute extraction requires labeled data representative across thousands of product types, attributes, and multiple languages. This combinatorial scale translates to millions of annotations, rendering human labeling prohibitively costly. While recent work has demonstrated synthetic label generation using LLMs, deploying such approaches at industrial scale requires integrated quality control mechanisms. We present SynthAVE, a large-scale human-validated benchmark for attribute value extraction spanning 12,726 products across 229 product types, 792 attributes, and 4 languages (Spanish, French, Italian, German). To validate synthetic labels at scale, we introduce a multi-LLM arena framework where samples are independently evaluated by 21 judge configurations (7 model families $\times$ 3 prompts), with final labels determined via majority voting. The majority vote ensemble agrees with human experts at Cohen's $κ= 0.92$ (95.2% agreement), while individual judges show substantial inter-model agreement (Fleiss' $κ= 0.76$). This demonstrates that diverse models with varying individual judgments aggregate into highly reliable predictions, enabling cost-effective validation at scale while maintaining quality parity with human review.

</details>


### [11] [Think Big, Search Small: Where Capacity Matters in Hierarchical Search Agents?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07548)
*Qinnan Cai,Yibo Zhao,Xiang Li*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper investigates how model capacity should be distributed across roles in hierarchical multi-agent search systems. Through controlled experiments on multi-hop QA benchmarks, the authors find that scaling the delegation (task decomposition) role yields much larger accuracy gains than scaling execution sub-agents, and that a small, distilled executor can match frontier performance while reducing token usage by 37%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multi-agent search systems typically instantiate all agent roles from a single model of identical scale, leaving unaddressed the question of how model capacity should optimally be distributed across different roles (delegation vs. execution) in a hierarchical search architecture.

Method: The authors factorize hierarchical search into three roles: (1) delegation (task decomposition), (2) execution (retrieval and evidence extraction), and (3) answer generation (held fixed as a confound control). They conduct controlled capacity sweeps across six model scales along the delegation and execution axes on five multi-hop QA benchmarks, and also train a 1.7B-parameter executor via quality-filtered trajectory distillation.

Result: Three key findings: (1) Role factorization consistently outperforms single-agent baselines (EM improvement of 4.5–8.6 points). (2) Capacity sensitivity is highly asymmetric — scaling the delegation backbone improves EM by ~11 points, while scaling execution yields only ~2.6 points, identifying decomposition as the capability bottleneck. (3) A 1.7B distilled executor matches frontier sub-agent accuracy while consuming 37% fewer sub-agent tokens.

Conclusion: A concrete recipe for building hierarchical search agents: concentrate model capacity on the delegation role and downsize the execution role without sacrificing accuracy, advancing the cost-accuracy Pareto frontier.

Abstract: Large language model based search agents increasingly adopt multi-agent architectures in which a main agent decomposes a complex question into sub-queries and dispatches them to parallel sub-agents. However, existing systems instantiate all roles from a single model of identical scale, leaving open how model capacity should be distributed across roles. We factorize hierarchical search into three roles: a delegation role responsible for task decomposition, an execution role responsible for retrieval and evidence extraction, and an answer generation role held fixed as a confound control. We then conduct controlled capacity sweeps along the delegation and execution axes on five multi-hop QA benchmarks. The experiments yield three findings. First, role factorization consistently outperforms a single-agent baseline, improving exact match from 4.5 to 8.6 points across six model scales. Second, capacity sensitivity is asymmetric: scaling the delegation backbone improves EM by ~11 points, whereas scaling the execution sub-agent moves EM by only ~2.6 points, identifying decomposition as the capability bottleneck. Third, a 1.7B-parameter executor trained via quality-filtered trajectory distillation matches a frontier sub-agent in accuracy while consuming 37% fewer sub-agent tokens, advancing the Pareto frontier. These results suggest a concrete recipe for building hierarchical search agents: concentrate capacity at delegation and downsize execution without sacrificing accuracy. Our code is available at https://github.com/QinnanCai0115/role-factorized-search.

</details>


### [12] [PALS: Percentile-Aware Layerwise Sparsity for LLM Pruning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07557)
*Yazdan Jamshidi,Alexey Shvets*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: PALS adjusts per-layer sparsity ratios using 99th-percentile activation magnitudes (bounded ±5% around target), outperforming uniform Wanda on LLaMA-2-7B but showing architecture-dependent gains; gradient-based allocation surprisingly underperforms random.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing one-shot pruning methods (Wanda, SparseGPT) uniformly apply the same sparsity ratio across all transformer layers, ignoring known variation in layer importance. This overlooks opportunities to preserve performance by allocating sparsity more intelligently across layers.

Method: PALS (Percentile-Aware Layerwise Sparsity) computes the 99th percentile of activation magnitudes for each layer and uses this statistic to adjust per-layer sparsity ratios, with a ±5% bound around the global target ratio. The method is compared against uniform Wanda, as well as a gradient-based allocation approach, across multiple model architectures (LLaMA-2-7B, LLaMA-3-8B, Mistral-7B).

Result: At 50% sparsity on LLaMA-2-7B, PALS achieves 10.96 WikiText-2 perplexity vs. 12.92 for uniform Wanda (9 runs, p<0.001). Gains are architecture-dependent: LLaMA-3-8B shows marginal improvement and Mistral-7B shows none. Gradient-based allocation performs worse than random, indicating gradient magnitude does not predict the impact of discrete weight removal.

Conclusion: Activation-magnitude-based layerwise sparsity allocation (PALS) is a simple, cost-free enhancement to one-shot pruning that can yield significant gains, but its effectiveness varies by architecture. Gradient magnitude is not a reliable signal for guiding layerwise sparsity decisions.

Abstract: One-shot pruning methods like Wanda and SparseGPT apply the same sparsity ratio to every layer of a transformer, ignoring known variation in layer importance. We propose PALS (Percentile-Aware Layerwise Sparsity), which adjusts per-layer sparsity based on the 99th percentile of activation magnitudes, bounded to $\pm 5\%$ around the target ratio. On LLaMA-2-7B at 50\% sparsity, PALS achieves 10.96 WikiText-2 perplexity versus 12.92 for uniform Wanda (mean over 9 runs, $p < 0.001$). The benefit is architecture-dependent: LLaMA-3-8B shows marginal gains and Mistral-7B shows none. We also find that gradient-based allocation -- the seemingly more principled approach -- produces results worse than random, suggesting that gradient magnitude does not predict the impact of discrete weight removal. PALS adds negligible cost to the pruning pipeline and requires no fine-tuning.

</details>


### [13] [Future Confidence Distillation in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07626)
*Sahil Kale*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper studies how confidence estimation in LLMs evolves temporally during answer generation. It compares pre-solution (Feeling-of-Knowing) and post-solution (Judgement-of-Learning) confidence, finding that post-solution confidence is better calibrated. The authors propose "future confidence distillation," which trains pre-solution predictors using post-solution teacher signals, achieving strong calibration without requiring complete answers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM confidence estimation methods treat confidence as a static property of completed responses, ignoring the temporal evolution of confidence-related information during the answering process. This limits the ability to anticipate reliability early, which is critical for downstream decisions like retrieval or adaptive computation.

Method: The authors compare pre-solution Feeling-of-Knowing (FOK) and post-solution Judgement-of-Learning (JOL) confidence across frontier and open-source LLMs using both verbalized confidence and linear probes on hidden representations. They then introduce future confidence distillation: training linear predictors on pre-solution hidden states using teacher signals from post-solution correctness probes, enabling early confidence estimation that anticipates post-solution calibration quality.

Result: Post-solution confidence consistently outperforms pre-solution confidence in calibration and discrimination. Linear probes recover richer confidence information than verbalized estimates. Distilled pre-solution predictors recover most of the calibration gains of post-solution probes, are highly sample-efficient, and transfer across datasets within the same domain.

Conclusion: Confidence-related information evolves throughout the answering process and can be effectively anticipated from pre-solution representations. Future confidence distillation enables reliable, low-cost confidence estimation without requiring full answer generation, with practical benefits for confidence-aware LLM systems.

Abstract: Reliable confidence estimation is essential for deploying large language models (LLMs) in confidence-aware systems, where downstream decisions such as retrieval, tool use, and adaptive computation depend on accurately estimating answer reliability. Existing approaches, however, largely treat confidence as a property of completed responses, overlooking how confidence-related information evolves throughout the answering process. In this work, we investigate confidence from a temporal perspective by comparing pre-solution Feeling-of-Knowing (FOK) and post-solution Judgement-of-Learning (JOL) confidence estimates across frontier and open-source LLMs. We show that post-solution confidence is consistently better calibrated and more discriminative than pre-solution confidence, while linear probes trained on hidden representations recover substantially richer confidence-related information than models explicitly verbalise. Building on this observation, we introduce future confidence distillation, which trains predictors operating on pre-solution hidden representations using teacher confidence estimates produced by post-solution correctness probes. Despite requiring only pre-solution representations for inference, distilled predictors recover much of the calibration improvement achieved by post-solution confidence, remain highly sample efficient, and transfer across datasets within the same domain. Together, our findings demonstrate that confidence-related information evolves throughout the answering process and can be anticipated before answer generation is complete, enabling significantly more reliable yet low-cost confidence estimation.

</details>


### [14] [DiaLLM: An Investigation into the Robustness-Generation Gap in English Dialect Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07669)
*Jordan Painter,Dipankar Srirag,Adarsh Kappiyath,Diptesh Kanojia,Aditya Joshi,Lu Yin*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: DiaLLM addresses the neglected problem of dialectal generation by continually pretraining and aligning LLMs on Australian, Indian, and Northern British English. The study finds that dialectal robustness (benchmarks) and generation quality are dissociated, with alignment methods reshaping generation in ways benchmarks miss. Explicit variety-targeted adaptation yields dialectally recognisable, human-preferred output, but aggressive reward optimisation is not preferred, revealing a reward-quality gap corroborated by linguistic analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLMs can understand dialectal English but are limited to generating only standard, US-centric English. The generative half of the dialect problem remains largely unaddressed, motivating a systematic, controlled comparison of continual pretraining and alignment strategies for dialectal generation across multiple English varieties.

Method: The authors introduce DiaLLM, which continually pretrains three open-weight LLM families on the International Corpus of English (covering Australian, Indian, and Northern British English). They then apply implicit and explicit post-training paradigms, each combined with three model alignment strategies (including variety-targeted adaptation and reward-based optimisation), enabling the first controlled comparison of these components. Evaluation includes automated benchmarks, human preference judgments, and independent linguistic analysis.

Result: Key findings: (1) dialectal robustness and generation are dissociated — benchmarks improve with continual pretraining and SFT, but alignment reshapes generation in ways benchmarks fail to capture; (2) explicit variety-targeted adaptation reliably produces dialectally recognisable, human-preferred output over broad alignment; (3) the method that most aggressively optimises dialectal reward is not preferred by humans, revealing a reward-quality gap; (4) no single alignment method dominates across all settings.

Conclusion: Closing the gap between reward optimisation and human-perceived dialectal quality requires richer reward designs and continued investment in dialectal resources. The findings highlight the limitations of current benchmarks for evaluating dialectal generation and the need for multi-faceted evaluation combining automated metrics, human judgment, and linguistic analysis.

Abstract: Large language models increasingly \emph{understand} dialectal English, yet still \emph{produce} only standard, US-leaning English, leaving dialectal generation, the harder half of the problem, largely unaddressed. We introduce \textbf{DiaLLM}, which continually pretrains three open-weight language model families on the International Corpus of English and applies implicit and explicit post-training paradigms, each combined with three model alignment strategies, giving the first controlled comparison of these components across Australian, Indian, and Northern British English. Our results reveal that dialectal robustness and generation are \emph{dissociated}: benchmarks are shaped by continual pretraining and SFT, while alignment visibly reshapes generation in ways benchmarks do not capture. Explicit variety-targeted adaptation produces output reliably recognised as dialectal and preferred over broad alignment, yet the method that most aggressively optimises the dialectal reward is not preferred by human evaluators. Independent linguistic analysis corroborates this reward-quality gap, most clearly on two of the three families. No single alignment method dominates, and closing the gap will require richer reward designs and continued investment in dialectal resources. We release all code, checkpoints, and preference datasets.

</details>


### [15] [Does Bielik Know What It Doesn't Know? Activation Dispersion Separates Entity Familiarity from Factual Reliability Across Model Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07670)
*Grzegorz Brzezinka*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: LLMs possess strong internal representations of entity familiarity detectable via activation dispersion in MLP layers (AUROC 0.95–1.00), but this signal is at ceiling even at small scales while behavioral factual accuracy scales sharply with model size. The models almost never abstain despite this internal awareness, suggesting entity familiarity and factual reliability are distinct phenomena governed by different scaling curves.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether a language model's internal activations reveal entity familiarity before generating answers, whether that signal predicts factual reliability, and how this internal awareness relates to the model's behavioral tendency to hallucinate about unfamiliar entities versus its refusal behavior.

Method: Four Polish Bielik models (1.5B–11B parameters) were probed across four entity domains (athletes, cities, writers, musicians), each with 42 well-known, 42 obscure-but-real, and 42 fabricated entities, yielding 504 prompts per model. Two unsupervised dispersion measures — inverse participation ratio and spectral entropy — were computed over post-SwiGLU MLP activations in a single forward pass. A supervised linear probe was also trained. Analyses included selection-aware permutation tests, held-out layer selection, cross-entity-type transfer, matched-template counterfactuals, and comparison against a five-sample semantic-entropy baseline. Answer correctness was evaluated with a strict judge.

Result: Dispersion measures separate known from fabricated entities at AUROC 0.95–1.00 across all domains and scales; linear probes reach 0.99–1.00. Permutation floors are 0.70–0.74 (p ≤ 1e-3). Signal survives held-out layers (0.93–0.99) and transfers across entity types (mean off-diagonal 0.92–0.99). The representational signal is at ceiling at 1.5B, while behavioral accuracy scales sharply (0, 2, 10, 19 of 42 known athletes correct across scales). Separating correct from hallucinated answers within known entities is much harder (probe 0.93; dispersion no better than first-token-entropy baseline). Semantic-entropy baseline reaches only 0.71–0.83 at 5× inference cost. Models almost never abstain: 2 refusals and 1 hedge in 2,520 answers.

Conclusion: Entity familiarity and factual reliability are distinct phenomena on different scaling curves: internal awareness of entity familiarity is already saturated in small models, but the capacity to leverage this signal for correct factual generation only emerges at larger scales. This decoupling, combined with near-zero abstention rates, highlights a critical gap in trustworthy deployment of LLMs.

Abstract: Large language models hallucinate most about entities they have never seen. We ask whether a model's activations betray entity familiarity before a single answer token is generated, and whether that signal predicts the factual reliability of the answers. On four Polish Bielik models (1.5B-11B parameters), we probe four entity domains (athletes, cities, writers, musicians), each with 42 well-known, 42 obscure-but-real, and 42 fabricated entities addressed by a one-sentence question (504 prompts per model). Two unsupervised, single-forward-pass dispersion measures over post-SwiGLU MLP activations, inverse participation ratio and spectral entropy, separate known from fabricated entities at AUROC 0.95-1.00 across all domains and scales; a supervised linear probe reaches 0.99-1.00. Both clear selection-aware permutation floors of about 0.70-0.74 (empirical p<=1e-3), survive held-out layer selection (0.93-0.99), and persist on real names (known vs. obscure-but-real: 0.96-1.00). The signal transfers across entity types (mean off-diagonal AUROC 0.92-0.99); a matched-template counterfactual shows the only large drops are template-caused, not entity-type effects, and the signal is diffuse across heads. This representational signal is already at ceiling at 1.5B, whereas behavioral factual reliability scales sharply: 0, 2, 10, and 19 of 42 known athletes are answered fully correctly by the 1.5B, 4.5B, 7B, and 11B models under a strict judge. Within known entities, separating correct from hallucinated answers is much harder (probe 0.93; dispersion no better than a first-token-entropy baseline). A five-sample semantic-entropy baseline reaches only 0.71-0.83 at 5x the inference cost. Despite this internal awareness, the models almost never abstain: an audit of 2,520 answers finds 2 refusals and 1 hedge. Entity familiarity and factual reliability are distinct phenomena on different scaling curves.

</details>


### [16] [Accurate, Interdisciplinary and Transparent Structure-property Understanding with Deep Native Structural Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07708)
*Chen Tang,Yizhou Wang,Jianyu Wu,Lintao Wang,Shixiang Tang,Pengze Li,Encheng Su,Jun Yao,Jiabei Xiao,Yuqi Shi,Jielan Li,Hongxia Hao,Zhangyang Gao,Fang Wu,Ben Fei,Xiangyu Yue,Pan Tan,Bozitao Zhong,Jinouwen Zhang,Aoran Wang,Yan Lu,Jiaheng Liu,Xinzhu Ma,Liang Hong,Mingyue Zheng,Phil Torr,Bowen Zhou,Wanli Ouyang,Lei Bai*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: SciReasoner is a multimodal scientific foundation model that unifies structural reasoning across proteins, small molecules, and inorganic crystals by discretizing 3D coordinates and topologies into a structure-aware token vocabulary. It achieves SOTA on 67 of 86 benchmarks and its reasoning traces are preferred over frontier LLMs in 98% of expert evaluations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Applying AI to structure-property relationships faces a dual challenge: models must preserve domain-native structural information (coordinates, bonding, periodicity) while also demonstrating how structural evidence supports predictions under scientific constraints (stereochemistry, symmetry, energetics). Existing approaches fail to jointly address representation and reasoning in a unified manner across biological, chemical, and materials domains.

Method: SciReasoner discretizes 3D coordinates, topologies, and periodic connectivities into a unified structure-aware vocabulary, treating structural tokens as addressable evidence units. It then performs multimodal reasoning over these tokens under domain-specific physical constraints, generating inspectable reasoning traces that link structural evidence to predictions.

Result: In protein function prediction, Cellular Component F_max improved from 0.42 to 0.55 for low-homology proteins. Retrosynthesis accuracy rose from 0.63 to 0.72 with fragment-level disconnection traces. In materials, representations cleanly separate elemental/compound phases and band-gap regimes. Overall, SOTA on 67/86 benchmarks, with reasoning traces preferred or comparable to frontier LLMs in 98% of double-blind evaluations.

Conclusion: By making structure an inspectable substrate for constrained scientific reasoning, SciReasoner bridges accurate prediction with interpretable inference. The unified tokenization strategy demonstrates that a single architecture can effectively reason across disparate structural domains when structure is treated as addressable evidence.

Abstract: Structure-property relationships are foundational to biology, chemistry and materials science, where function, reactivity and physical response emerge from spatial, chemical and periodic organization. Mechanistically explaining these relationships requires interpreting structural evidence through scientific principles and physical constraints, from stereochemistry and bonding to symmetry, energetics and periodic order. However, applying artificial intelligence to this process presents a joint challenge of representation and reasoning: models must preserve domain-native structural information while showing how specific evidence supports predictions under these constraints. Here we introduce SciReasoner, a multimodal scientific foundation model for native structural reasoning across proteins, small molecules and inorganic crystals. SciReasoner discretizes coordinates, topologies and periodic connectivities into a unified structure-aware vocabulary, treating structural tokens as addressable evidence units during reasoning. In homology-controlled Gene Ontology prediction, SciReasoner improves Cellular Component annotation for low-homology and orphan-like proteins, increasing $F_{\max}$ from 0.42 to 0.55. In chemistry, it raises single-step retrosynthesis accuracy from 0.63 to 0.72 while generating fragment-level disconnection and precursor-verification traces. In materials science, its representations separate elemental and compound phases and resolve high- and low-band-gap regimes. Across 86 benchmarks, SciReasoner achieves state-of-the-art performance on 67 tasks. Double-blind expert evaluation rates its reasoning traces as preferred or at least comparable to those of a frontier large language model in 98% of cases. By making structure an inspectable substrate for reasoning under scientific constraints, SciReasoner connects accurate prediction with interpretable scientific inference.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [17] [TriRoute: Unified Learned Routing for Joint Adaptive Attention, Experts, and KV-Cache Allocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06601)
*Andrii Balashov,Olena Ponomarova*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TriRoute introduces a unified lightweight controller that jointly optimizes three coupled inference-time decisions — attention mode, FFN expert selection, and KV-cache bit-width — Pareto-dominating independent combinations of MoD, MoE, and KV-quantization while preserving robustness on rare entities, code, and arithmetic.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing conditional computation techniques (MoE for FFN sparsification, MoD for block skipping, KV-cache quantization for memory compression) operate on isolated axes, ignoring strong coupling among attention resolution, expert selection, and cache precision. The authors argue these decisions should be made jointly because a token warranting full attention likely also needs high-precision caching regardless of expert assignment.

Method: TriRoute employs a single lightweight controller shared across three axes, emitting per-token per-layer coordinated policies via: (1) Gumbel-Softmax with straight-through estimation for categorical attention mode selection (skip/local/full); (2) load-balanced top-k gating for FFN expert routing (with a null expert subsuming MoD functionality); and (3) KV-cache bit-width allocation. Training uses a Lagrangian budget constraint to make average compute/memory cost tunable. A coupling-aware balancing loss with per-axis normalization addresses the identified cross-axis routing-collapse cascade where collapse on one axis propagates to others.

Result: On decoder-only models from 160M to 1.3B parameters at compute-optimal token counts, TriRoute Pareto-dominates the best independent MoD+MoE+KV-quantization combination at matched inference FLOPs and memory. It better preserves tail-case robustness on rare entities, code, and arithmetic that pure perplexity optimization degrades. Post-hoc analysis reveals interpretable structure: full attention and high-precision cache are allocated to sentence-initial positions, rare subwords, and named entities, while function words receive cheap routing.

Conclusion: Joint routing across attention, FFN experts, and KV-cache quantization yields superior efficiency-accuracy trade-offs compared to independently optimized techniques. The learned routing policies are interpretable, aligning with linguistic notions of token importance. The cross-axis collapse problem is a key challenge in joint training that can be mitigated via per-axis normalization and coupling-aware balancing.

Abstract: Conditional computation can decouple language model quality from per-token inference cost, yet leading techniques act on a single axis in isolation: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) sparsifies the FFN, Mixture-of-Depths (MoD) skips whole transformer blocks, and KV-cache quantization compresses attention memory. We argue these three decisions (attention resolution, expert selection, and cache bit-width) are strongly coupled and should be made jointly: a token rare enough to warrant full attention may also need high-precision caching regardless of which expert processes it. We introduce TriRoute, a single lightweight controller shared across all three axes that, for every token at every layer, emits a coordinated policy: (i) an attention mode (skip/local/full), (ii) a sparse set of FFN experts (with a null expert recovering MoD), and (iii) a KV-cache bit-width. The controller trains end-to-end via a heterogeneous relaxation (Gumbel-Softmax with straight-through estimation for categorical decisions and load-balanced top-k gating for experts) under a Lagrangian budget constraint that turns the average compute and memory cost into a controllable knob. We identify a cross-axis routing-collapse cascade in naive joint training, where collapse on one axis propagates to the others, and address it with per-axis normalization and a coupling-aware balancing loss. On decoder-only models from 160M to 1.3B parameters at compute-optimal token counts, TriRoute Pareto-dominates the best independent MoD+MoE+KV-quantization combination at matched inference FLOPs and memory, while better preserving tail-case robustness on rare entities, code, and arithmetic that pure perplexity optimization erodes. Post-hoc analysis reveals interpretable structure: the controller allocates full attention and high-precision cache to sentence-initial positions, rare subwords, and named entities, while cheaply routing function words.

</details>


### [18] [A Quiet Failure in Calibrated Virtual Screening: Marginal Conformal Prediction Under-Covers the Minority Class, and a Class-Conditional Fix Recovers It](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06605)
*Muhammadjon Tursunbadalov,Mustafojon Tursunbadalov*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Conformal prediction's marginal coverage guarantee fails severely on imbalanced molecular datasets: minority-class coverage collapses (as low as 4.2%) while global coverage appears fine. Class-conditional (Mondrian) conformal prediction restores per-class reliability. A conservation identity quantifies the failure, and a practical diagnostic is proposed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conformal prediction is increasingly adopted in drug discovery for uncertainty quantification with formal coverage guarantees. However, the standard marginal guarantee only assures global coverage — whether it holds per-class under the severe class imbalance common in ADMET and toxicity datasets is critically underexamined. The authors aim to expose and remedy this hidden failure mode.

Method: The authors evaluate marginal conformal prediction across four imbalanced drug-discovery datasets (blood-brain-barrier penetration, clinical-trial toxicity, and two others) using three model architectures: random forest, graph neural network, and a frozen chemical language model. They derive a conservation identity linking minority coverage shortfall to majority surplus and imbalance ratio. They benchmark class-conditional (Mondrian) conformal prediction as a remedy, test robustness across scaffold splits and alternative nonconformity scores, localize failures to generic molecular scaffolds, and propose a one-number diagnostic plus a cost-utility model for abstention decisions.

Result: At 90% target coverage, minority-class coverage drops to 64.8% (BBB) and as low as 4.2% (clinical toxicity), with the rare class nearly abandoned. The failure is model-agnostic (p<0.001 for all architectures), driven by baseline calibration on rare labels rather than architecture. The conservation identity predicts the coverage gap to within one percentage point. Mondrian conformal prediction restores minority coverage to target on all datasets with modestly larger prediction sets. Failures localize to generic scaffolds (benzene, pyridine cores), and the cost model shows abstention flips screening utility from net-negative to net-positive.

Conclusion: Marginal conformal prediction's guarantee is dangerously misleading under class imbalance in drug discovery — global coverage masks catastrophic minority-class undercoverage. Class-conditional conformal prediction is a practical, effective fix. The proposed diagnostic and scaffold-level failure analysis provide actionable tools for practitioners deploying conformal methods in imbalanced molecular settings.

Abstract: Conformal prediction is being adopted in drug discovery to put an honest number on model reliability: pick an error rate alpha, and the method returns prediction sets containing the true label with probability at least 1 - alpha. We show this guarantee can be dangerous on imbalanced datasets. Across four datasets, standard (marginal) conformal prediction hits its global 90% coverage target while leaving the minority class badly exposed: realized minority coverage falls to 64.8% on blood-brain-barrier penetration and to 4.2% on clinical-trial toxicity, where the rare class is nearly abandoned. The failure is not tied to one model: a random forest, a graph network, and a frozen chemical language model all reproduce it (p < 0.001 in every case), with severity tracking baseline calibration on rare labels rather than architecture. A conservation identity explains the effect: the minority's shortfall equals the majority's surplus amplified by the imbalance ratio, predicting the measured gap to within one point and ordering severity across datasets. The failure survives realistic scaffold splits and a second conformal score, while aggregate accuracy and overall coverage stay reassuringly high, which is exactly why it is easy to miss. Class-conditional (Mondrian) conformal prediction closes the gap on every dataset, restoring minority coverage to target for a modest increase in prediction-set size. We localize the failures to generic molecular scaffolds - plain benzene and pyridine cores occurring in both classes - propose a one-number diagnostic, and show with a cost model that abstaining on affected compounds flips a screening campaign from net-negative to net-positive utility. Our contribution is demonstrating on real chemistry how severe and invisible this known conformal-theory gap becomes under imbalance, and laying out a practical protocol restoring per-class reliability.

</details>


### [19] [NEST: Tackling Dataset-Level Distribution Shifts via Regime-Oriented Mixture-of-Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06607)
*Lanhao Li,Bingshu Xie,Lijun Sun,Xin Xue,Haoyi Zhou,Jianxin Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: NEST is a long-term time-series forecasting framework that addresses dataset-level distribution shifts by decomposing the dataset into distinct operational regimes via unsupervised clustering in a moment-entropy space, then using a dense Mixture-of-Experts architecture with a regime-oriented router and specialized expert kernels to capture regime-specific dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing long-term forecasting methods focus on local temporal distribution shifts but fail to address the global structural challenge where datasets comprise composites of distinct operational regimes (behavioral modes). Dataset-level distribution shifts driven by evolving system states degrade forecasting accuracy, requiring explicit structural modeling.

Method: NEST employs a two-phase dense MoE architecture: (1) Structural specialization via unsupervised clustering in a moment-entropy space to partition the dataset into distinct operational regimes; (2) A regime-oriented router that generates initial expert weights from temporal content and refines them through geometric modulation to regime centroids. Individual experts function as specialized kernels capturing regime-specific dynamics by evolving unique variate-attention patterns, rather than acting as monolithic predictors.

Result: Extensive evaluations on diverse benchmarks, including heterogeneous network traffic and physical phenomena, demonstrate that NEST consistently achieves state-of-the-art performance in long-term forecasting.

Conclusion: Explicitly modeling dataset-level structural regimes via a clustering-guided MoE framework significantly improves long-term forecasting accuracy in complex systems with evolving operational modes.

Abstract: Accurate long-term forecasting in complex systems is frequently compromised by dataset-level distribution shifts, where diverse underlying behavioral modes and evolving system states drive the dynamic multivariate time-series. While existing methods predominantly focus on local temporal shifts, they fail to explicitly model the global structural challenge where datasets are composites of distinct operational regimes. In this paper, we propose NEST, a specialized framework designed to model and recompose these evolving structures through a two-phase dense MoE architecture. NEST first facilitates structural specialization by partitioning the dataset into distinct operational regimes through unsupervised clustering in a principled moment-entropy space. We introduce a regime-oriented router mechanism that generates initial expert weights based on temporal content, subsequently refined through geometric modulation to regime centroids. Crucially, rather than acting as monolithic predictors, individual experts function as specialized kernels that capture regime-specific dynamics by evolving unique variate-attention patterns. Extensive evaluations on diverse benchmarks, including heterogeneous network traffic and physical phenomena, demonstrate that NEST consistently achieves state-of-the-art performance. Our code and datasets are available at https://github.com/Aaralshin/NEST

</details>


### [20] [D2PO: Optimizing Diffusion Samplers via Dynamic Preference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06609)
*Jinkyu Kim,Jinyoung Choi,Bohyung Han*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: D2PO reformulates diffusion sampler optimization as a preference-based alignment problem using Direct Preference Optimization, introducing dynamic preferences and an energy-based model formulation to improve low-NFE perceptual quality over traditional regression-based distillation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing student-teacher regression frameworks for diffusion sampling suffer from a fundamental misalignment: low-NFE student samplers trained to mimic high-NFE teachers preserve coarse global structure but sacrifice high-frequency texture fidelity, degrading perceptual quality.

Method: D2PO frames sampler optimization as preference alignment using DPO. Key components: (1) modeling the sampling policy as an energy-based model (EBM) to render preference comparisons tractable; (2) deriving an energy formulation directly from the pretrained score network for evaluating preferences in perturbed spaces capturing both structure and detail; (3) introducing dynamic preferences where preferred samples iteratively improve as policies are learned, replacing static teacher supervision with self-improving alignment.

Result: Extensive experiments show D2PO aligns diffusion samplers with perceptual quality more faithfully than conventional regression-based schedulers, consistently outperforming them under low-NFE constraints while unlocking the full potential of high-quality teachers.

Conclusion: D2PO provides a principled, preference-based alternative to regression distillation for diffusion sampler optimization, with dynamic preferences enabling progressive self-improvement that better preserves perceptual fidelity at low sampling budgets.

Abstract: We propose D2PO (Dynamic Direct Preference Optimization), a principled framework for optimizing diffusion sampling policies with respect to timestep schedules and classifier-free guidance (CFG) weights. Our work is motivated by a fundamental limitation of existing student-teacher regression frameworks; low-NFE student samplers are trained to mimic high-NFEteachers, often sacrificing high-frequency texture fidelity while preserving coarse global structures, thereby misaligning the sampler with perceptual quality. D2PO addresses this challenge by reformulating sampler optimization as a preference-based alignment problem, leveraging the Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) framework. To make DPO applicable to diffusion samplers, we model the sampling policy as an energy-based model (EBM), transforming preference comparisons into tractable energy differences. We further introduce a novel energy formulation derived directly from the pretrained score network, enabling preference evaluation in perturbed spaces that jointly capture structural consistency and fine-grained details. Moreover, we introduce dynamic preferences, where the preferred samples used for alignment progressively improve as the sampling policies are learned. This self-improving mechanism replaces rigid static teacher supervision with an iterative, preference-guided refinement process, providing progressively stronger alignment signals. Extensive experiments demonstrate that D2PO aligns diffusion samplers with perceptual quality more faithfully, unlocking the full potential of high-quality teachers and consistently outperforming conventional regression-based schedulers under low-NFE constraints.

</details>


### [21] [Deep Reinforcement Learning for Reliability Based Bi-Objective Portfolio Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06610)
*Sounaq Das,Tanmay Sen,Raghu Nandan Sengupta,Aditya Gupta*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A deep reinforcement learning framework (MORP-DRL) using PPO is proposed for multi-objective portfolio optimization that jointly optimizes expected return and downside risk (variance, CVaR, EVaR) under transaction costs and portfolio bounds, with asset returns modeled via GARCH-EVT-t-copula and quasi-Monte Carlo simulation, demonstrating competitive risk-return performance across multiple market regimes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing reliability-based portfolio optimization methods rely on static frameworks that fail to capture sequential decision-making, tail risk, and market frictions such as transaction costs. There is a need for a dynamic, multi-objective approach that addresses these shortcomings.

Method: The MORP-DRL framework uses PPO-based deep reinforcement learning to optimize expected return alongside three downside risk measures (variance, CVaR, EVaR). Uncertainty and heavy-tailed behavior are modeled through GARCH(1,1), Extreme Value Theory, and a t-copula dependence structure, with realistic scenarios generated via quasi-Monte Carlo simulation. Practical constraints (transaction costs, portfolio bounds) are incorporated, and NSGA-II serves as a benchmark.

Result: Experiments on ten global equity indices across pre-COVID, COVID, and post-COVID regimes show MORP-DRL achieves competitive risk-return performance, reduced downside risk during market stress, and scalability to high-dimensional settings.

Conclusion: The MORP-DRL framework effectively addresses the limitations of static portfolio optimization by leveraging deep reinforcement learning for sequential decision-making under uncertainty, offering robust performance and scalability across diverse market conditions.

Abstract: Portfolio optimization under uncertainty is inherently a multi-objective decision problem involving complex interactions among return, risk, market dynamics, and practical investment constraints. Existing reliability based portfolio optimization approaches primarily rely on static optimization frameworks and often fail to capture sequential decision making, tail risk, and market frictions such as transaction costs. To address these limitations, we propose a deep reinforcement learning framework for multi-objective reliability based portfolio optimization (MORP-DRL). The proposed framework jointly optimizes expected return and downside risk using three complementary risk measures: variance, Conditional Value-at-Risk (CVaR), and Entropic Value-at-Risk (EVaR). To model uncertainty and heavy-tailed market behavior, asset returns are represented using GARCH(1,1), Extreme Value Theory, and a t-copula dependence structure, while realistic scenarios are generated through quasi-Monte Carlo simulation. A Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) based strategy is developed under practical constraints including transaction costs and portfolio bounds, and is benchmarked against NSGA-II. Experiments on ten global equity indices across pre-COVID, COVID, and post-COVID market regimes demonstrate that MORP-DRL achieves competitive risk-return performance, reduced downside risk during periods of market stress, and scalability to high-dimensional portfolio settings.

</details>


### [22] [Multi-Agent AI Control: Distributed Attacks Hamper Per-Instance Monitors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07368)
*Oliver Makins,Orazio Angelini,Zohreh Shams,Mary Phuong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper empirically investigates multi-agent AI control, showing that distributed attacks across multiple agents fragment monitoring attention, making per-agent detection less effective. A stronger trusted monitor substantially reduces but does not eliminate undetected attacks, while explicit planning amplifies the fragmentation effect.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing AI control research focuses on single-agent, single-trajectory scenarios. Real-world deployments involve many agents sharing infrastructure, and catastrophic risks like model-weight exfiltration or training-run poisoning likely require coordinated multi-agent attacks. This gap motivates the first empirical study of multi-agent AI control.

Method: The authors build FakeLab, a synthetic AI-lab codebase with 9 services, 86 benign tasks, and 4 attack objectives. They evaluate single-agent monitoring against distributed attacks while systematically varying: number of agents, coordination mechanisms, model capabilities, and monitoring configurations. They compare weak vs. strong trusted monitors and test the impact of an explicit planner agent.

Result: Three main findings: (1) Fragmentation effect — as more agents coordinate, per-agent monitoring becomes less likely to catch any attacker. (2) This effect is not driven by benign-to-malicious code ratio but likely depends on model capability. (3) An explicit planner amplifies fragmentation, increases attack completion rate up to sevenfold, and mildly degrades executor agents' monitor-theory-of-mind. A strong trusted monitor reduces undetected success by over an order of magnitude but remains imperfect.

Conclusion: Multi-agent coordination significantly undermines single-agent monitoring paradigms. Stronger monitoring provides substantial but incomplete defense. The authors offer FakeLab to safety researchers, highlighting the urgency of developing control techniques robust to distributed, coordinated attacks.

Abstract: AI control is a family of techniques to prevent an AI with malicious goals from subverting its operator's intent.
