AI Control usually studies a single agent in one trajectory, but real deployments run many agents over shared infrastructure, and the most severe risks (model-weight exfiltration, training-run poisoning) plausibly need several agents acting in concert. We initiate the empirical study of multi-agent AI control, formalising distributed attacks in which several agents jointly aim for a malicious goal. We develop FakeLab: a synthetic AI-lab codebase (9 services, 86 benign tasks, 4 attack objectives). We evaluate single agent monitoring against distributed attacks, varying the number of agents, their coordination, model capabilities and precise monitoring configuration.
  Our central finding is the fragmentation effect: as more agents coordinate to attack, per-agent monitoring becomes less likely to catch any of the attackers. Secondly, this is not driven by the ratio of benign to malicious code -- we argue this is likely dependent on model capability. Third, an explicit planner amplifies the fragmentation effect, raises the rate of completing the attack up to sevenfold, and mildly sharpens executors' poor monitor-theory-of-mind. Finally: a weak "trusted" monitor misses most attacks, while a stronger one cuts undetected success by more than an order of magnitude but remains imperfect. We are happy to share FakeLab, our control setting, with safety researchers on request.

</details>


### [23] [STAGformer: A Spatio-temporal Agent Graph Transformer for Micro Mobility Demand Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06614)
*Ye Zihao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: STAGformer introduces a spatio-temporal agent graph transformer for station-level bike-sharing demand forecasting that reduces the quadratic cost of standard self-attention to O(NT) via learnable agent tokens, achieving state-of-the-art performance on NYC Citi-Bike and Chicago Divvy-Bike datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate station-level demand forecasting for bike-sharing systems is challenging due to complex spatio-temporal dependencies and large-scale urban networks. Existing transformer-based methods suffer from quadratic computational complexity in self-attention, limiting their scalability for global dependency modeling across many stations and time steps.

Method: STAGformer proposes a two-step agent attention mechanism using a small set of learnable spatial and temporal agent tokens that first aggregate global information and then broadcast it back to individual stations/time steps, reducing complexity to O(NT). The architecture integrates four modules: (1) a spatio-temporal encoder fusing dynamic node features with external factors (weather, time, POIs), (2) a graph propagation module for spatial neighbor aggregation, (3) a temporal convolution module for local pattern extraction, and (4) the agent attention module for global dependency modeling.

Result: Extensive experiments on NYC Citi-Bike and Chicago Divvy-Bike datasets show STAGformer consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines across multiple prediction horizons, with significant improvements in both RMSE and MAE. Ablation studies confirm the critical contribution of the agent attention mechanism for global spatio-temporal dependency modeling.

Conclusion: STAGformer demonstrates that efficient global spatio-temporal modeling with linear complexity is achievable through agent-based attention, offering a scalable and accurate solution for large-scale bike-sharing demand forecasting.

Abstract: Accurate station-level demand forecasting is essential for the efficient operation of bike-sharing systems, yet it remains challenging due to complex spatio-temporal dependencies and the large scale of urban networks. This paper presents STAGformer, a Spatio-Temporal Agent Graph Transformer that achieves efficient global modeling with linear computational complexity. The model introduces a two-step agent attention mechanism, where a small set of learnable spatial and temporal agent tokens first aggregate global information and then broadcast it back to individual stations and time steps, effectively capturing long-range interactions while reducing the quadratic cost of standard self-attention to O(NT). STAGformer integrates four core modules: a spatio-temporal encoder that fuses dynamic node features with external contextual factors (weather, time, points of interest), a graph propagation module for spatial neighbor aggregation, a temporal convolution module for local pattern extraction, and the agent attention module for global dependency modeling. Extensive experiments on two real-world datasets -- NYC Citi-Bike and Chicago Divvy-Bike -- demonstrate that STAGformer consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines across multiple prediction horizons, achieving significant improvements in both RMSE and MAE. Ablation studies validate the contribution of each component, with the agent attention mechanism proving critical for modeling global spatio-temporal dependencies.

</details>


### [24] [Inertia-1: An Open Exploration of Wearable Motion Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06617)
*Zongzhe Xu,Aakarsh Anand,Sarah Jiang,Chuntung Zhuang,Zitao Shuai,Sriram Sankararaman,Yuzhe Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Inertia-1 is a comprehensive, open-source exploration of wearable motion foundation models, using 18.2M+ hours of accelerometer data to systematically study how data, model, and training choices affect generalization across diverse downstream tasks including activity recognition, gait freezing detection, and disease prediction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work on wearable motion sensing examines design choices (e.g., sensor placement, sampling rate) in isolation under fixed settings and narrow tasks, leaving pretraining and scaling principles for motion foundation models poorly understood. Real-world sensing diversity remains uncaptured.

Method: The authors construct a controlled framework spanning the full lifecycle of wearable motion foundation models: data choices (sensor modality, device placement, sampling rate, window length), model choices (architectures, model size), and training choices (pretraining objective, data scale). Systematic evaluations conducted across 15 datasets covering human activity recognition, freezing-of-gait detection, and disease prediction.

Result: The study yields state-of-the-art recipes for diverse downstream tasks and reveals key findings for building motion foundation models that generalize across tasks and sensing conditions.

Conclusion: Inertia-1 serves as both a high-performance solution for downstream wearable sensing tasks and a comprehensive, practical, open cookbook for wearable motion representation learning, guiding future research in the field.

Abstract: Wearable motion sensing provides a continuous and scalable window into human behavior and health, making it a natural fit for foundation models, yet its pretraining and scaling principles remain poorly understood. Prior work studies isolated design choices, such as sensor placement or sampling frequency, often under fixed settings and narrow downstream tasks that fail to capture real-world sensing diversity. We introduce Inertia-1, a fully open exploration of wearable motion foundation models. Using massive corpora of accelerometer data from global sources spanning more than 18.2M hours, we build a controlled framework for studying the full lifecycle of wearable motion foundation models, covering data choices such as sensor modality, device placement, sampling rate, window length; model choices such as architectures and model size; and training choices such as pretraining objective and data scale. Extensive evaluations across 15 datasets spanning human activity recognition, freezing-of-gait detection, and disease prediction reveal intriguing findings for building motion foundation models that generalize across tasks and sensing conditions. Collectively, Inertia-1 not only presents state-of-the-art recipes for diverse downstream tasks, but also serves as a comprehensive, practical, and open cookbook for wearable motion representation learning.

</details>


### [25] [LLM-Guided Task-Semantic Field Factorization for Industrial Process Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06623)
*Youcheng Zong,Runda Jia,Mingxuan Ren,Dakuo He*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TSF is an LLM-guided framework that encodes process documents and variable semantics into a lightweight "task-semantic field" offline, then injects this knowledge into standard time-series backbones during online training/inference, achieving up to 25.5% MAE reduction with negligible overhead (~2–3k parameters, <0.008 ms/step).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Process industries face scarce labeled data, frequent operating regime changes, and costly retraining. Existing process documents (variable names, units, physical meanings) are underutilized — standard time-series models treat variables as anonymous columns, and text-enhanced methods fail to convey semantic-logical relations between variables and the prediction target within each numerical window.

Method: TSF uses an LLM offline to construct a task-semantic field from task protocols and variable documents. This field encodes semantic-logical relations between variables. During online training/inference, the current numerical window activates relevant variable semantics, allowing semantic information to participate in each prediction. The LLM is not used during online operation; only conventional time-series backbones are needed, with the semantic field serving as a lightweight augmentation.

Result: On multiple complex industrial forecasting and soft-sensing tasks, TSF reduces MAE by 6.4% on average, with the largest reduction reaching 25.5%. The method adds only ~1.8–3.0k parameters with less than 0.008 ms/step of additional online inference overhead.

Conclusion: TSF demonstrates that existing process documents can be transformed into measurable forecasting gains across different backbones and semantic generators while remaining lightweight enough for practical industrial deployment.

Abstract: Process industries rely on time-series forecasting and soft sensing to estimate quality variables that are hard to measure online. Labeled data are scarce, operating regimes change frequently, and retraining models or rebuilding alignment pipelines for each scenario is costly. Such settings often provide variable tables and process documents that record variable names, units, physical meanings, and process roles. However, standard time-series backbones usually treat inputs as anonymous numerical columns. Existing text-enhanced methods also rarely make the semantic-logical relations between input variables and the prediction target available to the model within each numerical window. To address this problem, this article proposes Task-Semantic Field Factorization (TSF), a large language model (LLM)-guided framework. TSF builds a task-semantic field from task protocols and variable documents before training and uses the LLM only for offline semantic construction. Online training and inference remain with conventional time-series backbones. During training and inference, the current numerical window activates variable semantics, so semantic information participates in each prediction and supports adaptation to different prediction targets and operating shifts. On multiple complex industrial forecasting and soft-sensing tasks, TSF reduces MAE by 6.4\% on average in improved settings, with the largest reduction reaching 25.5\%. It adds only about 1.8--3.0k parameters, with less than 0.008 ms/step of additional online inference overhead. These results show that TSF turns existing process documents into measurable forecasting gains across backbones and semantic generators while remaining lightweight for deployment.

</details>


### [26] [STST-JEPA: Shallow-Target Spatio-Temporal Joint Embedding Prediction Architecture For EEG Self-Supervised Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06629)
*Roy Segal,Yoni Svechinsky,Tomer Fekete*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: STST-JEPA is a self-supervised transformer pretrained on 47,703 EEG sessions (ages 5–81) that achieves state-of-the-art brain-age prediction (MAE 3.06 years, r=0.924) and ranks first on NeuralBench benchmarks for sex classification, age prediction, and psychopathology regression, with age-prediction residuals correlating with cognitive efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: EEG-based brain-age modeling is attractive due to EEG's low cost, portability, and temporal richness, but existing models struggle with cross-site montage heterogeneity, small labeled cohorts, subject-level non-stationarity, and poor performance across the full pediatric-to-older-adult age range. No EEG foundation model has demonstrated competitive age regression across this full lifespan.

Method: The authors introduce STST-JEPA, a self-supervised transformer pretrained on 47,703 sessions from brain.space and HBN. The model uses two objectives: (1) a latent-prediction (JEPA-style) loss that predicts masked-token representations against an exponential-moving-average tokenizer target, and (2) an auxiliary signal-reconstruction loss. Both are applied to 30-second multi-channel EEG windows under spatiotemporal block masks. A lightweight attentive probe on frozen embeddings yields age predictions; light fine-tuning of final layers is used for downstream tasks.

Result: On held-out validation (3,367 sessions), the frozen-embedding probe achieves MAE of 3.06 years (r=0.924) versus a ~10-year baseline. After light fine-tuning, the model achieves rank-1 on NeuralBench for sex classification (balanced accuracy 0.911), age prediction (r=0.749), and psychopathology composite regression (r=0.215). Age-prediction residuals negatively correlate with cognitive efficiency across multiple tasks.

Conclusion: STST-JEPA demonstrates that self-supervised pretraining with joint latent-prediction and signal-reconstruction objectives on large-scale, heterogeneous EEG data yields a versatile foundation model that sets new benchmarks for brain-age estimation and multiple downstream EEG tasks across the lifespan.

Abstract: Brain age -- the age inferred from a physiological recording -- is an emerging biomarker whose deviation from chronological age tracks neurological and psychiatric burden, and EEG is an attractive substrate for it because it is cheap, portable, and temporally rich. Yet EEG brain-age models must contend with cross-site montage heterogeneity, small labelled cohorts, and dominant subject-level non-stationarity, and few EEG foundation models have been shown to deliver competitive age regression across the full pediatric-to-older-adult range in which such a biomarker would actually be deployed. We introduce STST-JEPA, a self-supervised transformer for resting-state and task EEG, pretrained on 47,703 sessions spanning ages 5-81 from the brain.space and Healthy Brain Network (HBN) corpora. The model combines a latent-prediction objective - predicting masked-token representations against an EMA-of-tokenizer target - with an auxiliary signal-reconstruction term, applied to 30-second multi-channel windows under spatiotemporal block masks. A lightweight attentive probe trained on frozen pretrained embeddings achieves a best held-out-validation mean absolute error of 3.06 years (r = 0.924) for age regression on 3,367 sessions, against a predict-the-mean baseline of approximately 10 years MAE. With light task-specific fine-tuning of the model's final layers, the same pretrained encoder achieves rank-1 placements - with the model's native 30-second windows - on the public NeuralBench x brain.space EEG leaderboard for sex classification (balanced accuracy 0.911), age prediction (r = 0.749), and psychopathology composite regression (r = 0.215). We further show that the model's age-prediction residual is negatively correlated with cognitive efficiency over several tasks we examined.

</details>


### [27] [When Certificates Fail: A Unified Safety Framework for Embedded Neural Interface Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06630)
*Jasmeet Singh Bindra*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper identifies a critical gap between formal robustness certificates and actual task performance in neural interfaces, showing mathematically valid certificates can coexist with severe accuracy degradation. It proposes an empirical audit framework addressing three alignment failures: verification insufficiency, proxy-fidelity divergence, and latent information exfiltration, demonstrating that operational safety auditing is essential beyond mere certificate verification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robustness certificates in neural interfaces can remain mathematically valid even when task accuracy collapses, indicating a fundamental disconnect between formal verification and real-world operational safety. The authors argue this represents a broader alignment failure where training objectives diverge from user welfare, necessitating a systematic audit framework for responsible deployment.

Method: The authors propose a unified empirical audit framework targeting three alignment failures: (1) verification insufficiency — testing whether certificates hold while task behavior degrades under adversarial attack (PGD at ε=0.25); (2) proxy-fidelity divergence — examining whether task-optimized representations damage underlying neural signal structure using time-domain and spectral reconstruction metrics; (3) latent information exfiltration — measuring whether public-task embeddings retain recoverable private attributes (subject identity). The framework is instantiated on BCI Competition IV 2a and SEED-IV datasets using EEGNet, CSP+LDA, and FBCSP+LDA decoders, with official session-level validation, null controls, and paired statistical tests.

Result: Under PGD attack at ε=0.25, EEGNet classification accuracy drops by 25.7% while Lipschitz-style certificates remain valid across all 9 subjects. A time-domain auxiliary objective reduces reconstruction MSE by 0.1132 but worsens spectral log-MSE, demonstrating proxy-fidelity divergence. Subject identity is recoverable at 48.1% from public-task embeddings versus 6.7% chance, confirming latent information exfiltration. The verification gap persists across EEGNet, CSP+LDA, and FBCSP+LDA, proving architecture-independence.

Conclusion: Mathematical certification alone is insufficient for neural-interface safety; operational auditing addressing verification insufficiency, proxy-fidelity divergence, and latent information leakage is necessary for responsible deployment.

Abstract: Formal robustness certificates for embedded neural-interface models can pass while task accuracy collapses: at perturbation budget e=0.25, EEGNet classification accuracy drops by 25.7% under projected-gradient attack while the Lipschitz-style certificate remains valid for all 9 tested subjects. We argue that this gap between mathematical certification and operational safety is one instance of a broader alignment failure in neural interfaces, where training objectives diverge from user welfare. We propose a unified empirical audit framework organized around three such failures: verification insufficiency, in which certificates pass while task behavior degrades; proxy-fidelity divergence, in which task-optimized representations damage neural signal structure (a time-domain auxiliary objective reduces reconstruction MSE by 0.1132 while worsening spectral log-MSE); and latent information exfiltration, in which public-task embeddings retain private attributes (subject identity recoverable at 48.1% versus 6.7% chance). We instantiate the framework on BCI Competition IV 2a and SEED-IV using multiple deep and classical EEG decoders, official session-level validation, null controls, and paired statistical tests. The verification gap persists across EEGNet, CSP+LDA, and FBCSP+LDA, and is therefore architecture-independent. Our results establish that operational safety auditing, not certificate verification alone, is necessary for responsible neural-interface deployment.

</details>


### [28] [When Do Geometric Algebra Layers Beat Scalarization? A Controlled Study on SO(3)-Equivariant Vector Laws](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06634)
*Fabien Polly*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Clifford algebra Cl(3,0) networks offer no advantage over minimal scalarization on single-stage 3D vector laws, but dramatically outperform on compositional targets that nest group operations, with performance gains tied specifically to rotation chain depth rather than general compositionality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether geometric algebra structure provides benefits beyond exact SO(3)-equivariance in low-data 3D learning, and to characterize precisely when any advantage materializes.

Method: Systematic comparison of Cl(3,0) networks against a minimal scalarization baseline (invariant dot products → small MLP → coefficients on equivariant basis {v_i, v_i × v_j}) across single-stage laws, compositional targets with nested rotations, rotation-free nested operations, and extrapolation tests. Baselines also include Vector Neurons, e3nn, triple-product invariant augmentation, and multiplicative coefficient networks.

Result: On single-stage laws, scalarization matches or beats Cl(3,0) at lower training cost. On compositional targets nesting group elements, Cl(3,0) achieves with 100 samples what scalarization needs 3000 for; the gap persists against strengthened baselines. Required network depth tracks rotation chain length; scalarization collapses below constant predictor on 4-rotation chains. On rotation-free nested cross products, scalarization wins by 24×. No model extrapolates invariant magnitudes better than a constant predictor.

Conclusion: Geometric algebra layers are not a general-purpose shortcut for low-data 3D learning but become useful specifically when targets compose group elements in depth, with the advantage tied to rotation chain length rather than generic compositional structure.

Abstract: Compact networks built from Clifford algebra Cl(3,0) primitives are exactly SO(3)-equivariant and learn synthetic 3D vector laws from few samples. We ask whether the geometric algebra structure itself contributes anything beyond exact equivariance. We compare against a minimal scalarization baseline: invariant dot products fed to a small MLP that outputs coefficients on the equivariant basis {v_i, v_i x v_j}, which is also exactly equivariant. On single-stage laws (rotation by axis-angle, cross product, central force), scalarization matches or beats the Cl(3,0) network at a fraction of the training cost, so the geometric algebra adds nothing there. On compositional targets whose computation graph nests group operations (apply R2 R1 to a point; map a local force through an orientation, then take a torque), the Cl(3,0) network beats scalarization by an order of magnitude in the low-data regime, reaching with 100 samples what the baseline needs 3000 for, and the gap survives strengthening the baseline with the triple-product invariant and 17x more parameters, external Vector Neurons and e3nn baselines, and a multiplicative coefficient network. Ablations show the required network depth tracks the rotation chain length, and scalarization falls below the constant predictor on chains of four rotations. The advantage is not composition per se: on a rotation-free nested cross product, which flattens into polynomial invariant coefficients, scalarization wins by 24x. No tested model, equivariant or not, extrapolates invariant magnitudes: on radius and separation shifts every model is worse than a constant predictor once errors are normalized. We conclude that geometric algebra layers are not a general shortcut for low-data 3D learning, but become useful precisely when the target composes group elements in depth.

</details>


### [29] [Gimitest: A Comprehensive Tool for Testing Reinforcement Learning Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07029)
*Dennis Gross,Quentin Mazouni,Helge Spieker,Arnaud Gotlieb*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Gimitest is an open-source, comprehensive testing framework for single- and multi-agent RL policies that works across diverse environments and gym frameworks, addressing the limitations of existing automated testing methods which are restricted to specific scenarios and algorithms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing automated testing methods for RL policies are narrow in scope — they target only selected environments, testing scenarios, and RL algorithms. There is a need for a unified, comprehensive testing framework that can evaluate RL policy reliability (including safety and adversarial robustness) under varying conditions across both single- and multi-agent settings.

Method: The authors propose a comprehensive testing framework and implement it as Gimitest, an open-source tool. It supports multiple gym frameworks (e.g., Farama Gymnasium, PettingZoo) and allows modifications of their integrated components, enabling flexible, cross-environment testing of RL policies under diverse conditions.

Result: Gimitest is demonstrated to be effective in testing multiple RL policies across standard environments, including Farama Gymnasium (single-agent) and PettingZoo (multi-agent), showcasing its versatility as a testing tool.

Conclusion: Gimitest provides a comprehensive, extensible, and open-source solution for testing single- and multi-agent RL policies, filling a critical gap left by existing environment- and algorithm-specific testing approaches.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) policies can be unsafe and vulnerable to attacks. Ensuring their reliability is often a pain point as existing automated testing methods target only selected environments, testing scenarios, and RL algorithms. To address this, we propose a comprehensive framework for testing single- and multi-agent RL policies under varying conditions. Our implementation of this framework, Gimitest, is an open-source tool that supports various gym frameworks and allows for modifications of their integrated components. This article describes the framework and details Gimitest's functionality and architecture. It showcases its effectiveness in testing multiple RL policies in environments such as the official Farama Gymnasium and PettingZoo.

</details>


### [30] [Optimized Instance Alteration for Explaining and Assessing Robustness of Classifiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06637)
*Evgenii Kuriabov,David Miller,Jia Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A unified optimization framework using an explainability-aware L0 penalty diagnoses misclassification causes and assesses black-box classifier robustness, introducing the Tolerance Region Confusion Matrix to measure class-to-class transition probabilities under bounded perturbations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods treat misclassification diagnosis and robustness assessment separately. A unified, interpretable framework is needed to both explain why misclassifications occur and quantify how easily instances can be flipped across decision boundaries under small, understandable perturbations.

Method: An optimization framework that modifies input instances toward a target label using two objectives: (1) an explainability-aware L0 (XA-L0) penalty encouraging sparse, interpretable feature modifications, and (2) a classifier loss steering the perturbed instance to the desired class. For robustness, the Tolerance Region Confusion Matrix (TOR-CM) is introduced, capturing class-to-class transition probabilities when perturbations are constrained within a tolerance budget.

Result: The method was validated on both image and tabular datasets, demonstrating effective joint delivery of interpretable misclassification diagnosis and robustness assessment.

Conclusion: The proposed unified optimization approach successfully integrates explainability and robustness evaluation for black-box classifiers, with the TOR-Confusion Matrix providing a principled metric for classifier susceptibility under bounded, interpretable perturbations.

Abstract: In this work, we propose a unified approach for diagnosing misclassification and assessing the robustness of black-box classifiers. Central to our method is an optimization framework that modifies an instance so that the classifier predicts a specified target label, while ensuring that the modification remains easily explainable. The objective function contains two components: an explainability-aware $L_0$ (XA-$L_0$) penalty that promotes sparse and interpretable modifications, and a classifier loss objective that steers the perturbed instance toward the desired output. This integrated optimization formulation is used both to identify the underlying causes of misclassification and to evaluate robustness by determining how an instance can change within a tolerance region before being reassigned to another class. To quantify robustness, we introduce the Tolerance Region Confusion Matrix (TOR-Confusion Matrix), which measures a classifier's susceptibility by modeling the class-to-class transition probabilities induced by tolerance-bounded perturbations. We validate the proposed method on both image and tabular datasets, demonstrating its ability to jointly deliver interpretability and robustness assessment.

</details>


### [31] [ORCAID: Oblique Rule-Based Continuous-Action Interpretation for Deep RL Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07235)
*Ignacio D. Lopez-Miguel,Ezio Bartocci,Thomas Eiter,Martin Tappler*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ORCAID extracts interpretable rule-based policies from deep RL agents in mixed continuous-discrete environments with continuous action spaces, using oblique decision trees with a three-stage split search (random initialization, local refinement, backward elimination) and leaf merging.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Explainability in RL remains challenging, especially for continuous action spaces. Distilling an interpretable policy (e.g., rule-based) from a trained deep RL agent is difficult, and existing methods struggle with the complexity of continuous control.

Method: ORCAID trains an oblique decision tree to mimic a deep RL policy. The tree partitions the state space via hyperplanes and fits local linear models at leaves. The core algorithmic contribution is a three-stage split search: (1) efficient random initialization of candidate hyperplanes, (2) local refinement via optimization, and (3) backward elimination to remove redundant splits. Adjacent leaves with similar linear models are then merged to produce a compact set of human-interpretable rules.

Result: Evaluated across multiple RL environments, ORCAID-extracted policies maintain strong performance with few parameters and can even improve upon the original deep RL policy's performance.

Conclusion: ORCAID effectively bridges the gap between high-performing deep RL agents and interpretable rule-based policies in continuous action spaces, offering both transparency and competitive or superior performance.

Abstract: Explainability remains a key issue in reinforcement learning (RL). Distilling an interpretable policy from an agent trained in a complex environment is particularly challenging when the action space is continuous. We introduce ORCAID, a novel method for extracting interpretable rule-based policies from RL agents operating in mixed continuous-discrete environments with continuous action spaces. Our main contribution is an efficient oblique decision tree training algorithm that partitions the state space by hyperplanes and fits local linear models. The key idea lies in a three-stage split search: efficient random initialization, local refinement, and backward elimination. Finally, adjacent leaves are merged to yield a concise set of interpretable rules describing a given deep RL policy. We evaluate ORCAID across multiple RL environments, demonstrating that the extracted rule-based policies maintain strong performance with a low number of parameters and can even be used to improve the performance of the original deep RL policy.

</details>


### [32] [UASPL: Uncertainty-Aware Self-Paced Learning with Evidential Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06638)
*Yifan Zhang,Yuxin Hu,Zhuobin Hao,Xiaozhuan Gao,Lipeng Pan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: UASPL integrates evidential neural network-based uncertainty estimation into self-paced learning, enabling more reliable sample selection by considering predictive reliability — not just loss magnitude. The method uses a Subjective Logic framework with a general loss function that couples sample selection preferences, outperforming existing SPL variants on classification performance, interpretability, and generality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional self-paced learning (SPL) selects "easy" samples based solely on small loss values. However, small loss does not guarantee reliable predictions — a sample with low loss may still be uncertain. This flaw means SPL can misidentify unreliable samples as easy, undermining the curriculum learning principle.

Method: The authors propose UASPL (Uncertainty-Aware Self-Paced Learning), which incorporates evidential neural networks to quantify predictive uncertainty. Within the Subjective Logic framework, they design a general loss function that integrates both loss magnitude and uncertainty estimation. This loss function naturally couples a sample selection preference, making the selection process interpretable, and is extensible to multiple SPL variants.

Result: Experiments on multiple datasets demonstrate that UASPL outperforms other SPL methods in classification performance, interpretability, and generality.

Conclusion: Incorporating uncertainty awareness via evidential neural networks significantly improves self-paced learning. UASPL provides a more principled, interpretable, and generalizable approach to curriculum-based sample selection.

Abstract: Self-paced learning (SPL) is an effective learning paradigm that simulates the human learning process by progressing from easy to difficult samples based on the value of the loss function during the learning process. It has shown great potential in improving model performance and training efficiency. However, the prediction results of samples with smaller loss values are not necessarily reliable, indicating that such samples are not always simple samples for the model. Hence, this article proposes an uncertainty-aware self-paced learning based on evidential neural networks, termed UASPL, which integrates predictive reliability into sample selection through a general loss function within the Subjective Logic framework. This loss function incorporates uncertainty estimation and can be extended to different variants of SPL. Moreover, this loss function couples a sample selection preference, thereby ensuring the interpretability of the sample selection process. Finally, the experimental results on multiple datasets show that UASPL outperforms other SPL methods in terms of classification performance, interpretability, and generality. The source code is available at: https://github.com/treelife979/UASPL.

</details>


### [33] [At-Grok Is Not Converged:A Measurement-Validity Audit for Grokking Representation Metrics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06639)
*Truong Xuan Khanh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Effective rank measurements overstate compression at the grokking transition by 3-5x (MLP) and 1.3-1.5x (transformer); compression lags accuracy by ~10,000 steps. LayerNorm explains the lag size, and the paper provides an audit toolkit for properly separating onset from compression with safeguards against false confidence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work claims that grokking coincides with a rapid compression of network embeddings as measured by effective rank. The authors challenge this, showing that effective rank measurements are misleading — they overstate compression magnitudes, misidentify which cells compress, and conflate the timing of accuracy onset with compression onset. A rigorous methodology is needed to audit such claims.

Method: The authors analyze modular arithmetic grokking dynamics in MLPs and transformers, comparing effective rank trajectories against ground-truth compression behavior. They introduce a one-variable ablation (adding/removing LayerNorm) to isolate what controls the lag between accuracy and compression. They develop an audit suite with: separation of onset from compression, censoring flags, exclusion of boundary cells that never fully generalize, plateau checks on the reference floor, and an adversarial suite that caught a bug in their own pipeline. A secondary depth-law experiment tests generality across architectures.

Result: Effective rank at grokking overstates converged compression by 3-5x on MLPs and 1.3-1.5x on transformers, and on MLPs it erases which cells compress. Compression lags accuracy by ≥10,000 steps. Adding LayerNorm to a transformer reduces compression-at-grok from 87% to 25%, ruling out scale invariance. The MLP depth law (norm budget vs. converged floor) fails on transformers and flips sign under free weight decay.

Conclusion: Grokking and embedding compression are distinct phenomena separated by a significant lag modulated by architectural choices like LayerNorm. Effective rank is an unreliable proxy for compression timing and magnitude. The released audit toolkit provides principled safeguards for future mechanistic interpretability studies of grokking.

Abstract: On modular arithmetic, a network's embedding keeps compressing for tens of thousands of steps after it has already generalized. Reading effective rank at the grokking transition overstates the converged value by 3-5x on an MLP, and by 1.3-1.5x on a transformer trained to convergence; on the MLP it also erases which cells compress at all. Compression lags the accuracy transition by an amount on the order of the time-to-grok, at least 10,000 steps, rather than coinciding with it. A one-variable ablation shows what sets the lag size: adding LayerNorm to an otherwise identical transformer moves the fraction of compression done by the grok step from 0.87 to 0.25, and a pre-registered control rules out scale invariance as the mechanism. We package this as an audit that separates onset from compression, flags censoring, excludes boundary cells that never fully generalize, and checks that the reference floor has plateaued, with an adversarial suite that caught a false-confidence bug in our own branch. A secondary, MLP-specific depth law linking norm budget to converged floor fails a generality test on a transformer and flips sign under free weight decay. Code and the toolkit are released.

</details>


### [34] [The Rank-One Corner: How Much Value Equivalence Does a Task Need from a World Model?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06640)
*Donna Vakalis*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A world model's internal representations of task-relevant structure ("closure") are determined by the dimensionality of its training objective, not its capacity. Replacing a scalar reward with a multi-dimensional objective increases recoverable structure (R² from 0.10 to 0.76), with each added dimension installing a corresponding predictive direction—revealing value equivalence as a dimensional, not binary, property.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work treats model quality as monolithic (reconstruction fidelity, reward prediction accuracy), but tasks only require a narrow subset of predictive coordinates—the "closure." The paper investigates what determines how much of this closure a latent representation captures, challenging the assumption that model capacity or observation richness is the deciding factor.

Method: Experiments use a DreamerV3 stack in a controlled environment with known ground-truth closure. The authors manipulate the dimensionality of the training objective (from scalar/1D up to 4D), then probe latent representations with linear readouts to measure how many predictive directions are installed. Capacity-matched comparisons and "in-situ pressure checks" rule out alternative explanations. A companion closed-loop task where structure is frame-observable tests regime boundaries.

Result: A scalar value signal installs only a 1D projection of multi-dimensional closure (R²=0.10 via linear probe). Using the full objective raises R² to 0.76. Sweeping objective dimensionality from 1 to 4 installs exactly that many directions through an auxiliary head; the same staircase pattern appears (attenuated) in the model's own value head. When task structure is directly observable frame-by-frame, reconstruction alone installs the structure and a scalar objective suffices—delineating the regime boundary.

Conclusion: Value equivalence is dimensional, not all-or-nothing: the scalar reward is merely its rank-1 corner case. A model installs as much task-relevant structure as the dimensionality of the objective it is trained to predict. The objective decides what a latent represents precisely where cheaper training signals (e.g., reconstruction) cannot already recover that structure.

Abstract: A learned world model is usually judged by how faithfully it reconstructs its observations or predicts reward, as though quality were something the model simply has or lacks. But what a task actually needs from a model is narrower: the few predictive coordinates its queries depend on, which we call the closure. We show that how much of that closure a latent comes to represent is set not by the model's capacity or its observations but by the dimensionality of the objective it is trained against, and we measure this directly on a DreamerV3 stack in a controlled environment with known ground-truth closure. An aligned scalar value signal -- the objective at the heart of value equivalence -- installs only a one-dimensional projection of a closure that needs several dimensions: read through a single linear probe, the recoverable structure rises from R^2=0.10 to 0.76 as the scalar is replaced by the full objective. Sweeping the objective's dimensionality from one to four installs exactly that many predictive directions through an auxiliary head, and the same staircase appears -- at attenuated magnitude but the same rank -- through the model's own value head, so the dissociation is dimensional rather than an artifact of head form. Capacity-matched comparisons and in-situ pressure checks rule out the obvious alternatives. The law governs a regime, and we measure its boundary: on a companion closed-loop task whose structure is observable frame by frame, reconstruction installs that structure and the scalar objective suffices -- the objective decides what a latent represents exactly where cheaper training signals cannot already recover it. Value equivalence is thus not all-or-nothing but dimensional: the familiar single-reward objective is its rank-one corner, and a model installs as much of a task's structure as the objective it is asked to predict.

</details>


### [35] [The Approximation Ratio for the Risk of Myopic Bayesian Active Learning for Linear Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06642)
*Stephen Mussmann*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proves a tight (up to constant factors) approximation ratio for the greedy algorithm in optimal experiment design, showing the ratio is linear in the maximum initial leverage score (MILS), a newly identified fundamental quantity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite the widespread use of the greedy algorithm in optimal experiment design and myopic Bayesian active learning for linear regression, no theoretical approximation guarantees existed that characterize its worst-case performance relative to optimal long-term planning.

Method: The authors analyze the greedy algorithm's risk in the linear regression setting and derive an approximation ratio bound that depends on the maximum initial leverage score (MILS). They prove tightness of this bound up to an absolute constant factor.

Result: A first-of-its-kind approximation ratio for the greedy algorithm is established. The ratio is linear in MILS, and this dependence is shown to be tight (up to an absolute constant). The results are illustrated with numerical simulations.

Conclusion: The greedy algorithm's worst-case performance is governed by the maximum initial leverage score, providing both a theoretical justification for when greedy performs well and a cautionary bound for when MILS is large.

Abstract: Active learning studies the fundamental question: what data should we choose to observe? The greedy algorithm in optimal experiment design is a common heuristic and also equivalent to myopic Bayesian active learning for linear regression, the common framework where long-term planning is replaced with the one-step optimal choice. In this work, we prove a first-of-its-kind approximation ratio for the greedy algorithm's risk that is tight up to an absolute constant. The approximation ratio is linear in the maximum initial leverage score (MILS), a newly identified quantity fundamental to the greedy algorithm's performance. Finally, we illustrate the results with simple numerical simulations.

</details>


### [36] [Diffusion enabled Optimal Transport distances for graph matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06646)
*Iman Seyedi,Francesco Archetti*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DsrFGW introduces diffusion processes into semi-relaxed fused Gromov-Wasserstein optimal transport for robust graph comparison, consistently outperforming srFGW on 36 synthetic tasks, especially under noise and partial observation, with accuracy gains up to 20pp and dramatically improved ARI scores.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional Gromov-Wasserstein and semi-relaxed variants (srGW, srFGW) for graph comparison struggle with sparse, noisy, or partially observed graphs. The authors draw inspiration from Graph Diffusion Distance — the principle that graphs are similar if they enable similar information transmission patterns — to overcome these limitations.

Method: DsrFGW extends the semi-relaxed fused Gromov-Wasserstein framework by incorporating diffusion processes that propagate information across graph nodes. This captures both local and global structural patterns while reducing sensitivity to noise and missing edges. The method unifies node features and structural connectivity through optimal transport with adaptive diffusion scales.

Result: Evaluation across 36 synthetic pairwise graph matching tasks (easy, medium, hard) shows DsrFGW consistently outperforms srFGW: accuracy improvements of 0–20 percentage points, dramatic ARI gains (srFGW often yields negative ARI in medium-difficulty settings, i.e., worse than random), and improved clustering quality in 92% of tasks under severe noise. Optimal diffusion scales adapt automatically to problem difficulty.

Conclusion: DsrFGW establishes itself as a robust, diffusion-enhanced framework for graph comparison under structural uncertainty, significantly advancing the state of the art over existing semi-relaxed Gromov-Wasserstein methods.

Abstract: This paper introduces Diffusion Semi-Relaxed Fused Gromov-Wasserstein (DsrFGW), a novel method for graph comparison that unifies node features and structural connectivity through optimal transport. While traditional Gromov-Wasserstein and semi-relaxed variants (srGW, srFGW) capture graph structure, they often struggle with sparse, noisy, or partially observed graphs. Inspired by Graph Diffusion Distance, which posits graphs are similar if they enable similar information transmission patterns, DsrFGW incorporates diffusion processes allowing information propagation across nodes, capturing local and global structural patterns while reducing sensitivity to noise or missing edges. An extensive evaluation on 36 synthetic pairwise graph matching tasks (easy, medium, hard) demonstrates consistent superiority over srFGW, achieving accuracy improvements of 0-20 percentage points and dramatic Adjusted Rand Index (ARI) gains: in medium-difficulty scenarios, srFGW often achieves negative ARI (worse than random) while DsrFGW offers better performance in terms of both internal and external clustering quality measures (i.e., Adjusted Rank Index and Accuracy with respect to the true underlying clusters, respectively). Even under severe noise, DsrFGW improves clustering quality in 92% of the synthetic tasks with optimal diffusion scales adapting to problem difficulty, establishing DsrFGW as a robust framework for graph comparison under structural uncertainty.

</details>


### [37] [Final Checkpoints Are Not Enough: Analyzing Latent Reasoning Faithfulness Along Training Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06648)
*Hengyu Jin,Shu Yang,Di Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper tracks how the faithfulness of latent reasoning evolves across training, finding that faithfulness trajectories diverge by training stage, reasoning paradigm, and answer format — with causal contribution decaying for binary choice but rising for open-ended decoding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work identifies unfaithful behaviors in latent reasoning at converged checkpoints but leaves unexamined how these behaviors develop during training. The authors aim to fill this gap by tracking faithfulness evolution across training for different latent reasoning paradigms.

Method: The authors track faithfulness across saved checkpoints using two techniques: (1) verifiable counterfactual edits on the input, and (2) noise-ablation activation patching on the latent reasoning steps. They compare different latent reasoning paradigms across both output-level and activation-level analyses.

Result: Three key findings: (i) at the output level, different paradigms can look similarly unfaithful at convergence but follow divergent trajectories; (ii) at the activation level, causal contribution of latent reasoning steps decays across training for both paradigms, correlating with output-level flips; (iii) activation-level trajectories diverge by answer format — decaying on binary choice tasks but rising on open-ended decoding.

Conclusion: Latent reasoning faithfulness is not a static property but depends critically on training stage and answer format, suggesting that evaluations at convergence alone provide an incomplete picture of model behavior.

Abstract: Latent reasoning methods perform multi-step inference entirely in the model's continuous hidden states, promising more compact and efficient reasoning. However, these opaque hidden states raise a question of faithfulness: whether these latent reasoning steps causally drive the final answer. Prior work investigates this question at converged checkpoints and reports several unfaithful behaviors, such as latent reasoning steps that can be replaced without changing the answer, but leaves how these behaviors form during training unexamined. We instead track how faithfulness evolves across saved checkpoints for different latent reasoning paradigms, applying a verifiable counterfactual edit on the input and a noise-ablation activation patch on the latent reasoning steps. We find that (i) at the output level, latent reasoning methods can look similarly unfaithful at convergence under counterfactual edits while following qualitatively divergent trajectories; (ii) at the activation level, the causal contribution of latent reasoning steps to the final answer decays across training for both paradigms, with the examples that flip on the output side in (i) also being the examples on which this contribution decays; and (iii) the activation-level trajectory diverges by answer format, decaying on binary choice and rising on open-ended decoding. These findings highlight that latent reasoning faithfulness depends on training stage and answer format.

</details>


### [38] [From Jumps to Signatures: a Generative Method for Temporal Point Processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06652)
*Niels Cariou-Kotlarek,Vasileios Lampos*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces interarrival embedding to extend rough path signatures to discrete event sequences, enabling sigTPP (a signature-based generative model for temporal point processes trained with path-level loss) and three distributional discrepancies for evaluating generative TPP models. sigTPP achieves state-of-the-art performance across multiple benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rough path signatures provide universal feature maps and distribution characterizations for continuous paths, but these guarantees do not extend to the càdlàg paths of Temporal Point Processes (TPPs). Neural TPP models lack global sequence-level training objectives, and evaluation of variable-length event sequences suffers from a lack of distributional discrepancy measures. A unified pathwise framework is needed to address these gaps.

Method: The authors propose the interarrival embedding: a stable, injective lift from jump paths (càdlàg) to continuous paths of bounded variation. This extends signature methods to discrete event sequences. Building on this, they develop sigTPP, a generative model for TPPs trained with a path-level loss on complete trajectories. They also analyze the space of counting paths to derive three distributional discrepancies for model evaluation.

Result: sigTPP achieves the best average rank across eight complementary metrics. It outperforms or matches the strongest baseline within one standard error on 64% of dataset-metric pairs. Using a relative score, sigTPP improves against every baseline by at least 19% on average across synthetic and real-world datasets.

Conclusion: The interarrival embedding successfully bridges rough path signature theory with discrete event data, enabling both principled generative modeling (sigTPP) and rigorous distributional evaluation for TPPs. The strong empirical results validate the pathwise framework as a robust approach for event sequence modeling.

Abstract: Rough path signatures are a universal feature map for continuous paths and, via the expected signature, characterise path distributions. These guarantees do not directly extend to cadlag paths of Temporal Point Processes (TPPs), limiting the use of signature methods for event sequences. Furthermore, neural TPP models, including recent generative approaches, optimise per-event objectives with no global sequence-level loss, while evaluation of variable-length event sequences lacks distributional discrepancy measures. This paper proposes a common pathwise framework for addressing these limitations. We introduce the interarrival embedding, a stable, injective lift from jump paths to continuous paths of bounded variation, extending signature methods to discrete event sequences. Our theoretical contributions give rise to sigTPP, the first signature-based generative model for TPPs, trained using a path-level loss on complete trajectories. We further analyse the space of counting paths and derive three distributional discrepancies, providing mathematically justified tools for evaluating generative TPP models. Across synthetic and real-world datasets, sigTPP achieves the best average rank based on eight complementary metrics, outperforms or is within a standard error of the strongest baseline in 64% of the dataset-metric pairs, and according to a relative score, improves against every baseline by at least 19% on average.

</details>


### [39] [Efficient Long-Horizon Learning for Learned Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06772)
*Xiaolong Huang,Benjamin Thérien,James Harrison,Eugene Belilovsky*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ELO is an efficient meta-training algorithm for learned optimizers that improves long-horizon training by reallocating compute to longer rollouts and using decoupled progressive expert supervision, achieving strong performance on language modeling and image classification with minimal meta-training cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current learned optimizer meta-training suffers from two key problems: (1) inefficient scaling to long-horizon inner problems, and (2) frequent failure to surpass hand-designed optimizers like Adam/Muon. The authors aim to close this gap with better meta-training strategies.

Method: ELO introduces two main techniques: (1) reallocation of redundant meta-training compute from short horizons to longer failure regimes for efficient long-horizon learning, and (2) decoupled progressive expert supervision that provides stable meta-learning signals to improve LO generalization. The method is evaluated on both element-wise and matrix-based learned optimizers.

Result: ELO substantially improves long-unroll performance and out-of-distribution generalization of base learned optimizers. ELO-Celo2 consistently outperforms well-tuned AdamW across all tasks (GPT-2-124M/350M on FineWeb, ViT-B/16, ResNet-50 on ImageNet-1K) and remains competitive with Muon on language modeling. All ELO baselines require less than 7 H100 GPU-hours for meta-training.

Conclusion: ELO demonstrates that efficient long-horizon meta-training with progressive expert supervision enables learned optimizers to finally surpass hand-designed baselines like AdamW at minimal compute cost, marking a significant step toward practical learned optimization.

Abstract: Learned optimization aims to improve upon hand-designed optimizers (e.g., Adam and Muon) by meta-learning small neural network optimizers over a distribution of tasks. While recent work has greatly advanced the architectural design and inductive biases of learned optimizers (LOs), current meta-training approaches still suffer from two main difficulties: (1) they cannot efficiently scale meta-training to long-horizon inner problems and (2) they often fail to surpass comparable hand-designed optimizers. To address these limitations, we propose Efficient Long-hOrizon (ELO) learning, an efficient meta-training algorithm that (1) reallocates redundant meta-training compute to longer failure regimes, achieving efficient long-horizon learning, and (2) enforces decoupled progressive expert supervision, providing stable meta-learning signals that additionally improve the generalization of LOs. Our empirical study evaluates ELO for meta-training both element-wise and matrix-based LOs. Across downstream language modeling (GPT-2-124M/350M on FineWeb) and image classification (ViT-B/16, ResNet-50 on ImageNet-1K) tasks, ELO substantially improves the long-unroll performance and out-of-distribution generalization of the base LOs. In particular, ELO-Celo2 consistently outperforms well-tuned AdamW across all evaluated tasks, while remaining competitive with Muon on language modeling. \textit{Notably, all ELO baselines require less than 7 H100 GPU-hours for meta-training.}

</details>


### [40] [LEMUR 2: Unlocking Neural Network Diversity for AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06839)
*Tolgay Atinc Uzun,Waleed Khalid,Saif U Din,Sai Revanth Mulukuledu,Akashdeep Singh,Chandini Vysyaraju,Raghuvir Duvvuri,Avi Goyal,Yashkumar Rajeshbhai Lukhi,Muhammad A. Hussain,Krunal Jesani,Usha Shrestha,Yash Mittal,Roman Kochnev,Pritam Kadam,Mohsin Ikram,Harsh R. Moradiya,Alice Arslanian,Dmitry Ignatov,Radu Timofte*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LEMUR 2 is a large-scale, extensible NAS benchmark comprising over 14,000 architectures and 750,000+ training records, unifying generative, evaluative, and deployment pipelines across multimodal tasks and heterogeneous hardware to enable LLM-driven AutoML and cross-domain architectural generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing NAS benchmarks (NAS-Bench, NATS-Bench) are limited to narrow, task-specific design spaces and lack cross-domain or deployment-aware evaluation. There is a need for a unified framework that captures architectural diversity, cross-task transferability, and real-device performance to support the emerging paradigm of LLM-driven AutoML.

Method: LEMUR 2 generates architectures via AST-based code mutation, genetic/reinforcement-learning evolution, fractal architecture synthesis, and LLM-guided synthesis (including NN-RAG, a retrieval-augmented system using motifs from 900+ PyTorch modules). It integrates NN-VR and NN-Lite pipelines for automated deployment and latency benchmarking on mobile and Unity-based VR platforms. The dataset spans multimodal tasks (image captioning, text-to-image synthesis, language modeling).

Result: The framework produces a dataset of over 14,000 distinct architectures and 750,000+ structured training records with performance metrics, hyperparameters, and task outcomes, along with real-device latency metadata across heterogeneous hardware platforms.

Conclusion: LEMUR 2 provides a new data foundation for reproducible, data-driven AI design, enabling LLM fine-tuning on architectural data and supporting cross-platform empirical validation. It advances LLM-driven AutoML and architectural generalization across modalities and hardware.

Abstract: Existing NAS benchmarks (e.g., NAS-Bench, NATS-Bench) cover only narrow, task-specific regions of the architectural design space and lack cross-domain or deployment-aware evaluation. LEMUR 2 introduces a large-scale, extensible framework unifying generative, evaluative, and deployment pipelines to unlock neural-network diversity. It comprises over 14,000 distinct architectures and more than 750,000 structured training records documenting model performance, hyperparameters, and task outcomes. These models were produced through AST-based code mutation, genetic and reinforcement-learning evolution, generation of fractal architectures, and synthesis guided by a Large Language Model (LLM). This includes deep models generated with the retrieval-augmented system NN-RAG, which derived and used architectural motifs from over 900 PyTorch modules extracted from public repositories. LEMUR 2 further employs NN-VR and NN-Lite pipelines for automated deployment and latency benchmarking on heterogeneous mobile and Unity-based VR platforms, providing real-device performance metadata. It spans multimodal tasks, image captioning, text-to-image synthesis, and language modeling, supporting cross-domain analysis of architectural transferability. By linking diverse architectures, tasks, and deployment data, LEMUR 2 provides the data foundation for LLM fine-tuning and coupling diverse architectural origins with large-scale, cross-platform empirical validation. This dataset defines a new basis for reproducible and data-driven AI design, advancing the emerging paradigm of LLM-driven AutoML and architectural generalization across modalities and hardware.

</details>


### [41] [FedCVESA: Taking Away Training Data in Federated Learning via Correlation Value Encoding and Segmented Aggregation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07314)
*Chongkai Li,Bang Zhang,Wenjian Luo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces FedCVESA, a white-box attack in federated learning where a malicious server actively encodes private training data from target clients into the global model using a Pearson-correlation regularizer, then recovers that data later. Segmented aggregation preserves encoded carrier parameters against overwriting during federated averaging.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While Taking Away Training Data (TATD) attacks exploiting deep model memorization have been demonstrated in centralized settings, they remain unexplored in federated learning. The key challenge is that multi-client averaging during FL aggregation can overwrite encoded private data, potentially neutralizing such attacks. The authors aim to systematically investigate whether FL can serve as a parameter-level memorization channel for active TATD attacks under a malicious-server threat model.

Method: The authors propose FedCVESA (Federated Correlation Value Encoding Attack), extending the centralized CVEA to FL. A malicious server selects n target clients and applies a Pearson-correlation regularizer to their loss functions, causing private training data to be progressively encoded into designated "carrier parameters" of the global model. To mitigate the overwriting effect of standard federated averaging, they introduce segmented aggregation: carrier parameters are preserved from target clients without averaging, while remaining parameters undergo standard FedAvg. The method is evaluated under white-box malicious-server assumptions with Dirichlet non-IID data partitions.

Result: Experiments on MNIST, Fashion-MNIST, and CIFAR-10 under Dirichlet non-IID partitions demonstrate that FedCVESA successfully reconstructs semantically meaningful private training images from the final global model while maintaining acceptable main-task accuracy. This confirms that FL can be exploited as a parameter-level memorization channel for active TATD attacks in the studied setting.

Conclusion: Federated learning is vulnerable to active, white-box TATD attacks where a malicious server can deliberately encode and later extract private client data through parameter-level memorization, even under non-IID data distributions. The proposed segmented aggregation technique effectively preserves encoded data against averaging-based overwriting, revealing a previously understudied privacy threat in FL.

Abstract: Federated learning (FL) avoids explicit data exposure by keeping raw data on local clients, yet privacy risks remain in the training process and the learned model itself. Recently, centralized Taking Away Training Data (TATD) attacks have shown that malicious training could abuse the memorization capacity of deep models to store and later recover training data. However, this memorization-based threat has not been systematically studied under FL environments, where multi-client averaging could overwrite encoded training data. In this paper, we study a white-box TATD attack in which a malicious server selects n target clients from K participating clients and actively writes private training data into the global model during federated training. We propose FedCVESA, a federated variant of Correlation Value Encoding Attack (CVEA), by adding a Pearson-correlation regularizer to the loss function of target clients, so that private training data are gradually encoded into selected model parameters, referred to as carrier parameters. To reduce the overwriting of carrier parameters during server aggregation, we further propose segmented aggregation over dispersed carrier parameters, preserving selected carrier parameters while keeping standard averaging on the remaining parameters. Experiments on MNIST, Fashion-MNIST, and CIFAR-10 under Dirichlet non-IID partitions show that the proposed method can steal semantically meaningful private training images from the trained model while maintaining acceptable main-task utility in a controlled proof-of-concept setting. These results demonstrate that FL can become a parameter-level memorization channel for active TATD attack under the studied white-box malicious-server setting.

</details>


### [42] [Agon: Competitive Cross-Model RL with Implicit Rival Grading of Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07690)
*Vladislav Beliaev*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Agon introduces adversarial self-play between two models that alternate drafting and solving roles, each rewarded for out-reasoning the other. This provides implicit process-level training signal without labels or reward models, creating a progressively stronger rival. It doubles GRPO's pass@1 on hard math reasoning and generalizes across model families.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current reinforcement learning for reasoning models (e.g., GRPO) only grades final answers, not the reasoning trace. On hard problems, this incentivizes models to generate longer outputs rather than genuinely improving reasoning quality, since no signal exists for what constitutes "good thinking." A method is needed that provides implicit process-level feedback without requiring expensive human labels or reward models.

Method: Agon pairs two comparably strong but behaviorally different models in an adversarial self-play loop. Both attempt the same problem in alternating roles: one drafts a solution, the other reads the draft and then solves the problem. Each model is rewarded for out-solving the other (i.e., succeeding where the rival, having seen its work, fails). This creates implicit process-level grading — to win, a model must produce reasoning that holds up under a rival's scrutiny. Because both models are continuously optimized, each faces an increasingly stronger adversary, a dynamic single-model RL cannot replicate. At inference, the pair deploys as a two-stage cascade: one drafts, the other answers after reading the draft.

Result: On the hard split of DeepMath with Qwen3, Agon doubles GRPO's pass@1. This improvement is roughly eight times the gain achieved by an untrained Mixture-of-Agents pass over the same base model. The result replicates on competitive-programming code tasks and across different model families (Qwen3.5, Gemma 4).

Conclusion: Adversarial self-play between paired models provides effective implicit reasoning supervision, substantially outperforming single-model RL approaches. The authors identify latent-space reasoning between the two models as the natural next step.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards (e.g. GRPO) is the engine behind today's reasoning models, yet it grades only the final answer. On hard problems this trains models to write more rather than to think better, since the trace itself is never graded and no label for good thinking exists. We introduce Agon, which makes two competing models each other's graders. Both attempt the same problem; in alternating roles, one drafts a solution and the other reads it while solving, and each is rewarded for out-solving the other. To win, a model must out-reason a rival that has seen its work, so reasoning is judged implicitly during training, with no process labels and no reward model. Because both models are optimized, each faces a progressively stronger rival, which single-model RL cannot provide. The two need only be comparably strong and behaviorally different. At inference the pair deploys as it trains, a two-stage cascade in which one model drafts and the other answers after reading the draft. On the hard split of DeepMath with Qwen3, this doubles GRPO's pass@1, roughly eight times the gain of an untrained Mixture-of-Agents pass over the same base. The ordering replicates on competitive-programming code and across model families (Qwen3.5, Gemma 4). For now the models talk in text; the next step is to let them reason together in latent space.

</details>


### [43] [Where to Intervene? Benchmarking Fairness-Aware Learning on Differentially Private Synthetic Tabular Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07471)
*Vinícius Gabriel Angelozzi,Héber H. Arcolezi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper presents the first systematic benchmark evaluating fairness interventions on differentially private synthetic tabular data. Using the state-of-the-art AIM synthesizer, the authors assess pre-, in-, and post-processing fairness strategies across four datasets and varying privacy budgets. Key finding: post-processing methods offer the most stable fairness-utility trade-offs under DP constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Differential Privacy (DP) and fairness are both critical for responsible ML in high-stakes domains, but they often conflict—DP can amplify demographic disparities. Little prior work has systematically studied whether fairness interventions remain effective when applied to DP synthetic data, motivating this first comprehensive benchmark.

Method: The authors benchmark four pipeline configurations (Baseline, DP-only, Fair-only, DP+Fair) using the AIM marginal-based DP synthesizer across four datasets. They evaluate three categories of fairness interventions (pre-processing, in-processing, post-processing) under a wide range of privacy budgets (ε), measuring both utility and multiple group fairness metrics.

Result: DP alone degrades both utility and fairness. Applying fairness interventions partially restores equitable outcomes. Post-processing methods consistently provide the most stable fairness-utility trade-offs across privacy budgets and synthesizers, achieving strong fairness gains while maintaining competitive utility compared to pre- and in-processing approaches.

Conclusion: Fairness interventions can mitigate DP-induced disparities in synthetic tabular data, with post-processing emerging as the most robust strategy. The authors release all code and artifacts for reproducibility and to spur further research on the privacy-fairness-utility trade-off.

Abstract: Machine learning models are increasingly deployed in high-stakes domains, raising concerns about both privacy and fairness. Differential Privacy (DP) has become a gold standard for privacy-preserving data analysis, while fairness-aware mechanisms aim to mitigate discrimination against underrepresented groups. However, these objectives can conflict: DP often amplifies disparities across demographic groups, and little is known about whether established fairness interventions remain effective under DP constraints. In this work, we present, to our knowledge, the first systematic evaluation of fairness interventions on differentially private synthetic tabular data. Our benchmark centers on the Adaptive Iterative Mechanism (AIM), identified as the state-of-the-art marginal-based DP synthesizer (Cormode et al. 2025). We thus evaluate fairness interventions across four datasets, multiple group fairness metrics, and three categories of mitigation strategies (pre-processing, in-processing, and post-processing) under a wide range of privacy budgets. We compare four pipeline configurations: (Baseline) training on original data; (DP-only) training on DP synthetic data; (Fair-only) applying fairness mechanisms on original data; and (DP+Fair) combining fairness mechanisms with DP synthetic data. Our results demonstrate that while DP alone can degrade both utility and fairness, applying fairness interventions can partially restore equitable outcomes. Among them, post-processing methods tend to provide more stable fairness-utility trade-offs across privacy budgets and synthesizers, achieving strong fairness improvements while preserving competitive utility relative to other intervention stages. We release all code, data, and experimental artifacts in an open-source repository to ensure full reproducibility and to support future research on the privacy-fairness-utility trade-off.

</details>


### [44] [Distributed Sparse Interventions in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07128)
*Maximilian S. Ernst,Lorenz Linhardt,Aaron Peikert,Oliver Eberle*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DSI is a sparse, neuron-level intervention method that identifies small sets of neurons (as few as 0.01%) across layers to steer language model behavior, capturing nonlinear interactions that global direction-based steering misses, and enabling fine-grained control and analysis of task composition.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing model steering approaches operate along global directions in activation space and assume task representations are linear and additive. This misses substantial neuron-specific nonlinear effects. The authors aim to develop intervention methods that account for nonlinearities and cross-layer neuron interactions to achieve more precise behavioral control.

Method: Distributed Sparse Interventions (DSI): identifies sparse sets of neurons across layers whose manipulation elicits task-relevant computations. DSI accounts for nonlinearities and inter-neuron interactions, and adopts a set-based perspective that enables computation over identified neuron sets for cross-task analysis.

Result: DSI successfully activates task behavior in instruction-tuned LMs by intervening on as few as 0.01% of neurons. The set-based approach enables analysis of individual neurons' roles across different tasks, demonstrating fine-grained control and offering insights into task composition.

Conclusion: Sparse, distributed, neuron-level interventions are highly effective for steering model behavior, outperform global direction-based methods by capturing nonlinearities, and advance understanding of how tasks are represented and composable in language models.

Abstract: Language models perform a wide range of tasks at varying levels of abstraction with the capacity to flexibly infer tasks from context, execute multiple tasks simultaneously, and select among competing tasks. To study the role of model components in task behaviour, their causal influence can be investigated through interventions. Prior work on model steering has largely focused on interventions along global directions in activation space, modeling task representations as approximately linear and additive. By studying interventions at the neuron level, we find substantial, neuron-specific nonlinear effects on model outputs that are not captured by current steering approaches. We introduce Distributed Sparse Interventions (DSI), an intervention approach that considers nonlinearities and interactions between neurons across layers to identify sparse sets of neurons that elicit task-relevant computations. Across a range of tasks, we demonstrate that DSI can activate task behaviour in instruction-tuned language models by localising and intervening on as few as 0.01% of neurons, highlighting the effectiveness of sparse, distributed interventions in the neuron basis. Additionally, adopting a set-based perspective enables computations over the identified neuron sets, offering insights into the roles of individual neurons by analysing their effects across tasks. Through sparse interventions, DSI enables fine-grained control over model behaviour, localisation of task-relevant neuron sets, and furthers our understanding of task composition.

</details>


### [45] [Predicting LLM Safety Before Release by Simulating Deployment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07184)
*Marcus Williams,Hannah Sheahan,Cameron Raymond,Tomek Korbak,Deng Pan,Peilin Yang,Leon Maksin,Ningyi Xie,Phillip Guo,Ian Kivlichan,Micah Carroll*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Deployment simulation regenerates model responses from real conversation prefixes to audit misalignments and estimate pre-deployment misbehavior rates. Tested across four GPT-5 deployments, it outperforms adversarial baselines and yields estimates close to production traffic, while also showing feasibility using public chat datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional pre-deployment safety evaluations suffer from insufficient coverage, unrepresentativeness, and evaluation-awareness (models recognizing they are being tested). These limitations mean evaluators have little reliable evidence about how often undesired behavior will actually occur post-deployment.

Method: De-identified conversations from a prior model deployment are used as seeds; the initial conversation prefix is held fixed, and the candidate model regenerates the next response. These regenerated responses are audited for novel misalignments and used to estimate misbehavior prevalence.
