The method is evaluated via registered, outcome-blinded predictions for GPT-5.4 and retrospective analyses of three earlier GPT-5 releases, with comparisons against adversarially selected production data and traditional evaluations. The approach is also tested when seeded from public chat datasets rather than private production logs.

Result: Deployment simulation produced informative estimates of post-deployment misbehavior rates and outperformed baselines using adversarially selected production data. Its point estimates were substantially closer to production traffic than traditional evaluations. Tool resampling realism was identified as a central challenge, with results suggesting it is surmountable. Seeding from public chat datasets still yielded informative estimates about production misbehavior rates.

Conclusion: Deployment simulation enables more quantitative pre-deployment risk assessment by helping evaluators forecast real-world language model behavior. It supports external researchers in running deployment-grounded evaluations without access to private production logs.

Abstract: Pre-deployment safety evaluations aim to inform the downstream risks of releasing a new AI model. Yet most evaluations provide limited evidence about how often undesired model behavior will occur in deployment: they generally have insufficient coverage, are unrepresentative, and are generally recognizable as tests. To address these concerns, we study a simple way to simulate a model deployment: starting from de-identified conversations from a previous model deployment, we hold fixed the initial conversation prefix and regenerate the next response using a candidate model. The resulting responses can then both be audited for novel misalignments and used to estimate the prevalence of model misbehavior before deployment. We evaluate deployment simulation across four GPT-5-series deployments, using registered, outcome-blinded predictions for GPT-5.4 and retrospective analyses of three earlier releases. We find that deployment simulation produces informative estimates of post-deployment misbehavior rates and outperforms baselines based on adversarially selected production data; its evaluation-awareness point estimates were also much closer to production traffic than those from traditional evaluations. We also identify the realism of tool resampling as a central challenge for further improving predictions and share results suggesting that this challenge is surmountable even in complex tool-use settings. Finally, we show that deployment simulation can be seeded from public chat datasets and remain informative about production misbehavior rates, suggesting a path for external researchers to run deployment-grounded evaluations without access to private production logs. Overall, deployment simulation helps evaluators forecast how language models will behave in the real world and supports more quantitative assessment of deployment risk.

</details>


### [46] [Geometric--Nongeometric Optimizer Calculus: A Modular Language for Reachable Gradient Methods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07206)
*Zavier Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces a modular calculus for analyzing adaptive optimizers by decomposing them into geometric (metric/preconditioner) and nongeometric (memory, step-size, noise, etc.) components. Proves a direction-expressivity theorem, defines residual diagnostics for metric families, and frames optimizer design as Pareto optimization over module budgets rather than a single ranking.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Adaptive optimizers combine many intertwined mechanisms (preconditioning, momentum, step-size control, stochasticity, etc.), making it difficult to audit which components contribute to observed behavior. Existing optimizer comparisons lack a systematic language to isolate geometric expressivity from other factors like estimation error or discretization.

Method: Proposes "geometric–nongeometric optimizer calculus," a modular framework separating optimizers into: (1) a geometric module—a positive cometric family mapping covectors to parameter-space directions; and (2) seven nongeometric modules (information, memory, control, operator, noise, target, discretization). Proves a direction-expressivity theorem characterizing when full positive-definite geometry expresses all strict descent directions. Defines restricted direction residuals and trajectory-level residual complexity to quantify mismatch between actual and ideal descent directions. Validates with diagnostic prototypes: a full-metric probe on quadratic benchmarks and a Muon-style PyTorch implementation.

Result: The direction-expressivity theorem shows that away from critical points, full positive-definite geometry expresses exactly the strict descent directions. Diagonal and block geometries have exact expressivity conditions characterized by restricted direction residuals. The decomposition cleanly separates direction-level diagnostics from condition-number complexity. Diagnostic prototypes demonstrate the framework: a high-information full-metric probe achieves numerical precision on deterministic quadratics, and a Muon-style implementation shows matrix-operator updates are auditable within the calculus.

Conclusion: Optimizer design should be framed as Pareto optimization over module budgets rather than seeking a single universal ordering. The proposed calculus provides a principled language for auditing and designing adaptive optimizers by separating geometric expressivity from nongeometric mechanisms, enabling systematic diagnosis of optimizer behavior without conflating distinct sources of error.

Abstract: Adaptive optimizers mix several mechanisms: a metric or preconditioner maps gradients to descent directions, while estimation, memory, step-size control, constraints, stochasticity, target modification, and discretization determine which directions are available and how they are used. We introduce geometric--nongeometric optimizer calculus, a modular language for auditing reachable gradient methods under explicit oracle, budget, state, and rule constraints. The geometric module is a positive cometric family that maps covectors to parameter-space directions; the nongeometric modules are information, memory, control, operator, noise, target, and discretization mechanisms. The main formal result is a direction-expressivity theorem: away from critical points, full positive-definite geometry expresses exactly the strict descent directions. We then define restricted direction residuals for admissible metric families, prove exact expressivity conditions for diagonal and block geometries, and separate this direction-level diagnostic from condition-number geometric complexity. The resulting design problem is a Pareto optimization over module budgets, not a single universal optimizer ordering. We also lift pointwise residuals to a trajectory-level residual complexity that couples direction mismatch with the variation of the explaining geometry. We include diagnostic prototypes only as evidence for the language: a high-information full-metric probe solves deterministic quadratic benchmarks to numerical precision, while a practical Muon-style PyTorch candidate gives small-scale evidence that matrix-operator updates can be audited by the calculus. The paper is a theory and benchmark-language manuscript; it does not claim large-scale optimizer state-of-the-art performance.

</details>


### [47] [Hypergraph Neural Stochastic Diffusion: An SDE Framework for Uncertainty Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07330)
*Zhiheng Zhou,Mengyao Zhou,Dengyi Zhao,Xingqin Qi,Guiying Yan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HyperNSD is a stochastic differential equation framework that models hypergraph representations as evolving stochastic processes, enabling intrinsic uncertainty quantification through trajectory variability rather than post-hoc confidence scores.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing uncertainty estimation methods for hypergraphs rely on final predictions or costly ensembles/Bayesian inference, failing to capture how uncertainty evolves during representation learning. Hypergraph uncertainty also has unique sources: noisy attributes, ambiguous labels, variable incidence structures, and complex higher-order dependencies beyond what pairwise graphs exhibit.

Method: HyperNSD uses a neural SDE with two learnable components: a drift function capturing deterministic higher-order diffusion over node-hyperedge incidence structures, and a stochastic forcing function modeling structural ambiguity and representation noise. Predictive uncertainty is quantified directly from the variability of stochastic representation trajectories. Neural drift and diffusion networks enable joint learning of prediction and uncertainty propagation.

Result: The framework is theoretically proven to be well-posed, perturbation-stable, permutation-equivariant, and numerically convergent. Experiments on multiple hypergraph benchmarks show reliable uncertainty estimation for OOD and misclassification detection while maintaining competitive prediction accuracy.

Conclusion: HyperNSD provides a principled stochastic-dynamical framework for trustworthy higher-order representation learning, bridging the gap between hypergraph neural networks and rigorous uncertainty quantification.

Abstract: Hypergraph neural networks have shown powerful capability in modeling higher-order relations, yet their predictive uncertainty remains underexplored. Unlike pairwise graphs, uncertainty in hypergraphs arises not only from noisy attributes and ambiguous labels, but also from variations in node-hyperedge incidence structures and complex higher-order dependencies. Existing approaches mainly estimate uncertainty from final predictions or rely on computationally expensive ensembles and Bayesian inference, limiting their ability to capture uncertainty evolution during representation learning. In this paper, we propose Hypergraph Neural Stochastic Diffusion(HyperNSD), a stochastic differential equation framework for uncertainty estimation on hypergraphs. HyperNSD models hypergraph representations as stochastic processes evolving over node-hyperedge incidence structures. A learnable drift function captures deterministic higher-order diffusion dynamics, while a learnable stochastic forcing function characterizes structural ambiguity and representation noise. Predictive uncertainty is directly quantified through the variability of stochastic representation trajectories, providing an intrinsic uncertainty measure beyond post-hoc confidence scores. We formulate HyperNSD with neural drift and diffusion networks, enabling joint learning of prediction and uncertainty propagation. Theoretical analyses establish well posedness, perturbation stability,permutation equivariance, and numerical convergence of the proposed stochastic dynamics. Experiments on multiple hypergraph benchmarks demonstrate that HyperNSD achieves reliable uncertainty estimation for out-of-distribution and misclassification detection while preserving competitive prediction accuracy. These results provide a principled stochastic-dynamical framework for trustworthy higher-order representation learning.

</details>


### [48] [Nonlinear Bandit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07304)
*Tianshuo Zheng,Ting Wu,Zhi-Hua Zhou,Keqin Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper studies generalized linear bandits (GLB) under heavy-tailed noise, proposing the EHM algorithm based on online mirror descent with adaptive Huber loss and one-pass update. It achieves near-optimal regret, eliminates a commonly needed parameter via a link function property, extends to piecewise-constant contexts (PGLB-EHM), and further addresses nonlinear bandits (NB-EHM) with bisection and affine lifting to achieve sublinear regret.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Heavy-tailed distributions are prevalent in real-world applications (recommendation, finance, healthcare), yet standard GLB methods degrade under such noise. Existing robust approaches often incur high computational cost or require knowledge of nuisance parameters. The authors aim to design computationally efficient algorithms with strong regret guarantees that also relax restrictive parametric assumptions.

Method: The core algorithm, EHM, combines online mirror descent (OMD) with an adaptive Huber loss that updates in one pass (O(1) per round). A link function property (from Sawarni et al., 2025) removes the need for a commonly required parameter. For piecewise-constant contexts, the method is revised into PGLB-EHM with preserved regret bounds. For nonlinear bandits, NB-EHM incorporates a bisection method with special restrictions, and affine lifting extends it to the general NB setting.

Result: EHM achieves almost optimal regret of Õ(T^{1/(1+ε)}). PGLB-EHM retains the same regret order under piecewise-constant contexts. NB-EHM with affine lifting yields sublinear regret for general nonlinear bandits. All algorithms maintain O(1) per-round computational complexity.

Conclusion: The proposed framework effectively handles heavy-tailed noise in generalized linear and nonlinear bandits with strong theoretical guarantees and practical computational efficiency, eliminating nuisance parameter dependence and extending gracefully to non-stationary and nonlinear settings.

Abstract: In this paper we first study the problem of generalized linear bandit (GLB) under heavy-tailed noise. The characteristics of heavy-tailed distributions are widely observed in real-world applications such as personalized recommendation, financial markets, and medical treatments. Based on the online mirror descent (OMD) method, we propose an algorithm EHM that extends the adaptive Huber loss method (Wang et al., 2025) with one-pass update ($\mathcal{O}(1)$ computational complexity with respect to current round $t$ and the time horizon $T$), which simultaneously achieves an almost optimal regret of $\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(T^{\frac{1}{1+ε}})$ where $T$ is the time horizon. In addition, by utilizing a special property of some link function (Sawarni et al., 2025), our algorithm eliminates the need to know a commonly used parameter. Next, we study the GLB problem under the case when contextual characteristic becomes piecewise constant, and we slightly revised former algorithm to obtain the PGLB-EHM algorithm. After theoretical analysis, we prove that the regret upper bound order stays the same. Furthermore, we look deeper into a special case of nonlinear bandit (NB) and present the NB-EHM algorithm with bisection method and special restriction. Eventually we utilize the affine lifting approach and show that the general NB problem can be applied with NB-EHM to achieve a sublinear regret bound.

</details>


### [49] [Latency-Aware Bid Acceptance under Operational Feasibility: A Public Benchmark with Hindsight Ceilings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07343)
*Aswin Chandrasekaran*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FreightBidBench is a public, reproducible benchmark for online truckload bid acceptance using open government data. The paper develops two hindsight ceilings (an LP relaxation and a tighter Lagrangian-per-truck relaxation) and introduces a parametric surrogate-rollout cascade policy that recovers ~98% of full-rollout profit at 40–56% of the decision latency, and is statistically indistinguishable from rollout on tight-capacity scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Public, reproducible benchmarks for online truckload bid acceptance — a closed-loop stochastic decision problem involving real-time accept/reject decisions under operational feasibility, repositioning costs, and opportunity cost — are scarce. Existing routing benchmarks are static, and dynamic-fleet studies rely on private operator data, hindering reproducibility and fair comparison.

Method: The authors construct FreightBidBench, a dependency-free, closed-loop benchmark calibrated from public Freight Analysis Framework and USDA truck rate data, with explicit feasibility constraints (pickup reach, appointment windows, simplified hours-of-service, stochastic yard delays) and economics (service-failure penalties, terminal fleet value, daily price-premium windows). They develop two full-horizon hindsight ceilings: a simple LP-style relaxation and a tighter Lagrangian-per-truck information relaxation retaining per-truck hours-of-service and sequencing structure. They also introduce a parametric surrogate-rollout cascade policy with boundary-band and scarcity-pressure escalation triggers.

Result: The Lagrangian-per-truck relaxation is 20.7% tighter than the LP relaxation on a tight-capacity scenario and 39.3% tighter on a scarce-capacity scenario. The best simple policy retains 91.0% (tight) and 86.5% (scarce) of rollout profit; the standard-library surrogate retains 94.2% and 89.3%. The cascade at a single escalation band recovers roughly 98% on both scenarios at 40–56% of rollout's mean decision latency, and on the tight scenario is statistically indistinguishable from the rollout teacher (paired-bootstrap 95% CI on profit delta spans zero).

Conclusion: FreightBidBench provides the first public, reproducible closed-loop benchmark for truckload bid acceptance. The Lagrangian information relaxation offers a significantly tighter bound than LP relaxation. The surrogate-rollout cascade achieves near-optimal profit with substantially lower computational cost, making it practical for real-time deployment.

Abstract: Online truckload bid acceptance is a closed-loop stochastic decision problem in which a carrier or broker must, in real time, accept or reject a tendered load subject to operational feasibility, fleet repositioning costs, and opportunity cost against future demand. Public, reproducible benchmarks for this problem are scarce: existing routing benchmarks are static, while dynamic-fleet studies typically rely on private operator data. We introduce FreightBidBench, a public-calibrated, dependency-free, closed-loop benchmark in which feasibility (pickup reach, appointment windows, simplified hours-of-service, stochastic yard delays) and economics (service-failure penalty, terminal fleet value, daily price-premium window) are explicit, versioned, and reproducible from public Freight Analysis Framework and U.S. Department of Agriculture truck rate data. We develop two full-horizon hindsight ceilings: a simple LP style relaxation and a tighter Lagrangian-per-truck information relaxation that retains per-truck hours-of-service and sequencing structure and is 20.7% tighter than the LP relaxation on a tight-capacity scenario and 39.3% tighter on a scarce-capacity scenario. We introduce a parametric surrogate-rollout cascade with boundary-band and scarcity-pressure escalation triggers. On ten-seed tight and scarce scenarios, the best simple policy retains 91.0% and 86.5% of rollout profit and the standard-library surrogate 94.2% and 89.3%; a cascade at a single escalation band recovers roughly 98% on both at 40-56% of rollout's mean decision latency, and on the tight scenario is statistically indistinguishable from the rollout teacher (paired-bootstrap 95% CI on the profit delta spans zero).

</details>


### [50] [Mechanistic Interpretability for Neural Networks: Circuits, Sparse Features and Symbolic Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07316)
*Pranav Sawant,Jakub Krejčí*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A comprehensive survey of mechanistic interpretability, covering Transformer circuit analysis, superposition/polysemanticity challenges, Sparse Autoencoders for feature disentanglement, steering vectors for behavioral control, and connections to neurosymbolic AI for rule extraction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional XAI methods only capture surface-level input-output correlations and fail to open the "black box" of neural networks. For high-stakes deployments, understanding internal algorithms is critical for safety and auditability. Mechanistic interpretability aims to reverse-engineer these internal computations directly.

Method: The paper surveys several methodological pillars: (1) Transformer circuit analysis examining residual streams, attention heads, and induction heads; (2) Sparse Autoencoders (SAEs) and transcoders to decompose superposition and polysemantic activations into monosemantic features; (3) steering vectors and causal interventions to actively modulate model behavior; and (4) neurosymbolic frameworks that translate neural representations into executable symbolic rules.

Result: The survey demonstrates that mechanistic interpretability tools can successfully decompose tangled representations into human-interpretable features, reveal how induction heads implement in-context learning, enable causal control over model outputs via steering vectors, and bridge connectionist and symbolic AI through rule extraction.

Conclusion: Mechanistic interpretability represents a paradigm shift from correlational to causal understanding of neural networks. The convergence of circuit analysis, sparse coding, causal interventions, and neurosymbolic synthesis offers a viable path toward transparent, auditable, and steerable AI systems suitable for safety-critical applications.

Abstract: This article offers a comprehensive overview of mechanistic interpretability, an emerging field that seeks to reverse-engineer the internal algorithms of modern neural networks. While traditional explainable AI methods often stop at surface-level input-output correlations, this approach directly addresses the opaque "black box" nature of machine learning models, which is essential for ensuring safety and auditability in high-stakes deployments. The paper provides a detailed examination of Transformer circuit analysis, exploring how internal components like the residual stream, attention mechanisms, and induction heads drive complex tasks and in-context learning. It subsequently tackles the core challenge of superposition and polysemanticity, demonstrating how tools like Sparse Autoencoders (SAEs) and transcoders can decompose tangled network activations into distinct, human-interpretable features. Furthermore, the paper explores methods for actively controlling and modifying model behavior through steering vectors and causal interventions. Finally, it connects these mechanistic insights with neurosymbolic AI frameworks designed to translate neural representations into explicit, executable logical rules.

</details>


### [51] [On Adversarial Vulnerability of Vision-Language Models through the Lens of Intermediate Spectral Subspaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07375)
*Chethan Krishnamurthy Ramanaik,Tobias Callies,Michael Hecht,Eirini Ntoutsi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper investigates adversarial vulnerability in vision-language models (VLMs) through the lens of spectral structure in intermediate linear transformations, proposing a white-box attack (SSGRA) that aligns representations with bottom right singular vectors, demonstrating improved attack effectiveness and offering spectral interpretability for robustness enhancement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While adversarial vulnerability in DNNs has been studied via decision-boundary geometry, feature robustness, Jacobians, and inverse problem instability, the spectral structure of intermediate linear transformations—which propagate information through networks—remains unexplored. This gap is particularly relevant for transformer-based VLMs, whose linear layers admit interpretable spectral decompositions and whose widespread deployment makes robustness understanding critical.

Method: The authors propose SSGRA (Spectral-Subspace-Guided Attack), a white-box attack that operates by aligning intermediate representations with the subspace spanned by the bottom right singular vectors of linear transformation matrices in VLMs. This leverages spectral decomposition to guide adversarial perturbation generation.

Result: SSGRA demonstrates improved attack effectiveness over existing baseline methods, empirically validating the spectral vulnerability hypothesis in vision-language models.

Conclusion: The spectral structure of intermediate linear layers constitutes a meaningful and previously unexplored mechanism of adversarial vulnerability. SSGRA not only serves as an effective attack but also provides a spectral interpretation framework that can inform future efforts to improve VLM robustness.

Abstract: Adversarial vulnerability in deep neural networks (DNNs) has been studied from the perspectives of decision-boundary geometry, feature robustness, input-output Jacobians, and the instability of inverse problems. Here, we focus on the spectral structure of intermediate linear transformations that propagate information through modern DNNs, an unexplored mechanism of adversarial vulnerability. Specifically, we investigate transformer-based vision-language models, whose linear layers admit interpretable spectral decompositions and whose widespread adoption makes understanding their robustness increasingly important. We propose a white-box spectral-subspace-guided attack (SSGRA) that aligns intermediate representations with the subspace spanned by the bottom right singular vectors. Our experiments show improved attack effectiveness over existing baselines. In addition, SSGRA offers a spectral interpretation of adversarial vulnerability in VLMs, providing insights for improving their robustness.

</details>


### [52] [RLVP: Penalize the Path, Reward the Outcome](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07435)
*Bojie Li,Noah Shi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RL agents deployed in real-world settings must learn online from costly interactions while respecting path constraints (e.g., not harassing users). Outcome-only RL fails because it ignores constraint violations and wastes expensive rollouts. The authors propose "penalize the path, reward the outcome" — using cheap, verifiable penalties on bad actions to supply within-group variance, yielding high task success with near-zero constraint violations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world agentic deployments (e.g., placing phone calls) face two key challenges that existing RLVR approaches fail to address: (1) deployability depends on respecting outcome-neutral path constraints (business hours, authentication, not spamming users), which outcome-based rewards cannot encode; (2) interactions are expensive, requiring sample-efficient learning. Outcome-only RL wastes costly rollouts on all-fail groups where group-relative advantage collapses to zero, and progress-based densification targets the hard-to-verify direction.

Method: The authors analyze the structure of group-relative advantage, showing it is equivalent to within-group variance. They argue that a dense signal helps only when it supplies variance the outcome lacks. A verifiable penalty on the path reliably meets this condition, while progress potentials help only where partial progress is reachable. They propose the recipe "penalize the path, reward the outcome" and provide four design rules for effective penalties, including avoiding the inaction trap (where isolated penalties cause the agent to do nothing).

Result: The proposed approach — combining verifiable path penalties with outcome rewards — achieves high task success rates with near-zero constraint violations. In contrast, outcome-only training violates constraints on nearly every episode.

Conclusion: Verifiable penalties on the path are both practical (cheap to detect) and theoretically justified (they supply variance where outcomes cannot). The "penalize the path, reward the outcome" recipe enables safe, sample-efficient online RL for real-world agentic deployments, dramatically reducing constraint violations compared to outcome-only methods.

Abstract: Agents acting on our behalf in the real world (e.g. placing phone calls) must learn online from costly, often irreversible interactions rather than cheap simulator steps. Two things follow. First, deployability depends on the path, not only the outcome. An agent must respect outcome-neutral constraints such as not repeatedly calling an unresponsive user, respecting business hours, or completing required authentication constraints that outcome-based rewards cannot express, since violating them frequently improves apparent success. Second, because each interaction is expensive, the agent must learn efficiently from very few examples.
  Reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards (RLVR) is blind to both challenges: it optimizes solely on the outcome and wastes expensive rollouts on all-fail groups where group-relative advantage collapses to zero. Attempts to densify supervision by rewarding progress target the hard-to-verify direction. In contrast, real agentic environments can cheaply detect bad moves. Since group-relative advantage is equivalent to within-group variance, a dense signal helps only when it supplies variance the outcome lacks. A verifiable penalty on the path meets this condition reliably, while a progress potential helps only where partial progress is reachable. The resulting recipe "penalize the path, reward the outcome" achieves high task success with near-zero violations, where outcome-only training violates constraints on nearly every episode. We provide four design rules for effective penalties, including avoidance of the inaction trap that arises when a penalty is used in isolation.

</details>


### [53] [Generalist Vision-Language Models for Fast Radio Burst detection: a zero-shot benchmark against a specialized detector](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07382)
*Raiff H. Santos,Amilcar R. Queiroz,Tharcisyo S. S. Duarte,K. E. L. de Farias,Rafael A. Batista*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Small open-weight Vision-Language Models (VLMs) can detect FRBs in dynamic spectra under zero-shot prompting with no fine-tuning, achieving accuracy comparable to specialized detectors (93.65% vs 92.90%) while exhibiting lower false-positive rates on structured RFI, and can be repurposed for multi-class classification simply by rewriting the prompt.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current specialized deep learning models for FRB detection require large task-specific training datasets and cannot be reconfigured without retraining. The authors explore whether generalist VLMs can overcome these limitations through zero-shot prompting alone.

Method: From 3000 simulated L-band dynamic spectra (containing FRBs, structured RFI, and noise), a balanced binary benchmark of 2000 samples was constructed. Two small VLMs (Gemma 4 2B and 4B) were evaluated sample-by-sample against the state-of-the-art specialized detector SwinYNet under a zero-shot, prompt-only regime, with models returning structured decisions with natural-language justifications. Prompt rewriting alone enabled three-class classification on the full 3000 spectra.

Result: Gemma 4 2B achieved 93.65% accuracy (no statistically significant difference from SwinYNet's 92.90%), with significantly lower false-positive rates on structured RFI (6.4% vs 25.0%) and zero false positives on pure noise. SwinYNet retained superior probabilistic ranking (ROC-AUC 1.0000 vs 0.9482). For three-class classification, VLMs reached up to 86% accuracy without a single false FRB.

Conclusion: Small, locally-run generalist VLMs represent a viable zero-shot alternative to specialized FRB detectors, offering flexibility through prompt reconfiguration, lower false-positive rates, and competitive accuracy, though they do not yet match the perfect ranking performance of specialized models.

Abstract: Fast Radio Bursts (FRBs) are millisecond-duration radio transients whose automated detection increasingly relies on highly specialized deep learning models. These detectors achieve exceptional performance, but they require large task-specific training datasets and cannot be redefined without retraining. In this work, we evaluate whether small, open-weight, locally run generalist Vision-Language Models (VLMs) can detect FRBs in dynamic spectra under a zero-shot, prompt-only regime, with no fine-tuning and no labeled examples, returning structured decisions with a natural-language justification. From a controlled set of 3000 simulated L-band dynamic spectra containing FRBs, structured Radio Frequency Interference (RFI), and noise, we draw a balanced binary benchmark of 2000 samples and compare two such VLMs (Gemma 4 2B and 4B), sample by sample, against the state-of-the-art specialized detector SwinYNet. At the default threshold, Gemma 4 2B reaches an accuracy of 93.65%, with no statistically significant difference from SwinYNet (92.90%), while showing a significantly lower false-positive rate on structured RFI (6.4% vs. 25.0%) and no false positives on pure noise. SwinYNet retains a perfect probabilistic ranking on this benchmark (ROC-AUC of 1.0000 vs. 0.9482), a ceiling that the zero-shot VLM approaches from general-purpose pretraining alone. Rewriting the prompt alone reconfigures the same models for three-class FRB/RFI/noise classification on the full set of 3000 spectra, where they reach up to 86% accuracy without a single false FRB.

</details>


### [54] [Reward-Adaptive Iterative Discovery: A Case Study on Automated Game Testing for NHL26](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07498)
*Florian Fuchs,Jessy Gosselin-Grant,Boris Skuin,Michele Petteni,Alessandro Sestini,Joakim Bergdahl,Amir Baghi,Linus Gisslén*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper presents RAID, a reinforcement learning approach that automatically discovers diverse behavioral exploits in a hockey video game's goalie AI. By extending standard RL to avoid overfitting to single solutions, the method found six exploit strategies comparable to those identified by human playtesters in hours-long sessions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Manual playtesting for AI behavioral exploits is costly and time-consuming, requiring repeated re-testing after every game update. The goal is to automate exploit discovery while addressing RL's tendency to overfit to a single solution, thereby uncovering multiple, diverse exploits in parallel.

Method: RAID (Reward-Adaptive Iterative Discovery) uses an iterative RL framework that trains a population of goal-scoring agents. A key contribution is a simple extension atop existing RL algorithms that promotes diverse, high-quality solutions rather than converging to a single exploit. The approach is deployed on a development version of EA SPORTS NHL 26, specifically targeting the goalie AI.

Result: In a single experiment, RAID discovered six distinct hockey scoring exploit strategies. These were qualitatively similar to exploits identified by human playtesters during hours-long manual testing sessions.

Conclusion: RAID demonstrates that automated RL-based exploit discovery can match human-level findings while significantly reducing the manual effort and cost associated with re-testing game AI after development changes.

Abstract: Testing is a major effort for the gaming industry, requiring a significant part of development budget and people power. We present a case study on a development version of the ice hockey game EA SPORTS NHL 26, for which human playtesters test the goalie AI for behavioral exploits. To reduce the effort of re-testing the goalie AI after every game or behavior modification in the development phase, we propose Reward-Adaptive Iterative Discovery (RAID), a novel approach to automatically find exploits using an iterative Reinforcement Learning (RL) approach that trains a population of goal scoring agents. While previous approaches can already successfully find exploits, RL algorithms tend to overfit to a single solution. We introduce a simple extension on top of existing RL algorithms, such that they find multiple diverse high-quality solutions. For our first deployment of this approach, within a single experiment we were able to find six hockey scoring exploit strategies that were qualitatively similar to those that playtesters had found in hours-long manual testing sessions.

</details>


### [55] [Sparse Delta Memory: Scaling the State of Linear RNNs through Sparsity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07386)
*Loïc Cabannes,Pierre-Emmanuel Mazaré,Gergely Szilvasy,Matthijs Douze,Maria Lomeli,Ilze Amanda Auzina,Justin Carpentier,Gabriel Synnaeve,Hervé Jégou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Sparse Delta Memory (SDM) scales the hidden state of gated linear RNNs using sparse addressing, achieving higher memory capacity under isoFLOP constraints, which improves in-context learning, long-context retrieval, and—when using learned initial states—general knowledge and reasoning tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Linear attention models have fixed state sizes and constant per-token compute, but their limited state capacity causes them to underperform on long-context recall compared to softmax-attention transformers. Simply increasing state size improves recall but raises FLOPs. The goal is to achieve high memory capacity without proportionally increasing compute.

Method: The authors propose Sparse Delta Memory (SDM), which extends the Gated DeltaNet architecture by replacing dense key-value outer products with sparse reads and writes to a large explicit memory. This uses a sparse addressing scheme to scale the hidden state capacity by orders of magnitude while maintaining computational efficiency. Additionally, they explore learning the initial memory state to serve as a parametric memory.

Result: Under isoFLOP constraints and with equal parameter counts, higher state memory capacity via SDM significantly improves performance on in-context learning and long-context retrieval benchmarks. Learning the initial memory state further boosts performance across a wide range of common-knowledge and reasoning tasks.

Conclusion: SDM effectively decouples memory capacity from computational cost in gated linear RNNs, enabling substantial gains in recall-intensive and knowledge-intensive tasks without sacrificing efficiency.

Abstract: Linear attention models allow a fixed state size and a fixed amount of compute per token. However, due to their limited state size, linear attention models fall behind in long-context recall compared to softmax-attention-based transformer architectures. Increasing the state size of linear attention improves recall performance but at the cost of higher FLOPs. In this work, we introduce Sparse Delta Memory (SDM), an architecture that scales the hidden state of gated linear RNNs to orders of magnitude higher capacity using a sparse addressing scheme. SDM extends the Gated DeltaNet architecture by replacing the dense key-value outer product with sparse reads and writes to a large explicit memory. We show that, under an isoFLOP constraint and with an identical number of parameters, a higher state memory capacity significantly improves performance on in-context learning and long-context retrieval tasks. Moreover, by learning the initial state of the SDM memory and therefore using it as a parametric memory, we show that the model further improves on a wide range of common-knowledge and reasoning tasks.

</details>


### [56] [TimEE: End-to-end Time Series Classification via In-Context Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07500)
*Jaris Küken,Shi Bin Hoo,Martin Mráz,Frank Hutter,Lennart Purucker*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TimEE is a 4.5M-parameter foundation model for time series classification that uses in-context learning with purely synthetic pretraining. It directly outputs class predictions from a labeled support set and query in one forward pass, requiring no per-dataset training, and achieves state-of-the-art ROC AUC on the UCR benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The standard two-stage TSC paradigm (feature encoding + classifier) has three key limitations: (1) representation learning is decoupled from the classification objective, (2) per-dataset training is required, and (3) label information cannot be exploited during inference. The authors aim to overcome these via end-to-end in-context learning with synthetic priors.

Method: TimEE follows the Prior-Data Fitted Network (PFN) framework. It is meta-trained exclusively on synthetic TSC tasks, where each task comprises time series with distinct class identities generated via structured distributional shifts. At inference, given a labeled support set and a query time series, TimEE produces a class distribution in a single forward pass — no fine-tuning or per-dataset training needed.

Result: Despite never seeing real time series during pretraining, TimEE ranks first in ROC AUC and third in accuracy on the UCR benchmark among all compared methods, including both foundation models and supervised deep learning baselines. It is the first purely synthetic-pretrained model to achieve SOTA on UCR.

Conclusion: End-to-end in-context learning with synthetic priors is a promising, underexplored direction for TSC. Future work includes scaling, improved prior design, and richer generation mechanisms.

Abstract: Time series classification (TSC) is dominated by a two-stage paradigm: train a feature encoder -- either from scratch on the target dataset or via pretraining on large corpora -- and then fit a task-specific classifier on top. While effective, this decoupling optimizes representation learning independently of the classification objective, requires per-dataset training, and prevents the model from exploiting label information during inference. We introduce TimEE, a 4.5M-parameter foundation model for end-to-end TSC via in-context learning. Given a labeled support set and a query time series, TimEE directly outputs a predicted class distribution in a single forward pass with no per-dataset training required. Following the prior-data fitted network (PFN) framework, TimEE is meta-trained exclusively on synthetic TSC tasks, where each task contains time series with distinct class identities arising from structured distributional shifts in the generative process. Despite seeing no real time series during pre-training, TimEE ranks first in ROC AUC (and third on accuracy) on the UCR benchmark among all compared methods, which include both foundation models and supervised deep learning baselines. To our knowledge, TimEE is the first purely synthetic-pretrained model to reach state-of-the-art performance on the UCR benchmark. These results establish end-to-end ICL with synthetic priors as a compelling, largely unexplored direction for TSC, with scaling, prior design, and richer generation mechanisms as natural avenues for improvement. Code is publicly available at http://github.com/automl/timee.

</details>


### [57] [The Optimal Sample Complexity of Learning Autoregressive Chain-of-Thought](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07423)
*Zhiyuan Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proves that the sample complexity of exact-trace PAC learning for autoregressive Chain-of-Thought traces is bounded by the standard multiclass rate governed by the Daniely-Shalev-Shwartz (DS) dimension, with no dependence on rollout length. It introduces a new combinatorial parameter called parity dimension, which is rollout-stable (unlike DS dimension), and uses it to control one-inclusion density via low-coordinate spanning arguments on finite restrictions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Exact-trace learning penalizes the entire autoregressive trace if any single action is wrong, raising the concern that sample complexity could scale with trace length. The authors aim to show that this is not the case: sample complexity depends only on the complexity of the local next-token class, not the rollout horizon.

Method: The authors work in the realizable PAC setting with exact-trace loss. They relate the problem to multiclass learning of the induced rollout class and introduce parity dimension — a refinement of DS dimension based on even pseudo-cubes that remains invariant under autoregressive rollout. They prove a low-coordinate spanning theorem on finite restrictions to bound one-inclusion density, establishing an upper bound of O((DSdim(H) + log(1/δ))/ε). A lower bound via one-step stopping shows worst-case optimality. They also exhibit an example where DS dimension strictly increases under rollout, justifying the need for parity dimension.

Result: For every stopping rule halt and every pointwise halt-halting local class H: n_{PAC}^{ε,δ}(Roll_halt(H)) = O((DSdim(H) + log(1/δ))/ε). This bound has no dependence on rollout length. The dependence on DSdim(H) is worst-case optimal. Additionally, DS dimension can increase under autoregressive rollout, while parity dimension does not.

Conclusion: Exact-trace autoregressive CoT learning is essentially no harder than standard next-token multiclass classification in the realizable PAC setting. The introduction of parity dimension provides a rollout-stable combinatorial measure that may be of independent interest for analyzing sequential prediction tasks.

Abstract: We prove that, in the realizable PAC setting, the sample complexity of exact-trace learning for full autoregressive Chain-of-Thought traces is upper bounded by the standard multiclass rate of the local next-token class, where this rate is governed by the Daniely--Shalev-Shwartz dimension. Under exact-trace loss, one wrong action makes the whole trace incorrect; nevertheless, for every stopping rule $\mathtt{halt}$ and every pointwise $\mathtt{halt}$-halting local class $\mathrm{H}$, $n_{\mathrm{PAC}}^{\varepsilon,δ}(\operatorname{Roll}_{\mathtt{halt}}(\mathrm{H}))=O((\operatorname{DSdim}(\mathrm{H})+\log(1/δ))/\varepsilon)$, with no dependence on rollout length. The dependence on $\operatorname{DSdim}(\mathrm{H})$ is worst-case optimal, since one-step stopping recovers ordinary multiclass learning of $\mathrm{H}$. The proof introduces parity dimension, a rollout-stable refinement of DS dimension based on even pseudo-cubes. It controls one-inclusion density via a low-coordinate spanning theorem on finite restrictions and, unlike DS dimension itself, does not increase under autoregressive rollout. We also show why this detour is necessary: DS dimension can increase under rollout.

</details>


### [58] [Single-Rollout Asynchronous Optimization for Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07508)
*Zhenyu Hou,Yujiang Li,Jie Tang,Yuxiao Dong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SAO introduces single-rollout asynchronous optimization for RL training of LLMs, replacing group-wise sampling with single-rollout sampling, using double-side token-level clipping, and value-model training to improve stability and performance on agentic tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing asynchronous RL systems for LLMs prioritize throughput but neglect training stability and task effectiveness. The widely-used GRPO framework's group-wise sampling does not naturally fit asynchronous agentic training, creating off-policy and stability challenges that need addressing.

Method: SAO replaces group-wise sampling with single-rollout sampling (one rollout per prompt) to reduce off-policy effects, incorporates value-model training designs, and introduces a strict double-side token-level clipping strategy to improve optimization stability.

Result: SAO trains stably for one thousand steps, consistently outperforms GRPO and its variants on SWE-Bench Verified, BeyondAIME, and IMOAnswerBench, and is particularly effective in simulated online learning settings with evolving environments. It was deployed to train the GLM-5.2 model (750B-A40B).

Conclusion: SAO effectively addresses stability and off-policy challenges in asynchronous RL for LLMs. Single-rollout sampling combined with double-side clipping and value-model training proves superior to group-wise approaches, especially for long-horizon agentic tasks.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) is becoming increasingly important for post-training large language models (LLMs). Previous RL pipelines for LLMs were mostly synchronous and batch-interleaved, which is inefficient for long-horizon agentic tasks. Recently, asynchronous RL has emerged as a more efficient alternative by updating the model as rollouts arrive. However, existing asynchronous RL systems often emphasize throughput, while leaving training stability and task effectiveness largely underexplored. For example, a key challenge is that group-wise sampling in the widely-used GRPO framework does not naturally fit asynchronous agentic training. In this paper, we present Single-rollout Asynchronous Optimization (SAO) to address the stability and off-policy challenges in asynchronous RL. To reduce off-policy effects and improve generalization, we replace group-wise sampling with single-rollout sampling, that is, using one rollout per prompt. We further improve this single-rollout strategy with practical value-model training designs. To improve optimization stability, we introduce a strict double-side token-level clipping strategy. SAO is able to train stably for one thousand steps and consistently outperform GRPO and its variants on agentic coding and reasoning benchmarks, such as SWE-Bench Verified, BeyondAIME, and IMOAnswerBench. We also demonstrate that single-rollout RL is particularly effective in a simulated online learning setting, where the model must adapt to changing evolving environments. To this end, SAO is successfully deployed in the agentic RL pipeline for training the open GLM-5.2 model (750B-A40B).

</details>


### [59] [Collaborative Synthetic Data Generation for Knowledge Transfer in Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07565)
*Maximilian Andreas Hoefler,Karsten Mueller,Wojciech Samek*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FedKT-CSD is a one-shot federated learning framework that uses publicly pretrained autoencoders and secure aggregation with differential privacy to achieve communication efficiency, robustness to data heterogeneity, and formal privacy guarantees, outperforming even non-private baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing one-shot federated learning methods using synthetic data aggregation reduce communication overhead but lack formal privacy guarantees. The goal is to jointly achieve low communication, robustness to client data heterogeneity, and rigorous differential privacy in a unified framework.

Method: FedKT-CSD leverages publicly pretrained autoencoders as a shared latent space. Each client encodes private data in a single forward pass, computes class-conditional latent statistics (means/covariances), and transmits these to the server. The server aggregates statistics via secure aggregation, adds calibrated differential privacy noise, and decodes a synthetic dataset for training a global model.

Result: FedKT-CSD is competitive with and often outperforms non-private baselines across diverse datasets and heterogeneity settings, while scaling to a large number of clients — all under formal (ε,δ)-DP guarantees.

Conclusion: The framework successfully closes the gap between communication efficiency, heterogeneity robustness, and rigorous privacy in one-shot FL by exploiting pretrained autoencoders and latent-space aggregation, enabling practical privacy-preserving federated learning at scale.

Abstract: One-shot federated learning (OSFL) addresses the communication overhead of federated learning by limiting training to a single round, but doing so without sacrificing model quality is non-trivial, particularly when client data distributions diverge. Recent work has addressed this challenge by aggregating client knowledge on the server through the construction of transferable synthetic datasets or distillates. However, most of these methods lack formal privacy guarantees, leaving a gap in jointly achieving low communication, robustness to heterogeneity, and rigorous privacy. We propose FedKT-CSD (Federated Knowledge Transfer via Collaborative Synthetic Data), a framework inspired by neural image compression that closes this gap by leveraging publicly pretrained autoencoders as a shared latent space. Each client encodes its private data in a single forward pass, computes class-conditional latent statistics, and transmits these to the server. The server aggregates these statistics via secure aggregation, adds calibrated differential privacy noise, and decodes a synthetic dataset for training a global model and further downstream tasks. This design provides formal $(\varepsilon,δ)$-differential privacy by construction, while keeping client-side computation and communication lightweight. Despite operating under privacy constraints, FedKT-CSD is competitive with and even outperforms non-private baselines across diverse datasets and heterogeneity settings, and scales to a large number of clients. Our code is available at: https://github.com/an7123/FedKT-CSD

</details>


### [60] [ALER-TI: Aligned Latent Embedding Retrieval for Time Series Imputation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07640)
*Xuan-Thong Truong,Trung-Kien Le,Tung Kieu,Thi-Thu Nguyen,Nhat-Hai Nguyen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ALER-TI introduces a retrieval-augmented time series imputation framework that aligns latent embeddings between corrupted queries and historical patterns, overcoming the limitations of local-context-only methods in non-stationary scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing deep learning imputation methods rely heavily on localized temporal context, which fails in real-world time series with non-stationary dynamics, weak temporal correlations, and infrequent patterns that cannot be inferred from nearby observations.

Method: ALER-TI is a model-agnostic retrieval-augmented framework with a Latent Embedding Alignment (LEA) mechanism. LEA applies post-hoc masking in latent space to align historical candidate embeddings with the query's missingness pattern, enabling pre-computation and caching for efficient retrieval. A lightweight adaptation module allows integration with any imputation backbone.

Result: Experiments on six real-world datasets under varying missing rates show that ALER-TI consistently improves strong baseline imputation models and enhances robustness across diverse settings.

Conclusion: Retrieval-augmented imputation with latent embedding alignment effectively compensates for degraded local context, offering a practical and backbone-agnostic solution for robust time series imputation.

Abstract: Deep learning has significantly advanced time series imputation, yet most existing architectures primarily rely on localized temporal context within the corrupted input sequence. This reliance can be limiting in real-world scenarios, where time series often exhibit non-stationary dynamics, weak temporal correlations, and infrequent patterns that are difficult to reconstruct from nearby observations alone. In this paper, we propose ALER-TI, Aligned Latent Embedding Retrieval for Time Series Imputation, a retrieval-augmented framework that explicitly leverages historical patterns to supplement degraded local context for more reliable missing-value reconstruction. The core of ALER-TI is Latent Embedding Alignment (LEA), which mitigates the representation mismatch between corrupted queries and complete historical candidates. By applying post-hoc masking in the latent space, LEA aligns candidates with the query's missingness pattern while allowing historical embeddings to be pre-computed and cached for efficient retrieval. ALER-TI is model-agnostic and can be integrated with various imputation backbones through a lightweight adaptation module. Extensive experiments on six real-world datasets under different missing rates demonstrate that ALER-TI consistently improves strong baseline models and enhances robustness across diverse imputation settings.

</details>


### [61] [Fast Rates for Semi-Supervised Learning via Data-Augmentation Graph Regularization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07513)
*Adam M. Oberman*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper provides a theoretical explanation for the labeled-sample efficiency of self-supervised learning (SSL). By modeling data augmentation as a similarity graph, the authors prove a fast $O(1/n_L)$ transductive rate—superior to the supervised $O(1/\sqrt{n_L})$—using leave-one-out stability on the augmentation graph. The bound explicitly quantifies how augmentation quality (via graph-cut alignment error) determines label efficiency, and the analysis employs a streamlined loss that recovers ideal features without projector/negative-sample overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: SSL empirically achieves high accuracy with very few labels, but no prior theory explains *why* labeled-sample efficiency is so strong. Existing analyses rely on unrealistic assumptions (exact kernels, generalizing features) and only bound generalization gaps rather than explaining the full accuracy-vs-label-count curve.

Method: The authors cast data augmentation as constructing a similarity graph over unlabeled data. Downstream learning on this graph corresponds to graph-Laplacian-regularized learning. They adapt the leave-one-out stability framework of Johnson & Zhang (2007) to the augmentation graph, proving a transductive rate of $O(1/n_L)$. The bound takes the form $C/n_L + R_{\mathrm{DA}}(y)$, where $R_{\mathrm{DA}}(y)$ is the data-augmentation alignment error (graph-cut mass across label boundaries). A streamlined loss function is used that omits projectors, negative samples, and orthogonality constraints while still recovering the top-$K$ augmentation-kernel eigenspace in the infinite-data limit.

Result: A fast $O(1/n_L)$ transductive learning rate bound is proved, strictly better than the standard supervised $O(1/\sqrt{n_L})$ rate. The bound makes augmentation quality explicit: expected error ≤ $C/n_L + R_{\mathrm{DA}}(y)$. This explains the empirically observed accuracy-versus-label-count curve rather than merely bounding a generalization gap.

Conclusion: Good augmentations (those with low alignment error, i.e., few augmentation edges crossing label boundaries) enable strong performance from very few labels. The theoretical framework bridges the gap between practical SSL success and rigorous learning theory, validating that augmentation quality directly governs label efficiency.

Abstract: Self-supervised learning matches supervised accuracy from a fraction of the labels, but the labeled-sample efficiency behind this has lacked a theoretical explanation. We provide one. Data augmentation induces a similarity graph on the unlabeled data, so downstream learning on that graph is graph-Laplacian-regularized learning. We prove a fast transductive rate, $O(1/n_L)$ in the number of labels, in place of the supervised $O(1/\sqrt{n_L})$, by carrying the leave-one-out stability apparatus of Johnson and Zhang (JMLR 2007) over to the augmentation graph, and without the unrealistic assumptions of limit-based analyses (exact kernel, generalizing features). The bound makes augmentation quality explicit: the expected error is at most $C/n_L + R_{\mathrm{DA}}(y)$, where the data-augmentation alignment error $R_{\mathrm{DA}}(y)$ is the graph-cut mass of augmentations that cross a label boundary, so good augmentations let few labels suffice. The analysis uses a streamlined loss that drops the projector, negative-sample, and orthogonality overhead of standard objectives yet still recovers the top-$K$ ideal features in the infinite-data limit, the augmentation-kernel eigenspace studied by Zhai et al. The result explains the observed accuracy-versus-label-count curve rather than only bounding a generalization gap.

</details>


### [62] [Selective Timestep Weighting and Advantage-Based Replay for Sample-Efficient Diffusion RLHF](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07693)
*Eric Zhu,Abhinav Shrivastava,Soumik Mukhopadhyay*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes two strategies to improve feedback efficiency in diffusion RLHF: (1) per-timestep weighting that reweights denoising steps based on their informativeness, theoretically grounded in PPO convergence, and (2) a replay mechanism that prioritizes informative trajectories for reuse. Together they achieve up to 6× sample efficiency improvement over baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Applying RLHF to diffusion models is highly feedback-inefficient, requiring large amounts of human or reward model evaluations. This makes diffusion RLHF impractical in real-world settings where feedback is the primary bottleneck. The authors aim to reduce the feedback burden while preserving generalization to unseen prompts.

Method: The authors make the key observation that reward information is unevenly distributed across denoising timesteps and trajectories. Based on this, they propose: (1) a per-timestep weighting scheme that reweights denoising steps during policy optimization, with theoretical connections to PPO convergence properties; and (2) a replay mechanism that prioritizes informative trajectories, reusing past samples instead of querying new rewards.

Result: Under identical hyperparameter settings, the proposed approach achieves up to a 6× improvement in sample efficiency compared to widely used diffusion RLHF baselines.

Conclusion: Emphasizing informative timesteps and trajectories during optimization yields more effective gradient updates. The two complementary strategies — per-timestep weighting and prioritized replay — substantially improve feedback efficiency, making diffusion RLHF more practical for real-world deployment where feedback is costly.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF) has emerged as a powerful paradigm for aligning generative models with human preferences. However, applying RLHF to diffusion models remains highly feedback inefficient, as existing approaches typically require large amounts of human or reward model evaluations. This limitation reduces the practicality of diffusion RLHF in realworld settings where feedback is the primary bottleneck. In this paper, we propose two complementary strategies that substantially improve the feedback efficiency of diffusion RLHF while preserving generalization to unseen prompts. Our key observation is that reward information in diffusion trajectories is unevenly distributed: not all denoising timesteps or trajectories contribute equally to learning from a reward signal. By emphasizing informative timesteps and trajectories during optimization, we obtain more effective gradient updates. First, we introduce a per-timestep weighting scheme that reweights denoising steps during policy optimization. We theoretically connect this weighting to the optimal convergence properties of proximal policy optimization (PPO) and approximate the resulting weighting trend empirically. Second, we introduce a replay mechanism that prioritizes informative trajectories, enabling the model to reuse past samples instead of repeatedly querying new rewards. Together, these strategies significantly improve the feedback efficiency of diffusion RLHF. Under identical hyperparameter settings, our approach achieves up to a 6$\times$ improvement in sample efficiency compared to widely used diffusion RLHF baselines.

</details>


### [63] [Gradient-free Riemannian Langevin Sampler](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07519)
*Ricardo Baptista,Olivier Zahm*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GRiLS introduces a gradient-free MCMC sampler that uses a Riemannian metric to reshape local geometry for better exploration of multimodal distributions, outperforming existing methods without requiring target density derivatives.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard MCMC methods struggle with multimodal distributions due to poor mixing and mode trapping. While gradient-based methods like Langevin dynamics help, gradients are often unavailable or computationally prohibitive for complex targets. There is a need for an efficient gradient-free sampler that can navigate multimodal landscapes effectively.

Method: The authors propose GRiLS, which introduces a Riemannian metric to reshape local geometry and facilitate cross-mode transitions. The proposal requires only the mean and covariance of the target density (no gradients), estimated via an ensemble of interacting particles. This yields a gradient-free MCMC algorithm suited for expensive, derivative-free settings.

Result: Empirical evaluations on multimodal benchmarks show that GRiLS achieves improved mixing performance compared to both gradient-based and gradient-free MCMC baselines.

Conclusion: GRiLS provides an effective gradient-free alternative for sampling multimodal distributions, combining Riemannian geometry with particle-based covariance estimation to overcome mode trapping without requiring gradient evaluations.

Abstract: We address the problem of efficiently sampling multimodal probability distributions, where standard Markov Chain Monte Carlo methods often suffer from poor mixing and mode trapping. To mitigate these issues, we propose Gradient-free Riemannian Langevin Sampler (GRiLS), a novel proposal that improves exploration without requiring gradient evaluations of the target density. Our approach introduces a Riemannian metric which reshapes the local geometry in order to facilitate transitions across modes. The resulting gradient-free MCMC algorithm is particularly suitable for complex, computationally expensive targets where derivatives are unavailable or impractical. The GRiLS proposal requires knowing the mean and covariance of the target density, which we estimate using an ensemble of interacting particles. Empirical results on multimodal benchmarks demonstrate that GRiLS achieves improved mixing compared to existing gradient-based and gradient-free MCMC approaches.

</details>


### [64] [Avoiding unsafe sets when training with Langevin Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07538)
*Adam M. Oberman*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper analyzes the probability that noisy gradient descent (modeled as overdamped Langevin dynamics) enters a failure region during training on a strongly convex loss. Three bounds are derived: an exponentially small equilibrium probability, a shape-free relaxation bound with burn-in time ~d, and a sharper local-relaxation bound using the spectral measure of the failure region's indicator. A key insight is that strong convexity governs global relaxation speed, but the geometry of the unsafe set determines transient swelling behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: When training machine learning models with noisy gradient descent (SGD), the trajectory can be viewed as Langevin dynamics over a loss landscape. A natural safety question arises: what is the probability that the iterates enter a designated "failure region" (e.g., high-loss or unsafe parameter configurations)? The authors aim to bound this probability both at equilibrium and transiently during training, addressing whether the trajectory can temporarily "bulge" into dangerous regions even when the equilibrium probability is small.

Method: The analysis considers overdamped Langevin dynamics on a smooth, strongly convex loss in d dimensions, with a failure region A_H separated from the minimizer by an energy gap. Three analytical approaches are employed: (1) equilibrium analysis using Gibbs measure π, showing exponential smallness via large-deviations/energy-barrier arguments; (2) a shape-free chi-squared divergence bound relating transient probability ν_t(A_H) to equilibrium probability π(A_H) via the global spectral gap m; (3) a refined local relaxation rate defined through the spectral measure of the centered indicator function of A_H, combined with a maximum-principle ceiling, to tighten bounds for geometrically isolated regions. An Ornstein-Uhlenbeck example demonstrates the necessity of the burn-in time.

Result: Three main bounds: (1) At equilibrium, π(A_H) is exponentially small in dimension d, with an energy-barrier rate when noise is small. (2) The shape-free bound ν_t(A_H) ≤ π(A_H)(1 + √(χ₀²/π(A_H)) e^{-mt}) shows relaxation to roughly twice the equilibrium value after burn-in ~d. (3) For geometrically isolated failure regions, the local relaxation rate exceeds the global spectral gap m, proportionally shrinking the burn-in time, and a maximum-principle argument caps the trajectory probability uniformly in time. The OU example confirms that angular slices of the equilibrium shell can transiently swell by an exponential-in-d factor despite negligible equilibrium mass.

Conclusion: Strong convexity of the loss determines how fast training relaxes globally, but the shape and geometry of the unsafe set govern whether the trajectory transiently bulges through it. The local spectral approach provides tighter, geometry-aware bounds than the global shape-free bound, and the maximum-principle ceiling ensures uniform-in-time control. This framework offers a rigorous safety analysis for noisy gradient-based training procedures.

Abstract: Training a model with noisy gradient descent can be idealized as overdamped Langevin dynamics on the loss landscape, and a natural safety question is to bound the probability $ν_t(\mathcal{A}_H) = \mathbb{P}(Q_t \in \mathcal{A}_H)$ that the trajectory lies in a designated failure region $\mathcal{A}_H$. We study this for a smooth, strongly convex loss in $d$ dimensions and a failure region separated from the minimizer by an energy gap. Three bounds emerge. At the end of training, the equilibrium mass $π(\mathcal{A}_H)$ is exponentially small in $d$, with a complementary energy-barrier rate when the noise is small. Along the trajectory, a shape-free bound $ν_t(\mathcal{A}_H) \le π(\mathcal{A}_H)(1 + \sqrt{χ_0^2/π(\mathcal{A}_H)}\,e^{-mt})$ shows that the in-set probability relaxes to (twice) the static value after a burn-in time of order $d$, using only the global spectral gap $m$ of the loss. A worked Ornstein-Uhlenbeck example shows this burn-in is necessary: an angular slice of the equilibrium shell can transiently swell by a factor exponential in $d$, even though its equilibrium mass is tiny. To rule such swelling out we introduce a local relaxation rate attached to the failure region, defined through the spectral measure of its centered indicator rather than a Dirichlet-form Rayleigh quotient. For geometrically isolated regions this rate exceeds the global one, shrinking the burn-in proportionally, and combined with a maximum-principle ceiling it caps the trajectory probability uniformly in time. The picture is that strong convexity sets how fast training relaxes, but the shape of the unsafe set decides whether the trajectory bulges through it on the way home.

</details>


### [65] [Asymmetric Focal Loss Improves Graph Neural Network Prediction of Drug-Drug Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07611)
*Faranak Hatami,Mousa Moradi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ClinicalFocal loss, an asymmetric focal objective, substantially improved multi-relational drug-drug interaction prediction over standard binary cross-entropy, boosting accuracy by ~19 percentage points and reducing false negatives from 29.8% to 9.1%, without any architectural changes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard binary cross-entropy training for GNN-based polypharmacy side effect prediction allocates equal capacity to well-classified and difficult examples, potentially missing clinically significant interactions. The authors hypothesize that an asymmetric focal loss can address this by emphasizing hard positive examples.

Method: ClinicalFocal loss was integrated into a relation-aware graph convolutional network using molecular fingerprints, physicochemical descriptors, and learned embeddings. Evaluation used the TWOSIDES dataset with five-fold cross-validation under identical experimental conditions (architecture, features, data partitions, hyperparameters, random seeds) for both ClinicalFocal loss and the binary cross-entropy baseline.

Result: ClinicalFocal loss improved accuracy from 0.699 to 0.892 (+19.3 pp), F1 from 0.700 to 0.894 (+19.4 pp), AUROC from 0.766 to 0.914, and AUCPR from 0.714 to 0.860. False-negative rate dropped from 29.8% to 9.1%; specificity rose from 69.6% to 87.5%. Overall classification error decreased from 30.1% to 10.8% (64.1% relative reduction), with consistent improvements across all five folds.

Conclusion: Asymmetric focal optimization significantly improves both classification and ranking performance for DDI prediction, achieving 90.9% recall without architectural modification. Loss-function design is thus a direct, tunable lever for enhancing graph-based DDI prediction.

Abstract: Background: Graph neural networks improve computational prediction of polypharmacy side effects, but standard binary cross-entropy training allocates equal capacity to well-classified and difficult examples, potentially missing clinically significant interactions. We evaluated whether an asymmetric focal objective could improve multi-relational drug-drug interaction (DDI) prediction by emphasizing difficult positive interactions. Methods: ClinicalFocal loss was integrated into a relation-aware graph convolutional network using molecular fingerprints, physicochemical descriptors, and learned embeddings. The model was evaluated on TWOSIDES using five-fold cross-validation with identical experimental conditions (architecture, features, data partitions, hyperparameters, and random seeds) for ClinicalFocal loss and binary cross-entropy baseline. Results: ClinicalFocal loss increased accuracy from 0.699 to 0.892 (+19.3 percentage points) and F1 score from 0.700 to 0.894 (+19.4 percentage points). AUROC increased from 0.766 to 0.914, and AUCPR increased from 0.714 to 0.860. The false-negative rate decreased from 29.8% to 9.1%, while specificity increased from 69.6% to 87.5%. Overall classification error decreased from 30.1% to 10.8%, corresponding to a 64.1% relative reduction. Improvements were consistent across all five folds. Conclusions: Asymmetric focal optimization improved classification and ranking performance while achieving 90.9% recall for observed interaction triples, without modifying the underlying architecture. Loss-function design is a direct, tunable lever for improving graph-based DDI prediction.

</details>


### [66] [Higher-Order Geometric Updates for Levenberg-Marquardt Method via Riemann Normal Coordinates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07623)
*Jianing Liu,Dong H. Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RNC-LM extends the Levenberg-Marquardt optimizer by replacing its straight parameter-space step with a Riemannian normal coordinate curve, providing higher-order corrections that eliminate parameter-effects curvature order by order. This yields improved convergence on curved-valley, rank-deficient, and PINN failure-mode problems, with a 34x speedup on a large-scale potential-energy-surface fitting task.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In nonlinear least-squares, the chosen parameterization introduces parameter-effects curvature that the standard Levenberg-Marquardt method handles poorly because its tangent-space step is applied as a straight update in parameter coordinates. Geodesic acceleration offers a second-order correction but is exact only in the infinitesimal-step limit. The authors aim to construct finite-step updates with exact reparameterization consistency at arbitrary order.

Method: The authors reformulate the geodesic equation to construct Riemannian normal coordinate (RNC) updates that extend geodesic acceleration to arbitrary-order corrections. The RNC-LM method builds finite-step updates by canceling residual tangential acceleration order by order in a moving tangent frame. A line search along the RNC curve controls the step distance while keeping cost close to standard LM.

Result: On classical NLS benchmarks, RNC-LM shows improved convergence and robustness in curved valleys and rank-deficient problems. On a reaction-diffusion PINN failure-mode benchmark, it reduces relative L2 error to ~1e-3 and recovers physically meaningful solutions. On a large-scale machine-learning potential-energy-surface fitting task, it achieves a 34-fold speedup over standard LM.

Conclusion: RNC-LM effectively overcomes the limitation of parameter-effects curvature in LM optimization by providing finite-step reparameterization-consistent updates, leading to substantial practical gains in convergence speed, robustness, and solution quality across diverse nonlinear least-squares applications including PINNs and large-scale ML fitting.

Abstract: Nonlinear least-squares optimization is central to regression, physics-informed neural networks, and other machine-learning tasks. Such problems have a natural geometric interpretation, model predictions form a manifold in data space, while the chosen parameterization can introduce parameter-effects curvature that becomes a dominant source of nonlinearity. This exposes a limitation of the Levenberg-Marquardt (LM) method, its tangent-space step is applied as a straight update in parameter coordinates. Geodesic acceleration gives a second-order correction, but its removal of parameter-effect curvature is exact only in the infinitesimal-step limit. We propose a Riemann-normal-coordinate Levenberg-Marquardt method (RNC-LM) to improve this consistency for finite optimization steps. By reformulating the geodesic equation, RNC-LM extends geodesic acceleration to arbitrary-order corrections and constructs finite-step updates with progressively higher reparameterization consistency. A line search along the resulting RNC curve controls the traveled distance while keeping the cost close to standard LM. The method eliminates the tangential component of residual acceleration order by order in a moving tangent frame, making the actual objective reduction more consistent with the linear model prediction of LM. On classical nonlinear least-squares benchmarks, RNC-LM improves convergence and robustness in curved valleys and rank-deficient problems. On a reaction-diffusion PINN failure-mode benchmark, it reduces the relative L2 error to the order of 1e-3 and recovers a physically meaningful solution. On a large-scale machine-learning potential-energy-surface fitting task, it achieves a 34-fold speedup over standard LM.

</details>


### [67] [An optimal control approach for neural network architecture adaptation with a posteriori error estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07637)
*C G Krishnanunni,Thomas Scott,Tan Bui-Thanh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces a method for adaptively growing neural network depth by inserting layers where approximation error is largest, using a posteriori error estimation derived from a continuous-time optimal control formulation of neural network training. The approach borrows dual weighted residual methodology from finite element analysis to compute error bounds, and demonstrates superior generalization on scientific datasets including Navier-Stokes inverse problems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neural network architecture design, particularly depth selection, is typically heuristic. The authors seek a principled, mathematically rigorous method for determining where to add layers based on how approximation error distributes across the network depth, enabling targeted architecture refinement rather than uniform expansion.
