Method: The authors formulate neural network training as a continuous-time optimal control problem, where weights and biases are treated as piecewise linear functions across layers. They derive a posteriori error estimates using dual weighted residual methodology from finite element analysis, yielding computable upper bounds on functional error. These bounds decompose total approximation error into interval-wise (layer-wise) contributions. New layers are then inserted at locations of maximum estimated error, producing an adaptive depth-refinement strategy.

Result: Numerical experiments on scientific datasets, including learning the observable-to-parameter map for Navier-Stokes equations, show that the proposed depth-adaptation method consistently outperforms existing architecture adaptation methods in generalization performance.

Conclusion: The error-estimation-driven depth adaptation provides a rigorous, principled alternative to heuristic architecture design. The framework bridges optimal control theory and finite element error analysis to enable efficient, targeted network growth, with strong empirical validation on challenging scientific machine learning tasks.

Abstract: This work presents a novel approach for adapting neural network architecture along the depth based on a posteriori error estimation. By formulating neural network training as a continuous-time optimal control problem, we derive rigorous error estimates that quantify how approximation error distributes across network layers. This error decomposition enables a principled depth adaptation strategy: new layers are inserted at locations of maximum estimated error, allowing the network to efficiently capture complex, nonlinear variations in the underlying problem. Our framework introduces a novel network architecture that treats weights and biases as piecewise linear functions varying across layers, with the error estimator bounding the discrepancy between this discrete representation and the true continuous optimal control solution. The approach leverages dual weighted residual methodology from finite element analysis to derive computable upper bounds on the functional error. A key theoretical contribution is the derivation of explicit error bounds that decompose the total approximation error into interval-wise contributions, providing a rigorous basis for targeted architecture refinement. We demonstrate the effectiveness of our method on scientific datasets, including learning the observable-to-parameter map for the Navier-Stokes equation. Numerical results reveal that our approach consistently outperforms existing architecture adaptation methods in terms of generalization performance.

</details>


### [68] [Guidance Breaks the Fitted Operator: A Terminal-Fitted Repair for Classifier-Free Guidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07665)
*Shiheng Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Classifier-free guidance (CFG) at large scales causes oversaturation and instability due to a numerical mismatch: DDIM is no longer fitted to the guided terminal layer. The authors prove this arises from an anomalous exponent 1+w in the discriminative subspace, derive a clock barrier with three step-size thresholds, and propose a zero-extra-NFE repair — replacing CFG's w(r−1) with r^(1+w)−r — that eliminates blow-up and yields first-order accuracy as σ_min→0.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CFG is the dominant approach for strengthening class-conditioning in diffusion/flow-matching samplers, but at high guidance scales it oversaturates, destabilizes, and forces practitioners to use more sampling steps or limited-interval schedules. The root cause of this behavior is not well understood, prompting a rigorous numerical-analysis investigation.

Method: The authors analyze CFG through an asymptotic-preserving, numerical-analysis lens. They build on the result that deterministic DDIM is the unique fitted operator for the unguided terminal layer (exact on the final small-σ stretch). They show guidance re-stiffens the discriminative subspace to exponent 1+w, making DDIM no longer fitted there. They prove a guided clock barrier with three ordered step-size thresholds, interpret one-step oversaturation as a solver artifact, and derive a one-coefficient correction: replace w(r−1) with r^(1+w)−r on the guidance direction. Validation includes a calibration model, learned CIFAR-10 checkpoints, and Stable Diffusion 1.5 DDIM as a cross-domain smoke test.

Result: On the calibration model, the repair removes CFG's σ_min-divergent blow-up and is first-order accurate against the exact guided flow. On CIFAR-10, it yields 9/9 point-FID wins over CFG on the tested grid, cutting residual amplification and saturation while preserving classifier-proxy target accuracy in hard-cell blocks. On Stable Diffusion 1.5 DDIM, it acts as a high-guidance stabilizer at no extra cost. However, it is not a universal image-quality win and is not a uniformly better integrator against a dense vanilla-CFG reference.

Conclusion: CFG's high-guidance pathologies are primarily solver artifacts, not properties of the continuous guided law. The proposed one-coefficient repair effectively eliminates these artifacts at zero extra NFE, though its benefits are specific to high-guidance stabilization rather than universal image-quality improvement.

Abstract: Classifier-free guidance (CFG) is the standard way to strengthen class-conditioning in diffusion and flow-matching samplers, yet at large guidance it oversaturates and destabilizes, symptoms practitioners suppress with more steps or limited-interval schedules. We analyze CFG through an asymptotic-preserving, numerical-analysis lens. Building on a recent result that the deterministic DDIM step is the unique fitted operator for the unguided terminal layer, exact on the final small-sigma stretch of sampling, we show that guidance re-stiffens exactly the discriminative subspace to an anomalous exponent 1+w. DDIM is therefore no longer fitted there, and on coarse meshes its guided residual diverges as sigma_min goes to zero. We prove a guided clock barrier with three ordered step-size thresholds, and read one-step oversaturation as its endpoint: a solver artifact on the calibration model rather than the continuous guided law. The same analysis yields a one-coefficient, zero-extra-NFE repair: replace CFG's w(r-1) by r^(1+w)-r on the guidance direction. On the calibration model's discriminative crossover, this removes CFG's sigma_min-divergent blow-up and is first-order accurate against the exact guided flow as sigma_min goes to zero. On learned CIFAR-10 checkpoints, and as a cross-domain smoke test on Stable Diffusion 1.5 DDIM, it acts as a high-guidance stabilizer at no extra cost rather than a universal quality knob: it cuts residual amplification and saturation, gives 9/9 point-FID wins over CFG on the tested grid, and preserves classifier-proxy target accuracy in the hard-cell blocks. We report the limits alongside: it is not a universal image-quality win, and against a dense vanilla-CFG reference it is not a uniformly better integrator of that field.

</details>


### [69] [PeTeR: Post-Training Robustification of Probabilistic Circuits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07671)
*Adrian Ciotinga,Yeming Dai,YooJung Choi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PeTeR is a data-free post-training framework that robustifies pre-trained probabilistic circuits against distribution shifts without retraining, achieving competitive performance against data-dependent robust learning methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Probabilistic circuits (PCs) suffer from overfitting and poor generalization under data noise, small samples, or distribution shifts. While distributionally-robust optimization (DRO) with Wasserstein balls can help, existing DRO methods require training from scratch. The authors aim to enable robustness in already-trained PCs without retraining.

Method: The authors propose PeTeR (Post-training Robustification), a data-free framework that takes a pre-trained PC and robustifies it against distribution shifts. It operates without access to training data, leveraging the internal structure of PCs to adjust parameters for worst-case distributional robustness within a Wasserstein ball.

Result: Empirical evaluations on multiple density estimation benchmarks show PeTeR effectively robustifies baseline models against both random and adversarial perturbations, matching or exceeding the performance of data-dependent robust learning baselines.

Conclusion: PeTeR provides a practical, data-free alternative for achieving distributional robustness in probabilistic circuits, eliminating the need for costly retraining while preserving or improving performance relative to existing data-dependent approaches.

Abstract: Probabilistic circuits (PCs) can model complex joint distributions while supporting exact and efficient computation of many inference queries. However, standard likelihood-based PC learning is vulnerable to overfitting and fragile generalization when confronted with data noise, small sample sizes, or distribution shifts. This can be mitigated using distributionally-robust optimization which consider worst-case distributions within a Wasserstein ball of the empirical distribution, but current methods are limited to training a model from scratch in this framework. Instead, we propose PeTeR: a novel, data-free post-training framework designed to robustify pre-trained PCs against distribution shifts without retraining from scratch. Empirical evaluations across multiple density estimation benchmarks demonstrate that PeTeR effectively robustifies baseline models against both random and adversarial perturbations, achieving competitive or superior performance to data-dependent robust learning baselines.

</details>


### [70] [How Data Shapes RoPE Frequency Usage: From Positional Scale Matching to Length Generalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07678)
*Xinyi Wu,Siyuan Liu,Ali Jadbabaie*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RoPE frequency usage in transformers is driven by a data-centered frequency-matching principle: optimal frequencies scale inversely with the dependency width of training data. This explains observed non-uniform frequency usage, mid-low frequency bands in language models, and why position-interpolation-based length generalization works — natural language exhibits approximate self-similarity across positional scales.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While Rotary Position Embeddings provide a fixed grid of positional frequencies, trained models use these frequencies highly non-uniformly. The authors aim to understand what determines this selective frequency usage and to provide a principled explanation grounded in training data structure rather than architectural or optimization artifacts.

Method: The authors formalize each RoPE frequency as a positional lens and introduce a field-resolution tradeoff. They derive that for a data-induced dependency profile of width W, the optimal frequency scales as 1/W (frequency-matching principle). They validate this through controlled experiments on synthetic data with known dependency structures and on text-based data, then connect frequency selection to position-interpolation-based length generalization, analyzing when frequency scaling succeeds or fails based on whether natural language exhibits approximate self-similarity across scales.

Result: The frequency-matching principle explains controlled observations on both synthetic and text-based data. Mid-low frequency bands observed in language models arise from the multi-scale dependency structure of natural language. Empirically, natural language exhibits approximate self-similarity across positional scales, which explains why test-time frequency scaling supports long-context generalization. However, scaling fails when relevant long-context dependencies are not approximate dilations of those seen during training.

Conclusion: RoPE frequency usage emerges from a data-driven mechanism: frequencies are selected to match training-data dependency widths. Long-context generalization depends on two forms of scale matching — between learned frequencies and training-time dependencies, and between frequency scaling and how dependencies extend to longer contexts.

Abstract: Rotary Position Embeddings (RoPE) provide transformers with a fixed grid of positional frequencies, yet trained models use these frequencies highly non-uniformly. We study what determines this frequency usage and propose a data-centered explanation: RoPE frequencies are selected to match the relative-distance structure of the training data. Viewing each frequency as a positional lens, we formalize a field-resolution tradeoff and show that, for a data-induced dependency profile of width $W$, the optimal frequency scales as $1/W$. This frequency-matching principle explains controlled observations on synthetic and text-based data, and suggests that the mid-low frequency bands observed in language models arise from the multi-scale dependency structure of natural language. We further connect frequency selection to position-interpolation-based length generalization: scaling frequencies down expands the effective field while reducing resolution. This helps when longer-context dependencies are approximate dilations of those seen during training, but can fail when relevant dependencies do not scale with context length. Empirically, we show that natural language exhibits approximate self-similarity across positional scales, explaining why test-time frequency scaling can support long-context generalization. Overall, our results identify a data-driven mechanism behind emergent RoPE frequency usage and show that long-context generalization depends on two forms of scale matching: between learned frequencies and training-time dependencies, and between frequency scaling and how those dependencies extend to longer contexts.

</details>


### [71] [Neural Operator-enabled Topology-informed Evolutionary Strategy for PDE-Constrained Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07682)
*Xiangming Huang,Guannan Zhang,Lu Lu,Raphaël Pestourie*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: NOTES integrates a DeepONet neural operator with CMA-ES in a compact latent space, reducing design dimensionality by ~10× while achieving >95% efficiency in nanophotonic inverse design and low-compliance structural designs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generative models for inverse design of PDE-governed systems lack robustness and transferability, while evolutionary strategies struggle with high-dimensional, non-convex design spaces. A method is needed that combines the strengths of both approaches.

Method: NOTES couples a DeepONet-based neural operator with CMA-ES to perform global optimization in a compact latent space. The DeepONet learns topology-aware priors, reducing the design space dimensionality (e.g., 256→25), while CMA-ES optimizes within this latent representation, decoupling topology learning from governing physics in the PDE solver.

Result: For nanophotonic beam-deflector design (Maxwell's equations), NOTES achieves >95% efficiency, outperforming standalone CMA-ES, topology optimization, and other baselines. For structural optimization, it discovers designs with compliance as low as 246.

Conclusion: By decoupling topology learning via DeepONet from physics in the PDE solver, NOTES provides a flexible, robust, and transferable framework for inverse design across different physical systems and unseen operating conditions.

Abstract: The inverse design of physical systems governed by partial differential equations is computationally demanding due to the high dimensionality and non-convexity of design spaces. Generative models for inverse design often lack robustness and transferability, whereas evolutionary strategies are robust but struggle in high-dimensional spaces. This paper introduces a Neural Operator-enabled Topology-informed Evolutionary Strategy (NOTES) that integrates dimensionality reduction, representation learning, and evolutionary optimization for efficient and transferable inverse design. NOTES couples a DeepONet-based neural operator with the Covariance Matrix Adaptation Evolution Strategy (CMA-ES) to perform global optimization in a compact latent space that encodes topology-aware priors while discovering high-performance designs for unseen operating conditions. Applied to nanophotonic beam-deflector inverse design governed by Maxwell's equations, NOTES reduces the design dimensionality from 256 to 25 and consistently achieves over 95 percent efficiency, outperforming CMA-ES, topology optimization, and other baselines. Applied to structural optimization, NOTES discovers designs that achieve compliance down to 246. By decoupling topology learning of a DeepONet from the governing physics in a PDE solver, NOTES provides a flexible and transferable framework for the inverse design of physical systems.

</details>


### [72] [ECGLight: Compute-Light Framework For Paper ECG Digitization and Myocardial Infarction Screening](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07683)
*Shreyasvi Natraj,Cyrus Achtari,Felice Gragnano,Andrea Milzi,Marco Valgimigli,Diego Paez-Granados*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A lightweight, on-device pipeline that converts smartphone photos of paper ECGs into calibrated 12-lead signals and detects myocardial infarction with SHAP-based interpretability, running in under 30 seconds on CPU-only resources with over 95% accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Remote clinics still rely on paper ECG printouts due to limited connectivity and computational resources, making them inaccessible to modern AI-based decision support. This leads to missed diagnoses like acute coronary occlusion and delayed reperfusion therapy. Existing solutions treat digitization and diagnosis separately using resource-heavy models, lacking a unified, compute-light, on-device framework.

Method: An end-to-end pipeline that: (1) takes a smartphone photo/scan of a paper ECG, (2) reconstructs a calibrated 12-lead signal, (3) screens for myocardial infarction pathologies, and (4) provides SHAP explanations for interpretability. Trained and evaluated on 21,799 ECGs from PTB-XL and externally validated on the hospital-acquired ECG-Matrix dataset, running entirely on CPU in under 30 seconds per ECG.

Result: Achieved 95.51% accuracy (F1=0.9519) for MI detection on PTB-XL and 88.89% accuracy (F1=0.8862) for OMI detection on ECG-Matrix, all in under 30 seconds per ECG on CPU-only resources.

Conclusion: Legacy paper ECG records can be reliably digitized and analyzed anywhere in the world using lightweight on-device AI, enabling scalable clinical decision support where digital ECG export, internet connectivity, or high-end computing are unavailable.

Abstract: Electrocardiography (ECG) is one of the most widely used tests for diagnosing cardiovascular disease. Yet several remote clinics still utilize paper ECG printouts for their analysis due to limited connectivity and computational capacity. As a result, vast numbers of physical ECGs obtained in remote areas still remain incapable of being accessed by contemporary artificial-intelligence (AI)-based decision support as they require high computational resources or strong high-speed internet connectivity. This causes several cases where conditions like acute coronary occlusion (ACS) is overlooked and reperfusion therapy delayed. Although prior work has tackled digitization and diagnosis separately, and utilized advanced AI models for them, there still remains a lack of a compute-light, on-device framework that reconstructs paper ECGs at high fidelity, while accurately supporting multiple clinically relevant endpoints. We address this need with an end-to-end lightweight on-device digitization-to-diagnosis pipeline that converts a smartphone photo or scan of a paper ECG into a calibrated 12-lead signal and screens for Myocardial Infarction (MI) pathologies, with SHapley Additive exPlanations (SHAP) to support interpretability. Trained and evaluated on 21,799 ECGs from the PTB-XL dataset and further validated on hospital-acquired ECG-Matrix dataset, the complete system runs in <30 s per ECG on CPU-only resources, achieving 95.51% accuracy (F1 = 0.9519) for MI detection on PTB-XL and 88.89% accuracy (F1 = 0.8862) for OMI detection on ECG-Matrix. This work showcases that legacy paper records can be reliably democratized in any part of the world, providing a scalable decision support when digital ECG export, connectivity, or high-end compute are unavailable

</details>


### [73] [The Key to Going Linear: Analysis-Driven Transformer Linearization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07706)
*Anna Kuzina,Paul N. Whatmough,Babak Ehteshami Bejnordi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper isolates the effect of state update design in frozen-backbone linearization of self-attention, showing that key-dependent rank-1 orthogonal projections (as in softmax) explain why delta-style networks outperform gated accumulation. Structural interventions—sink tokens, short convolutions, and fixed-budget cache routing—close the remaining quality gap, scaling effectively to 32B-parameter LLaMA and Qwen models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The quadratic cost of causal self-attention makes long-context transformer inference prohibitively expensive. While many post hoc linearization methods exist, it is unclear which design components actually preserve model quality when applied to pretrained models without retraining (frozen-backbone regime). The work aims to systematically understand what drives performance degradation in linearized attention and how to mitigate it.

Method: The authors adopt a strict frozen-backbone regime to isolate the effect of state update design. They analyze softmax attention through the lens of key-dependent, rank-1 orthogonal projections, demonstrating why delta-style state updates (additive updates to a recurrent state) outperform purely gated accumulation. They introduce three structural interventions to reduce approximation error: (1) sink tokens to stabilize attention distributions, (2) short convolutions to capture local patterns, and (3) fixed-budget cache routing for efficient memory management. The approach is scaled across LLaMA and Qwen models up to 32B parameters.

Result: The proposed linearization approach outperforms prior post hoc baselines on MMLU and matches the long-context retrieval performance of complex adaptive-caching frameworks. The method scales effectively to models with up to 32B parameters.

Conclusion: Softmax-based state updates rely on key-dependent, rank-1 orthogonal projections, which explains the superiority of delta-style networks over gated accumulation. The identified structural interventions successfully close the remaining quality gap, enabling effective post hoc linearization of large-scale transformers for long-context inference without retraining.

Abstract: The quadratic cost of causal self-attention severely bottlenecks long-context transformer inference. While numerous post hoc linearization pipelines exist, it is difficult to identify which components preserve model quality. This work isolates the effect of state update design in a strict frozen-backbone regime. We show that softmax relies on key-dependent, rank-1 orthogonal projections, elucidating why delta-style networks outperform purely gated accumulation. We identify a potential source of approximation errors and introduce structural interventions, specifically sink tokens, short convolutions, and fixed-budget cache routing, which reduces the remaining gap. We scale this linearization approach across LLaMA and Qwen models up to 32B parameters, outperforming prior post hoc baselines on MMLU and matching the long-context retrieval of complex adaptive-caching frameworks.

</details>

### [74] [Digital Fragmentation and Generative AI Use Across 103 Million Application Events](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06681)
*Sumer S. Vaid,Ashley V. Whillans*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: Analysis of 103M application events from 1,017 knowledge workers reveals that day-to-day variation (44.6%) drives digital fragmentation more than stable individual differences (35.8%) or organizational factors (19.6%). Fragmentation peaks toward week's end, resets after weekends/holidays, correlates with communication app usage, and — critically — narrows after generative AI use, suggesting AI may structure rather than worsen fragmented work.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Digital fragmentation — frequent switching between applications — consumes nearly 10% of the work year, yet it is unclear whether this phenomenon arises from stable individual traits, organizational context, or day-to-day fluctuations. The authors aim to decompose the sources of variance in fragmentation to guide effective interventions.

Method: The study analyzed second-by-second application event logs (103 million events) from 1,017 employees across eight knowledge-worker-intensive organizations (law, financial services, etc.). Variance partitioning was used to decompose fragmentation into between-organization, between-person (stable individual differences), and within-person (day-to-day) components. Temporal patterns across the work week and associations with communication application use and generative AI use were also examined.

Result: Day-to-day within-person variation explained the largest share of fragmentation variance (44.6%), followed by stable individual differences (35.8%) and between-organization differences (19.6%). Fragmentation increased over the work week and reset after weekends and holidays. Days with higher communication app usage were more fragmented. Generative AI use occurred on more fragmented days, but post-AI periods exhibited narrower, longer, and more predictable application use patterns.

Conclusion: The workday is the most impactful level for understanding and intervening on digital fragmentation. Rather than intensifying fragmentation, generative AI use appears to help structure and consolidate subsequent work, pointing to a potential ameliorative role for AI tools in knowledge work.

Abstract: Knowledge workers switch between applications thousands of times per day, spending nearly a tenth of the work year transitioning between digital applications in a process called digital fragmentation. Whether this fragmentation reflects who an employee is, where they work, or what kind of day they are having, has remained an open question. We analyzed 103 million application events recorded second-by-second from 1,017 employees across eight organizations that largely employ knowledge workers (e.g., law, financial services). Day-to-day variation in fragmentation within individual employees accounted for 44.6% of the variation in digital fragmentation, slightly exceeding stable individual differences between employees (35.8%), and far exceeding variation between organizations (19.6%). Fragmentation rose over the work week and reset after weekends and holidays. Higher-than-typical use of communication applications coincided with more fragmented work. Generative AI use also occurred on more fragmented days, but the period following AI use was marked by narrower, longer, and more predictable application use. These findings identify the workday as a key level for understanding and intervening on digital fragmentation and suggest that AI may help structure fragmented work rather than merely intensify it.

</details>


### [75] [Exploring the Interaction of Explanation Styles, Context, and Trust of AI Privacy Redaction in AI-mediated Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06687)
*Roshni Kaushik,Maarten Sap,Koichi Onoue*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: This paper investigates how explanations of AI redaction decisions in mediated communication affect user trust. A user study (n=180) found that providing explanations significantly improved perceived privacy preservation, that extensive redactions made explanations more valued, and that individual differences (age, AI familiarity) influenced trust. The findings underscore the need for adaptive, context-aware explanations in privacy-sensitive AI systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI-mediated communication systems face a dual challenge in privacy-sensitive domains: they must redact sensitive content while maintaining user trust. Prior work has not adequately explored how users perceive these redaction interventions or whether explanations of system behavior can mitigate trust erosion, creating a gap in understanding how to design trustworthy, privacy-aware AI mediators.

Method: The authors designed a scenario involving a validated AI system that removes sensitive content from messages and generates explanations of its redaction decisions at varying levels of detail. A between-subjects user study was conducted with 180 participants, manipulating two independent variables: the amount of redacted content and the level of explanation detail provided. User trust, preferences, and perceived effectiveness were measured and analyzed using statistical tests (p-values, Cohen's d, Cohen's f).

Result: (1) Providing explanations significantly increased perceived effectiveness at preserving privacy (p<0.05, Cohen's d ≈ 0.3). (2) When extensive redactions occurred, participants relied more on explanations and rated them as more helpful (p<0.05, Cohen's f ≈ 0.2). (3) Individual differences — particularly age and baseline AI familiarity — significantly influenced trust levels.

Conclusion: Explanations enhance user trust in AI-mediated communication systems that perform redaction, but their effectiveness is context-dependent. Extensive redactions increase reliance on explanations, and individual differences modulate trust responses. The authors conclude that adaptive, context-aware explanation mechanisms are essential for balancing transparency and privacy in the design of trustworthy AI communication mediators.

Abstract: AI-mediated communication is increasingly being utilized to help facilitate interactions; however, in privacy sensitive domains, an AI mediator has the additional challenge of considering how to preserve privacy. In these contexts, a mediator may redact or withhold information, raising questions about how users perceive these interventions and whether explanations of system behavior can improve trust. In this work, we investigate how explanations of redaction operations can affect user trust in AI-mediated communication. We devise a scenario where a validated system removes sensitive content from messages and generates explanations of varying detail to communicate its decisions to recipients. We then conduct a user study with 180 participants that studies how user trust and preferences vary for cases with different amounts of redacted content and different levels of explanation detail. Our results show that participants believed our system was more effective at preserving privacy when explanations were provided (p<0.05, Cohen's d ~ 0.3). We also found that contextual factors had an impact; participants relied more on explanations and found them more helpful when the system performed extensive redactions (p<0.05, Cohen's f ~ 0.2). We also found that explanation preferences depended on individual differences as well, and factors such as age and baseline familiarity with AI affected user trust in our system. These findings highlight the importance and challenge of balancing transparency and privacy in AI-mediated communications and suggest that adaptive, context-aware explanations are essential for designing privacy-aware, trustworthy AI systems.

</details>


### [76] [Flowcode: An AI-Powered Programming Environment for Scaffolding Iteration in Creative Computing Education](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06721)
*Tiffany Tseng,Liliana Hanem Seoror,Jeevika Adda,Meitalia Factor,Rona Darabi,Kiley R Matschke,Tiffany Fu,Annie Lin,Alekhya Maram,Arya Sinha*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: Flowcode is a creative coding environment combining flowchart-based code visualization with a learning-oriented chat interface, designed to help novices understand and extend existing code rather than just generating it through "vibe coding." Two studies with new creative coders informed its iterative design, revealing how visualization and friction can foster productive AI-use in computing education.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: New creative coders often learn by building upon shared example projects, but two core challenges hinder this process: (1) comprehending how existing code works, and (2) extending it with one's own ideas. Existing AI coding tools risk promoting "vibe coding" — generating code without understanding — rather than supporting genuine learning.

Method: The authors designed Flowcode, a creative coding environment that integrates two key components: a flowchart visualization for exposing code structure, and a chat interface specifically tailored for learning support rather than mere code generation. The design was iteratively refined across two user studies with novice creative coders.

Result: Through iterative design across two studies, the authors identified key design insights: visual representations of code structure help novices build mental models, and introducing intentional friction in AI interactions can redirect users from passive code generation toward active understanding and extension.

Conclusion: Visualization tools like flowcharts and thoughtfully designed friction in AI interfaces can shift creative coding learners from superficial "vibe coding" toward deeper code comprehension and creative extension. Flowcode demonstrates a promising direction for integrating AI into computing education productively.

Abstract: Building upon found examples is a popular way people learn to code, especially in creative coding communities where sharing projects and remixing are common practices. But effectively doing so requires being able to 1) understand how existing code works, and 2) extend it by writing code that implements your own ideas, practices that can be challenging for new creative coders. We explored how to support these two processes through the design of Flowcode, a creative coding programming environment that integrates a flowchart for visualizing code structure and a chat interface tailored to support learning to code over vibe coding. We share how we iterated on the design of Flowcode over two studies with new creative coders, reflecting on the roles visualization and friction may play in enabling productive AI-use in computing education.

</details>


### [77] [Creating a Mixed-Reality Installation with Families through Theatrical Co-Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06754)
*Pavlos Panagiotidis,Roma Patel,Boriana Koleva,Steve Benford,Jocelyn Spence,Paul Tennent,Juan Pablo Martinez Avila,Laurence Cliffe*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: This paper presents a mixed-reality installation co-designed with families using applied theatre methods. The approach addresses two key challenges in participatory design with families: uneven child-adult participation and the difficulty of sustaining workshop dynamics in final designs. Theatre techniques enabled playful role-shifting and the re-staging of co-design interactions within a public installation, contributing a theatrical framing of participatory design focused on roles, authority, and social conditions for collective imagination.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Participatory design for environmental sustainability with families faces two persistent challenges: (1) habitual family roles and power imbalances between adults and young children constrain participatory imagination, and (2) the relational dynamics and embodied practices cultivated in workshops rarely survive into final design artefacts. The authors seek to address both gaps.

Method: The authors conducted three co-design workshops with families using applied theatre methods (e.g., playful status shifts, narrative co-creation, embodied role-play). These workshops informed the development of a nationally toured mixed-reality installation. User testing was conducted to evaluate how workshop dynamics translated into the public installation context.

Result: Applied theatre methods successfully rebalanced child-adult participation through playful manipulation of status and authority. Selected workshop dynamics — including roles, rhythms, and interactional patterns — were effectively re-staged within the public mixed-reality installation, demonstrating that relational and performative elements can persist beyond the workshop setting into the final design.

Conclusion: The paper contributes a theatrical account of participatory design, arguing that designers should attend not only to artefacts and ideas but also to roles, rhythms, authority structures, and the social conditions that enable collective imagination — particularly when working with intergenerational groups.

Abstract: Co-designing with families for environmental sustainability relies on participatory imagination, yet habitual family roles and uneven participation, especially between adults and young children, often constrain it. A second challenge is continuity: workshop relationships and embodied ways of working do not easily survive into the final design, where artefacts travel more readily than roles or interactional dynamics. We report on a nationally toured mixed-reality installation developed through applied-theatre-led co-design with families. Across three workshops and user testing, applied theatre methods supported families to co-create narratives, artefacts, and interactional roles that shaped the public event. We show how theatrical co-design can rebalance child-adult participation through playful status shifts, and how selected workshop dynamics can be re-staged within a public mixed-reality installation. We contribute a theatrical account of participatory design in which designers work not only with artefacts and ideas, but with roles, rhythms, authority, and the social conditions that support collective imagination.

</details>


### [78] [Devising Interactive Spaces: A Rehearsal-Oriented Tool for Creating Responsive Environments for Immersive Theatre](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06761)
*Pavlos Panagiotidis,Paul Tennent,Jocelyn Spence,Nils Jaeger*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: A rehearsal-oriented system enabling performers to create responsive built environments during theatre devising workshops, using bespoke sensing modules and a visual no-code programming layer for gesture, position, and speech-triggered light and sound outputs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To make sensing and actuation available as compositional materials during early-stage creative experimentation for immersive theatrical compositions, rather than presenting a production-ready show-control platform. The goal is to allow performers to experiment with responsive spaces with minimal technical support.

Method: The system connects bespoke sensing modules (gesture, position, speech recognition) to light and sound outputs through a visual no-code programming layer. It was developed, used, and refined across six workshops with eight professional performance-makers. Participants created light-and-sound scores, gesture/position-triggered scenes, responsive architectures, participatory prototypes, and a multi-room scratch performance. The system emphasizes quick configuration, visible mappings, and in-room testing.

Result: The system was successfully used and iteratively refined across six workshops, enabling professional performance-makers to create various responsive theatrical elements including light-and-sound scores, triggered scenes, responsive architectures, participatory prototypes, and a multi-room scratch performance with minimal technical support.

Conclusion: Sensing and actuation can be effectively integrated as compositional materials in early-stage theatre devising when supported by a no-code, quickly configurable system with visible mappings and in-room testing capabilities, enabling performers to experiment with responsive spaces autonomously.

Abstract: We present a rehearsal-oriented system for creating responsive built environments during theatre devising workshops. The system connects bespoke sensing modules for gesture, position, and speech recognition to light and sound outputs through a visual no-code programming layer. It was developed, used, and refined across six workshops with eight professional performance-makers, where participants created light-and-sound scores, gesture- and position-triggered scenes, responsive architectures, participatory prototypes, and a multi-room scratch performance. Rather than presenting a production-ready show-control platform, this demo focuses on how sensing and actuation can be made available as compositional materials during early-stage creative experimentation for immersive theatrical compositions. The system is designed to support quick configuration, visible mappings, and in-room testing, allowing performers to experiment with responsive spaces with minimal technical support. We describe the system architecture, its workshop use, and the practical conditions that helped integrate interactive sensing into embodied performance-making.

</details>


### [79] [ShapeTalk: Combining Natural Language and Sketch for Time-Series Pattern Querying](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07073)
*Guoruizhe Sun,Yueqiao Chen,Emily Guo,Yutong Yao,Dongyu Liu*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: ShapeTalk is a coordinated natural-language and sketch-based query system for univariate time-series pattern search, where text and sketch serve as complementary modalities linked through shared visual context, editable features, and synchronized results, powered by an LLM-based semantic parsing pipeline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing visual query tools for time-series pattern search require precise sketches or rigid structured filters, failing to support vague, composite, or fuzzy pattern descriptions that users naturally want to express.

Method: ShapeTalk uses complementary text and sketch modalities: natural language handles semantic and compositional descriptions via an LLM-based semantic parsing pipeline that translates free-form queries into editable shape-feature constraints, while sketching enables direct geometric refinement. Both modalities share visual context, editable feature representations, and synchronized result views for iterative query formulation.

Result: Evaluated through two usage scenarios, a user study with failure-case analysis, and pipeline assessment, results show ShapeTalk enables effective time-series pattern search, with natural language as an accessible entry point and sketching for refinement and recovery when text is insufficient.

Conclusion: The complementary use of natural language and sketching, mediated by LLM-based semantic parsing, provides an effective approach for time-series pattern search that accommodates both high-level semantic descriptions and precise geometric specifications.

Abstract: Searching for time-series segments that match user-defined patterns is important in domains such as finance, climate science, and healthcare. However, existing visual query tools often struggle to support vague, composite, or fuzzy pattern descriptions, often requiring users to express their intent through precise sketches or rigid structured filters. We present ShapeTalk, a coordinated natural-language and sketch-based querying system for univariate time-series pattern search. Rather than treating text and sketch as a fused input stream, ShapeTalk uses them as complementary representations of analytic intent: natural language supports semantic and compositional pattern descriptions, while sketching supports direct geometric refinement. The two modalities are linked through a shared visual context, editable feature representations, and synchronized result views, enabling users to move between text and sketch during iterative query formulation. At its core is an LLM-based semantic parsing pipeline that translates free-form natural-language queries into interpretable and editable shape-feature constraints. We evaluate ShapeTalk through two usage scenarios, a user study with failure-case analysis, and an assessment of the LLM-based semantic parsing pipeline. The results show that ShapeTalk supports effective time-series pattern search, with natural language serving as an accessible entry point and sketching providing a complementary mechanism for refinement and recovery when textual specifications are insufficient.

</details>


### [80] [Creativity from Friction: Human-AI Interaction for Exploratory Structural Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07521)
*Ricardo Maia Avelino,Rita Sevastjanova,Tom Van Mele,Philippe Block,Mennatallah El-Assady*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: Generative AI systems that aim to remove friction and deliver final answers are misaligned with the needs of creative fields like structural design. This paper proposes design dimensions for constrained human-AI co-creation that leverage vision-language models to make structural exploration conversational, multimodal, and responsive to evolving intent, preserving reflective friction while reducing repetitive modelling friction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current generative AI tools are optimized to minimize friction and produce complete outputs, but this conflicts with how practitioners in creative disciplines—specifically structural design and architecture—actually work. These practitioners need interactive systems that support externalizing ideas, exploring alternatives, and refining partial solutions. The inherent constraints (spatial, mechanical, material, cost) create productive friction that stimulates novel, creative outcomes, and this friction should be preserved rather than eliminated.

Method: The authors articulate design dimensions for constrained human-AI co-creation systems built on vision-language models. These dimensions emphasize making structural exploration conversational, multimodal, and responsive to evolving human intent. They implement a pilot design interface embodying these principles and conduct a study with domain experts to evaluate how structural designers perceive interactive AI systems and whether such systems can balance the reduction of repetitive modelling friction against the preservation of reflective design friction.

Result: Through the pilot interface and expert study, the paper demonstrates that structural designers respond positively to interactive AI systems that support design space exploration. The results indicate that such systems can successfully reduce repetitive modelling friction—freeing designers from tedious, non-creative tasks—while preserving reflective design friction, which is essential for generating novel, constraint-compliant design solutions.

Conclusion: There is a fundamental misalignment between the objectives of current generative AI (removing friction, providing final answers) and the iterative, constraint-driven creative processes of structural designers. AI systems should be redesigned according to co-creation principles: they should be conversational, multimodal, and responsive to evolving intent, selectively automating only the unproductive friction while safeguarding the generative tension that constraints provide.

Abstract: AI agents that generate final answers based on user input often do not meet the needs of creative fields. Fields such as structural design and architecture need interactive systems that help users externalise and develop ideas, explore alternatives, and refine partial solutions. The final product of such designs needs to comply with many constraints concerning, e.g., spatial configuration, mechanical behaviour, material quantities, and costs. These constraints create friction in the design process, which can stimulate novel and creative solutions. In this paper, we discuss the misalignment between current generative AI goals to remove friction and provide final solutions and the needs of creators, such as structural designers, who develop ideas through iterative work. We present the design dimensions of systems allowing for constrained human-AI co-creation that rely on vision-language models making structural exploration conversational, multimodal, and responsive to evolving human intent in ways that follow and augment the discipline's creative process. Through a pilot design interface based on these principles and a study with experts in the field, this paper shows how structural designers perceive interactive AI systems and how such systems can support design space exploration by reducing repetitive modelling friction while preserving reflective design friction.

</details>


### [81] [Two-player Alternate Uses Test: A Controlled Testbed for Interactive Human-AI and Human-Human Co-Creation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07522)
*Babak Hemmatian,Anita Keshmirian,Yijun Lin,Shravan Ramamoorthy,Maryam Jahadakbar,Eli Khuri-Reid,Jingtong Wang,Sarah Hadjarab,Sindre Veum,Pranav Gupta,Deepak Somaya,Lav R. Varshney*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: A controlled, two-player Alternate Uses Test platform compares human-human and human-AI co-creation. Results show GPT-4 partners match human partners in originality; BAS Drive moderates partnership benefits, cognitive outsourcing predicts lower originality in human dyads, and exposure to creative ideas ("seeding") boosts later performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing AI ideation research suffers from a trade-off: controlled studies use independent agents while field studies of human-AI collaboration lack experimental control. There is no platform enabling direct, controlled comparison of human-human and human-AI co-creation under matched interactive conditions.

Method: A two-player extension of the Alternate Uses Test (AUT) was developed as a controlled interactive platform. It decomposes performance into participant traits, partner perceptions, and content dynamics, with calibrated non-interactive baselines. An in-person pilot study (N=62) compared human-human and human-AI (GPT-4) dyads under matched time limits.

Result: Originality with GPT-4 partners was statistically equivalent to human partners. BAS Drive moderated whether interactive partnership benefited originality. Self-reported cognitive outsourcing predicted lower originality specifically in human-human dyads. Prior exposure to highly creative ideas improved subsequent performance (seeding effect).

Conclusion: The platform effectively isolates factors in human-AI co-creation. GPT-4 can serve as a viable creative partner matching human performance. Individual differences like approach motivation and cognitive outsourcing shape collaborative outcomes. The "seeding" intervention suggests scalable ways to enhance creative ideation. The platform, code, and dataset are released as an open testbed.

Abstract: Controlled research on AI ideation typically compares independent agents, while field studies of human-AI collaboration sacrifice experimental control. We introduce a controlled, two-player extension of the Alternate Uses Test (AUT) that enables comparison of human-human and human-AI co-creation under matched interactive conditions, alongside calibrated non-interactive baselines. The platform supports decomposition of performance into three typically confounded factors: participant traits, partner perceptions, and content dynamics. An in-person pilot (N = 62) demonstrates its utility. Under matched time limits, originality with a GPT-4 partner is statistically equivalent to that with a human partner. Approach motivation (BAS Drive) moderates whether interactive partnership benefits originality, and self-reported cognitive outsourcing predicts lower originality specifically in human-human dyads. Prior exposure to highly creative ideas improves later performance, suggesting a "seeding" intervention. We release the platform, code, and dataset as a shared testbed for controlled studies of human-AI co-creation.

</details>


<div id='cs.SE'></div>

# cs.SE [[Back]](#toc)

### [82] [Pre-Training on Software Engineering Texts: Effects on Domain Adaptation and General-Language Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06613)
*Fabian C. Peña,Steffen Herbold*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper investigates whether language models (LMs) should be continually pre-trained (CPT) or pre-trained from scratch (PTS) for software engineering (SE) textual artifacts. Under matched compute and token budgets, CPT yields small domain gains with no degradation in general language understanding, while PTS incurs a significant penalty on both fronts, only becoming competitive for small models with ample tokens.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LMs are widely used in SE but it is unclear whether they are optimized for SE textual artifacts (issues, commit messages, developer discussions), as most evidence is code-focused. The authors seek to determine the best strategy — CPT vs. PTS — for adapting encoder and decoder LMs to SE text.

Method: The authors compare CPT and PTS on a newly constructed SE corpus under constant-token and compute-matched budgets. They evaluate both domain adaptation using the newly introduced SELU benchmark and general-language understanding using SuperGLUE, controlling for model families, sizes, and pre-training resources.

Result: CPT dominates across model families and sizes: it yields small, mostly inconclusive domain gains on SELU while leaving SuperGLUE performance essentially unchanged. In contrast, PTS suffers a large and usually decisive penalty on both axes and only becomes competitive for small LMs under a token-rich budget.

Conclusion: Reusing an existing LM via CPT is the recommended strategy for adapting LMs to SE text. Training from scratch is not advisable unless one has a very large budget and is working with small models. The authors release their corpus and pre-trained models as a replication kit.

Abstract: Generalist and code-focused Language Models (LMs) are increasingly applied to software engineering (SE), yet whether they are optimized for understanding SE textual artifacts (e.g., issues, commit messages, developer discussions) remains unclear, as most evidence comes from code-focused benchmarks. We study how to adapt encoder and decoder LMs to SE text, comparing continual pre-training (CPT) against pre-training from scratch (PTS) on a new SE corpus, and evaluating both domain adaptation (SELU) and general-language understanding (SuperGLUE). To keep the comparisons fair, we control pre-training under constant-token and compute-matched budgets. We find that across families and sizes, reusing an existing LM dominates training a domain-native one from scratch: CPT yields small and mostly inconclusive domain gains while leaving general-language understanding essentially unchanged, whereas PTS pays a large and usually decisive penalty on both axes and becomes competitive only for small LMs under a token-rich budget. We distill these results into practical guidance for adapting LMs to SE text and release our corpus and pre-trained LMs in our replication kit.

</details>


### [83] [Specification Grounding Drives Test Effectiveness for LLM Code](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06636)
*Amin Haeri,Mahdi Ghelichi*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Providing an LLM-based code tester with the specification (as a rules checklist) dramatically improves code repair outcomes (+36–38 pp) compared to telling it to test edge cases without the spec. The gain comes from grounding in specification content, not increased test quantity. Grounding eliminates false alarms (33%→0%) while catching more real bugs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs generate code that passes typical tests but fails on edge cases and specification-defined corner conditions. A common self-repair approach has the model generate tests and iterate, but it is unclear whether the benefit derives from the mere existence of tests or from tests being grounded in a specification. This paper isolates and quantifies the contribution of specification grounding.

Method: The authors fix the tester, test budget, and repair loop, varying only a single prompt line: whether the tester receives the specification as a checklist of rules. The baseline tester is already instructed to probe invalid inputs and edge cases. Experiments span three Claude tiers (Haiku, Sonnet, Opus), a held-out set, and multi-vendor replication (GPT-5.3-codex, Gemini 3.5 Flash). Ablations test format (checklist vs. plain paragraph), test budget scaling, ensemble of ungrounded suites, property-based generation, and an AlphaCodium-style loop. Statistical significance via sign test over 18 tasks.

Result: Spec-grounded testing yields +38 pp on Claude tiers and +36 pp on held-out data. Doubling test budget barely helps; eight independent ungrounded suites plateau far below grounding. Ablation: spec as plain paragraph recovers 27/30 bugs vs. 2/30 without spec. Property-based generator catches 28/30 but invents out-of-spec requirements. AlphaCodium-style loop only matches baseline. Cross-vendor: GPT-5.3-codex +28 pp, Gemini 3.5 Flash +19 pp; sign test p=0.002. Grounding reduces false-alarm rate from 33% (68% against stdlib oracle) to 0%. On well-specified algorithmic problems, grounding neither helps nor hurts.

Conclusion: The primary driver of effective LLM self-repair is grounding tests in a specification, not test volume. Spec grounding simultaneously improves sensitivity (catching more real bugs) and precision (eliminating false alarms). The effect is robust across models, vendors, and formats, with the critical factor being specification content rather than presentation.

Abstract: Large language models frequently generate code that appears correct on typical inputs yet fails on edge cases, invalid inputs, and other specification-defined corner conditions. A popular fix has the model write its own tests and repair until they pass, but the source of the gain is unclear: does it come from the tests merely existing, or from their grounding in a specification of what the code should do? We isolate this factor. Holding the tester, test budget, and repair loop fixed, we change a single prompt line that controls whether the tester receives the spec as a checklist of rules. The baseline is strong: it is already told to probe invalid inputs and edge cases. Grounding the tests in the spec produces correct code +38 percentage points more often than this baseline across three Claude tiers (Haiku 4.5, Sonnet 4.6, Opus 4.8), and +36 points on a held-out set. Grounding, not test quantity, is the primary driver: doubling the test budget barely helps, and combining eight independent ungrounded suites plateaus far below grounding. An ablation isolates the spec's content, not its format: given the spec as a plain paragraph the tester recovers 27 of 30 bugs, but asked to plan tests without the spec it recovers only 2 of 30. The effect survives stronger baselines: a property-based generator catches 28 of 30 bugs but invents out-of-spec requirements, and an AlphaCodium-style loop only matches the baseline. It replicates across vendors (GPT-5.3-codex +28, Gemini 3.5 Flash +19), with a task-level sign test over 18 tasks significant at p=0.002. Grounding improves both sensitivity and precision: it catches more real bugs and wrongly rejects far less correct code, cutting the false-alarm rate from 33% (68% against a Python standard-library oracle) to 0%. On well-specified algorithmic problems it neither helps nor hurts.

</details>


### [84] [Progressive Crystallization: Turning Agent Exploration into Deterministic, Lower-Cost Workflows in Production](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07052)
*Arun Malik*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Proposes "progressive crystallization," a lifecycle where AI agent behaviors that are repeatedly validated get converted from expensive LLM-driven executions into cheap, deterministic workflows, while allowing automatic demotion if they regress. In production, this achieved 45% deterministic execution, >70% cost reduction, and improved safety.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI agents for IT operations (AIOps) are perpetual cost centers because every execution incurs full LLM inference costs, even for problems already solved before. The authors seek to eliminate this redundant spending by capturing and hardening successful agent trajectories into reusable, deterministic artifacts.

Method: Introduces a three-stage execution taxonomy: (1) fully agent-orchestrated, (2) hybrid (agent with deterministic sub-components), and (3) fully deterministic workflows. An evidence-based promotion mechanism converts repeatedly validated agent behaviors into deterministic workflows using trace extraction. A corresponding demotion mechanism automatically reverts workflows that exhibit regression. An economic model quantifies the cost trade-offs.

Result: Evaluated on a production cloud networking AIOps system handling tens of thousands of incidents/month over eight months: deterministic execution rose from 0% to 45%, per-incident agent costs dropped by over 70% despite incident volume doubling, and reproducibility/auditability improved safety.

Conclusion: Progressive crystallization effectively transforms agent-based exploration into production-grade deterministic automation, yielding substantial cost savings and safety improvements. The paper contributes the execution taxonomy, promotion/demotion criteria, trace extraction methodology, and an economic model, while also discussing limitations and threats to validity.

Abstract: AI agents deployed for IT operations are typically permanent cost centers because every execution requires full LLM inference, even for previously solved problems. This paper introduces progressive crystallization, a lifecycle that treats agent exploration as a discovery mechanism rather than a permanent execution model. It defines a three-stage execution taxonomy, from fully agent-orchestrated to hybrid to fully deterministic workflows, together with an evidence-based promotion mechanism that converts repeatedly validated agent behaviors into cheaper and more reproducible deterministic workflows, while automatically demoting workflows that regress. Evaluated on a production cloud networking AIOps system processing tens of thousands of incidents per month, the approach increased deterministic execution from 0% to 45% over eight months, reduced per-incident agent costs by more than 70% despite doubling incident volume, and improved safety through greater reproducibility and auditability. The paper also presents the execution taxonomy, promotion and demotion criteria, trace extraction methodology, economic model, safety considerations, and discusses limitations and threats to validity.

</details>


### [85] [Reliable and Developer-Aligned Evaluation of Agents for Software Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06713)
*Razvan Mihai Popescu*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper proposes a comprehensive evaluation methodology for LLM-powered coding agents grounded in real-world software development, addressing limitations of existing fragmented benchmarks through contamination-awareness, in-the-wild agentic assessment, and trajectory-aware metrics that capture realistic contexts, human alignment, and failure modes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluation techniques for LLMs in software development are fragmented, rely on hypothetical syntactic scenarios, and produce distorted projections of true model capabilities. As LLMs transition from assistive tools to autonomous collaborative agents, there is an urgent need for evaluation grounded in real-world development practice.

Method: The authors propose a three-pronged evaluation methodology: (1) contamination-awareness to mitigate data leakage from benchmark memorization; (2) in-the-wild agentic behavior assessment that tests models in realistic, autonomous development settings; (3) trajectory-aware benchmarks and metrics that capture the full sequence of model actions, decisions, and failure modes rather than just final outputs, ensuring alignment with human developer behavior.

Result: The abstract does not report specific quantitative results, as it describes the proposed evaluation framework and methodology rather than experimental findings.

Conclusion: A robust, realistic evaluation paradigm is necessary to accurately assess LLM-powered coding agents as they become autonomous contributors in development environments. The proposed methodology bridges the gap between synthetic benchmarks and real-world software engineering practice.

Abstract: Large language models are rapidly moving towards closing the development cycle, transitioning from simple assistive companions to autonomous contributors deeply embedded into collaborative development environments. Despite their accelerated adoption, existing evaluation techniques are limited due to their fragmented nature and distorted projection of true model capabilities, often obtained from hypothetical syntactic scenarios. This research aims to bridge this gap by providing a comprehensive evaluation methodology for LLM-powered agents that is grounded in real-world software development practice. Our evaluation approach focuses on contamination-awareness, in-the-wild agentic behavior assessment, and trajectory-aware benchmarks and metrics capturing realistic coding contexts, human-aligned behavior, and model failure modes.

</details>


### [86] [SmartHomeSecure: Automated Detection and Repair of Smart Home Configuration Errors Using Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06748)
*Yizhi Wang,Xinghua Gao,Reachsak Ly,Alireza Shojaei*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: SmartHomeSecure combines lightweight program analysis with constraint-guided LLM generation to automatically detect and repair errors in Home Assistant YAML configuration files. Evaluated on 100 real-world files with injected errors, three of four tested LLMs achieved 100% detection accuracy and 87–93% repair success rates with no hallucinations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: User-authored YAML configurations in smart home platforms are prone to syntax, formatting, and semantic logic errors that cause automation failures and safety risks. Existing validators, static analysis tools, and general-purpose LLMs lack domain-specific understanding and validated correction workflows for end-to-end diagnosis and repair.

Method: SmartHomeSecure is a modular web application with four layers (UI Shell, Feature Orchestrator, Domain Engine, Integration Layer). It parses YAML files, detects syntactic and semantic errors, normalizes error context, applies deterministic auto-fixes for routine defects, and constructs constrained prompts to guide LLMs toward minimal, structurally valid repairs. Evaluation used 100 real-world Home Assistant YAML files with manually injected errors across five categories (syntax/parsing, indentation, mapping, sequence, scalar quoting), tested against four models: gpt-oss-20b, gpt-oss-120b, llama-3.1-8b, and llama-3.3-70b.

Result: Three of four models achieved 100% error detection accuracy. Repair success rates ranged from 87% to 93%. Manual verification confirmed no hallucinated or incorrect repairs among successful outputs.

Conclusion: Combining domain-aware program analysis with constrained generative AI is a feasible and effective approach for improving the reliability and usability of smart home configuration repair.

Abstract: Smart home automation platforms increasingly rely on user-authored YAML configuration files to define device behaviors, but these files are prone to syntax, formatting, and semantic logic errors that can cause automation failures and safety risks. Existing YAML validators, static analysis tools, and general-purpose large language models offer limited support for end-to-end diagnosis and repair because they lack domain-specific understanding and validated correction workflows. This paper presents SmartHomeSecure, a prototype for automated detection and repair of Home Assistant configuration errors using lightweight program analysis and constraint-guided large language model generation. SmartHomeSecure parses YAML files, detects syntactic and common semantic errors, normalizes error context, applies deterministic auto-fixes for routine defects, and constructs constrained prompts that guide LLMs toward minimal and structurally valid repairs. The system is implemented as a modular web application with four layers: UI Shell, Feature Orchestrator, Domain Engine, and Integration Layer. Its repair pipeline was evaluated on 100 real-world Home Assistant YAML files with manually injected errors across five categories: syntax/parsing, indentation, mapping, sequence, and scalar quoting errors. Four models were tested: gpt-oss-20b, gpt-oss-120b, llama-3.1-8b, and llama-3.3-70b. Results show that three models achieved 100% error detection accuracy, with repair success rates ranging from 87% to 93%. Manual verification found no hallucinated or incorrect repairs among successful outputs. These findings suggest that combining domain-aware program analysis with constrained generative AI is a feasible approach for improving the reliability and usability of smart home configuration repair.

</details>


### [87] [Deployment Risk Assessment Using Diff-Aware Features: A Case Study at Prime Video](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06766)
*Mayur Kurup,Hyunjae Suh,Swathi Vaidyanathan,Pranesh Vyas,Srinidhi Madabhushi,Yegor Silyutin*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Amazon Prime Video proposes a privacy-preserving, diff-aware framework using LLMs as multi-language feature extractors to predict risky code changes, achieving 0.83 recall and 0.81 F1. Key insight: structural code complexity metrics outperform change-volume metrics (lines added/deleted) as risk predictors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current change control at Prime Video relies on blanket deployment freezes that block all changes indiscriminately, causing developer toil. Existing risky change predictors depend on developer-specific metadata or extensive historical data, raising privacy concerns and limiting applicability to new projects. A method is needed that assesses risk directly from code modifications without privacy-invasive data.

Method: The authors introduce a framework based on diff-aware features — characteristics extracted directly from code modifications. They systematically identify quantitative metrics (code-level and change-level) and qualitative indicators (coding style violations, change type classification). LLMs serve as multi-language feature extractors, eliminating language-specific tooling. The framework is evaluated on two datasets: Prime Video's production environment and the public ApacheJIT dataset, with ablation analysis to isolate feature importance.

Result: The best model achieves an average recall of 0.83 and F1 score of 0.81 across both datasets. Ablation analysis reveals that change-level volume metrics (e.g., lines added/deleted) are noisy predictors, while structural code complexity provides a substantially stronger risk signal. The approach generalizes across programming languages and organizational contexts.

Conclusion: Thoughtful feature curation — prioritizing structural complexity over volume metrics — enables effective, privacy-preserving change risk assessment. LLMs prove effective as code analysis tools beyond generation tasks, and the diff-aware approach avoids reliance on developer metadata, making it applicable to new projects across diverse languages.

Abstract: At Amazon Prime Video, we face the critical operational challenge of managing code deployments during live events and rapid feature releases without causing service outages. Current change control approaches use blanket deployment freezes that block all changes regardless of risk, creating significant developer toil. While prior research has explored risky change predictors, these rely on developer-specific metadata or extensive historical data, raising privacy concerns and limiting applicability to new projects. We introduce a framework centered on diff-aware features, characteristics derived directly from code modifications. Our key contribution is the systematic identification of which quantitative metrics (code-level and change-level metrics) and qualitative indicators (coding style violations, change type classification) are necessary for risk prediction. We employ LLMs as multi-language feature extractors, demonstrating their effectiveness for code analysis beyond generation tasks and eliminating the need for language-specific tooling. We evaluated our framework on two datasets: Prime Video's production environment and the public ApacheJIT dataset. Our best-performing model achieves an average recall of 0.83 and F1 score of 0.81 across both datasets for detecting risky code changes. Notably, ablation analysis reveals that change-level volume metrics (e.g., lines added/deleted) are noisy predictors, while structural code complexity provides a substantially stronger risk signal. These results demonstrate that thoughtful feature curation enables effective change risk assessment across different programming languages and organizational contexts while avoiding privacy concerns.

</details>


### [88] [What Makes a Good Bug Report for an AI Agent?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07593)
*Lara Khatib,Noble Saji Mathews,Meiyappan Nagappan,Pengyu Nie,Thomas Zimmermann*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper investigates what makes a good bug report for LLM-based automated program repair (APR) agents, finding that agent-optimal reports overlap with but differ from human-optimal ones: agents benefit most from concrete, executable, well-localized information (fix suggestions, reproduction scripts, source code references), while human-oriented features like natural language steps-to-reproduce and verbose descriptions contribute little or negatively correlate with repair success.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: APR agents are moving from research benchmarks to real developer workflows, but they still rely on bug reports designed for humans. Despite decades of research on what constitutes a good bug report for human developers, it is unknown whether these same features transfer effectively to LLM-based repair agents. This gap motivates a systematic empirical study.

Method: Two complementary analyses: (1) Statistical modeling examining associations between 27 bug-report features and repair success across 433 SWE-bench Verified issues attempted by 87 repair agents. (2) Controlled ablation experiments across 2 models (Qwen, Gemma) with 17 problem-statement mutations on SWE-bench Pro, systematically removing/isolating bug-report content, deleting fault-localization cues, and testing structural changes (flattening lists, removing section headers) while holding the underlying task fixed.

Result: Fix suggestions, reproduction scripts, repository source code, and localization information are associated with higher resolution likelihood; longer reports are associated with lower odds. Both models depend on localization cues and expected behavior descriptions. Structural changes alone (flattening lists, removing headers) reduce solve rates even without content removal. The two models diverge under missing information: Qwen searches more widely and can exhaust its turn budget, while Gemma commits early to a plausible interpretation and patches on it.

Conclusion: A good bug report for an APR agent overlaps with, but is not identical to, a good report for a human. Agents benefit most from concrete, executable, and well-localized information, whereas some qualities long emphasized for human readers (natural language reproduction steps, readable descriptions) contribute little or even correlate with lower success rates. This has implications for how bug reports should be authored or adapted when targeting LLM-based repair agents.

Abstract: Automated program repair (APR) agents are transitioning from research benchmarks to developer workflows, yet they still begin with bug reports written for human developers. While decades of research have established what makes a good bug report for humans (e.g., steps to reproduce, stack traces), it remains unclear whether these features transfer to LLM-based agents. We study this question in two analyses. First, we use statistical modeling to examine associations between 27 bug-report features and repair success across 433 SWE-bench Verified issues attempted by 87 repair agents. We find that fix suggestions, reproduction scripts, repository source code, and localization info are associated with higher resolution likelihood, while longer reports are associated with lower odds. Second, we conduct controlled ablations across 2 models and 17 problem-statement mutations on SWE-bench Pro, varying the information available to an agent while holding the underlying task fixed. We remove or isolate selected bug-report content, delete fault-localization cues, and test structural changes that flatten lists or remove section headers. We find that both models depend on localization cues and expected behavior, and that structural changes alone can reduce solve rates, even without removing any content. The two models diverge in how they handle missing information: Qwen searches more widely and can exhaust its turn budget, while Gemma commits to a plausible interpretation early and patches on it. Our findings indicate that a good bug report for an agent overlaps with, but is not identical to, a good report for a human: agents benefit most from concrete, executable, and well-localized information, whereas some qualities long emphasized for human readers, such as natural language steps to reproduce and readable descriptions, contribute little or even correlate with lower success.

</details>


### [89] [Rethinking Code Performance Benchmarks for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07619)
*Nhat Minh Le,Yisen Xu,Zhijie Wang,Tse-Hsun,Chen*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Existing LLM code performance benchmarks fail to reveal meaningful execution-time differences due to inadequate test suites. The authors propose a multi-agent LLM framework to generate performance-oriented tests, which exposes significant runtime improvements in ~25% of tasks where original tests found none.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior function-level performance benchmarks (EffiBench, Enamel, EvalPerf, Mercury) show LLM-generated code has little or no runtime difference from canonical solutions. The authors hypothesize this is not just due to weak evaluation methodology, but primarily due to insufficient performance test suites that fail to expose real performance differences.

Method: First, a rigorous re-evaluation of 1,538 tasks across four benchmarks: each task run 30 times with statistical testing comparing canonical vs. performant implementations. Then, a manual analysis of 308 non-significant tasks.
