Finally, a proposed multi-agent LLM framework with three specialized agents — one to generate, one to diagnose, and one to repair deterministic performance-oriented tests — that preserve functional correctness while better exposing runtime differences. Tested with DeepSeek-v3.1 and GPT-4o against 1,345 tasks.

Result: Under rigorous re-evaluation, only 6.11% of performant implementations were significantly faster than canonical solutions. Of 308 manually analyzed non-significant tasks, 99 had no meaningful performance change, while 209 had potential improvements unexposed by original tests. The proposed framework's generated tests revealed statistically significant improvements in 24.01% (DeepSeek-v3.1) and 25.43% (GPT-4o) of the 1,345 tasks where original tests found no difference, outperforming SOTA LLM-based performance test generation.

Conclusion: The core limitation of current performance benchmarks is insufficient test suites, not just evaluation protocols. LLM-based multi-agent test generation can effectively produce performance-oriented tests that surface real runtime differences, substantially improving benchmark utility for assessing LLM code efficiency.

Abstract: Many function-level performance benchmarks have been proposed to evaluate whether large language models (LLMs) can generate efficient programs. However, results on these benchmarks often show that LLM-generated implementations have little or no execution-time difference from canonical solutions. In this paper, we revisit four popular benchmarks: EffiBench, Enamel, EvalPerf, and Mercury. We evaluate 1,538 tasks under more rigorous setting by running each task 30 times and assessing the runtime differences between the canonical solutions and benchmark-provided performant implementations with statistical testing. With the benchmark-provided test suites, only 6.11% of the performant implementations are significantly faster than the canonical solutions. In a manual analysis of 308 non-significant tasks, 99 performant implementations contain no meaningful performance change, while 209 contain potential performance improvements that are not exposed by the original tests. These results suggest that the main limitation is not only the evaluation method, but also the limited sufficiency of the benchmark-provided performance tests. To address this limitation, we propose an LLM-based multi-agent framework to generate performance-oriented tests that expose runtime differences more effectively than the original tests. The framework uses three separate agents to generate, diagnose, and repair deterministic tests that preserve functional correctness while better exposing performance differences. Across 1,345 benchmark tasks for which the original tests found no significant performance difference, tests generated by our framework with DeepSeek-v3.1 and GPT-4o reveal statistically significant improvements in 24.01% and 25.43% of the tasks, respectively, outperforming the SOTA LLM-based performance test generation method.

</details>


<div id='cs.IR'></div>

# cs.IR [[Back]](#toc)

### [90] [Seeing and Reflecting: Multimodal Memory-Enhanced Agent Collaboration for Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07108)
*Hao Cong,Huizu Lin,Zihan Wang,Chengkai Huang,Quan Z. Sheng,Lina Yao*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: MMEACR proposes a multimodal memory-enhanced agent collaboration framework for recommendation that separates interpretable agent reasoning from fine-grained multimodal matching via a dual-track architecture, achieving strong performance especially in visually grounded scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based agentic recommender systems suffer from text-centric inputs and coarse-grained memory updates, leading to missed visual evidence, semantic noise, and preference drift. There is a need for a framework that integrates multimodal signals (text + images) with persistent, fine-grained memory for more robust recommendation.

Method: MMEACR employs a dual-track architecture: (1) a reasoning track where collaborative User and Item Memory Agents maintain persistent multimodal memories updated via attribute-guided reinforcement-and-reflection; (2) a matching track with decoupled multimodal embedding memory built from raw interaction narratives and item images to preserve cross-modal details. The two tracks are fused through weighted Reciprocal Rank Fusion for final ranking.

Result: Experiments across three real-world domains demonstrate that MMEACR achieves strong overall performance against competitive LLM-based and agent-based baselines, with particularly notable gains in visually grounded recommendation scenarios.

Conclusion: The dual-track separation of reasoning and matching, combined with persistent multimodal memory and reinforcement-based updates, effectively addresses the limitations of text-centric agentic recommenders, enabling robust and interpretable recommendations.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM)-based agentic recommender systems show promise in modeling user preferences through natural-language reasoning, yet they remain limited by text-centric inputs and coarse-grained memory updates, making agents prone to missing visual evidence, semantic noise, and preference drift. To address these limitations, we propose MMEACR, a Multimodal Memory-Enhanced Agent Collaboration framework for recommendation. MMEACR introduces a dual-track memory architecture that separates interpretable agent reasoning from fine-grained multimodal matching. In the reasoning track, collaborative User and Item Memory Agents maintain persistent multimodal memories and update them through an attribute-guided reinforcement-and-reflection mechanism. In the matching track, a decoupled multi-modal embedding memory is built from raw interaction narratives and item images to preserve detailed cross-modal signals beyond structured memory updates. The two tracks are integrated through weighted Reciprocal Rank Fusion to produce robust and interpretable rankings. Experiments on three real-world domains show that MMEACR achieves strong overall performance against competitive LLM-based and agent-based baselines, with notable gains in visually grounded recommendation scenarios.

</details>


### [91] [Granularity in Actoin: Graphing sources for social history](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07183)
*Sofus Landor Dam,Johan Heinsen*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: Presents a pipeline that converts historical sources into structured data using "action" as the fundamental unit of analysis, leveraging the GRAM framework and machine learning for automated graph construction, demonstrated through a case study on pretending behaviors among runaways and itinerants in 18th–19th century Denmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors are motivated by the need for data pipelines suited to granular social history, where fine-grained human actions—rather than broad categories—are the primary analytical units. Existing approaches lack automated tools that respect this action-centric granularity while remaining compatible with close reading and manual annotation.

Method: The pipeline builds on the GRAM (Graph of Roles and Actions Model) framework. It employs a suite of machine learning tools to automatically extract and skeleton-graph actions from historical textual sources. This "auto-GRAM" approach is designed to integrate with traditional close reading and manual graphing workflows, creating a hybrid human-machine analytical pipeline.

Result: The pipeline is demonstrated through a practical example: graphing actions of "pretending" across four archival collections concerning runaways and itinerants in 18th- and 19th-century Denmark. This shows the feasibility of the auto-GRAM approach for surfacing patterned action structures from disparate historical corpora.

Conclusion: Automated action-graphing pipelines like auto-GRAM hold promise for granular social history research, enabling scalable extraction of action-based structured data from historical sources while preserving the interpretive depth of close reading. The pretending case study illustrates the method's practical applicability across multiple archival collections.

Abstract: This working paper describes a pipeline for turning historical sources into structured data organised around the principle of foregrounding action as the basic and constitutive unit of analysis. It is rooted in a desire for pipelines that suit a granular approach to social history. The pipeline rests on the principles developed in the GRAM-framework (Graph of Roles and Actions Model), but leverages a range of machine learning tools to allow for an automated, skeletal graphing of actions. Ideally, such auto-GRAMS would integrate with close readings, including extensive manual graphing. Finally, we provide an example of how this approach might work in practice by graphing actions of pretending across four separate archival collections, relating to runaways and itinerants in eighteenth and nineteenth-century Denmark.

</details>


### [92] [Interpretable Uncertainty for Adaptive Retrieval and Reasoning in Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07380)
*Ritajit Dey,Iadh Ounis,Graham McDonald*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: Proposes an uncertainty-aware framework for adaptive QA that uses LLM internal representations to decide, in a single forward pass, when to trigger retrieval (knowledge insufficiency) and when to apply additional reasoning (knowledge ambiguity/conflict), offering a transparent and efficient alternative to opaque or costly RAG policies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs perform well on QA but suffer from hallucinations and lack transparency. RAG improves factuality, but existing retrieval decisions rely on opaque policies or computationally expensive multi-step prompting. There is a need for a principled, efficient, and interpretable mechanism to decide when and how to retrieve or reason further.

Method: An uncertainty-aware adaptive QA framework that decomposes uncertainty into knowledge insufficiency and knowledge ambiguity/conflict, estimated directly from LLM hidden states in a single forward pass. These explicit signals then gate system behavior: insufficient knowledge triggers RAG-based retrieval, while high ambiguity/conflict triggers additional reasoning.

Result: The method efficiently estimates decomposed uncertainty signals from internal representations in a single forward pass, enabling transparent and adaptive decisions about retrieval and reasoning without multi-step prompting or opaque policies.

Conclusion: Grounding adaptive QA decisions in efficiently estimable, decomposed uncertainty signals from LLM hidden states provides a practical, transparent alternative to existing RAG strategies and supports the design of interpretable user-facing QA tools.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) achieve a strong performance in question answering (QA), but remain prone to hallucinations and suffer from limited transparency. Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) can improve factuality, yet decisions about when and how to retrieve from external resources are typically based on opaque policies or computationally inefficient multi-step prompting procedures. We propose an uncertainty-aware framework for adaptive QA based on explicit signals derived from LLM internal representations. We distinguish between knowledge insufficiency and knowledge ambiguity or conflict, and efficiently estimate these from hidden states in a single forward pass. These signals guide system behaviour: RAG is triggered when knowledge is insufficient, while additional reasoning is applied when ambiguity or conflict is high. By grounding adaptive decisions in decomposed and efficiently estimable uncertainty signals, this approach provides a transparent and practical alternative to existing retrieval and reasoning strategies supporting the design of interpretable user-facing tools.

</details>


<div id='cs.CR'></div>

# cs.CR [[Back]](#toc)

### [93] [Blockchain Attacks and Defenses: A Layered and Cross-Domain Survey](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06593)
*Junjie Hu,Na Ruan*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This survey systematically categorizes blockchain attacks and defenses across a four-layer architecture (network, cryptographic, consensus, application) and cross-domain trust boundaries, tracing the evolution from infrastructure threats to programmable economic abuse in DeFi and cross-chain exploits, while identifying future research needs in protocol composition security.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Blockchains have evolved from simple ledgers into complex programmable platforms carrying significant financial value. The attack surface has shifted from isolated infrastructure disruptions to programmable economic abuse and cross-domain exploits, yet existing surveys lack a unified framework that captures both layered architecture threats and cross-domain trust boundary failures. The authors aim to fill this gap by providing a comprehensive categorization and identifying how local security assumptions break under protocol composition.

Method: The paper presents a systematic survey and taxonomy that categorizes the blockchain threat landscape along two orthogonal dimensions: (1) a four-tier layered architecture comprising the network layer, cryptographic layer, consensus layer, and application layer; and (2) cross-domain trust boundaries that emerge when protocols interoperate across different blockchain ecosystems. The survey synthesizes a decade of academic and industry research on attacks and corresponding mitigation strategies.

Result: The survey reveals that blockchain security research has shifted from lower-layer concerns (network-level attacks, consensus vulnerabilities) toward application-layer and cross-domain threats, driven by the rise of DeFi and cross-chain bridges. It demonstrates that local security assumptions in individual protocols frequently fail under composition, leading to novel exploit classes such as oracle manipulation, MEV extraction, governance attacks, and bridge vulnerabilities that cannot be captured by single-layer analysis alone.

Conclusion: Future Web3 security research must move beyond single-protocol analysis toward holistic, compositional security frameworks that account for cross-domain interactions. Key open challenges include formal verification of protocol compositions, secure interoperability design, economic security under adversarial conditions, and systematic defense-in-depth strategies spanning all architectural layers.

Abstract: Blockchains have evolved from simple distributed ledgers into programmable platforms that process complex application logic and carry significant financial value. All modern Web3 systems share a common goal: providing secure, decentralized, and trustworthy execution in an increasingly interconnected environment. However, this evolution has shifted the attack surface from isolated infrastructure disruptions to programmable economic abuse and cross-domain exploits. In this article, we focus on the research of blockchain attacks and defenses. In particular, we categorize the threat landscape and corresponding mitigation strategies according to both a four-tier layered architecture (network, cryptographic, consensus, and application) and cross-domain trust boundaries. We seek to answer these important questions: How has the research in blockchain security evolved over the past decade, especially with the rise of decentralized finance (DeFi) and cross-chain interoperability? How do local security assumptions fail when protocols are composed, and what are the driving needs for Web3 security research in the future?

</details>


### [94] [When Agents Remember Too Much: Memory Poisoning Attacks on Large Language Model Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06595)
*George Torres,Sharad Shrestha,Satyajayant Misra*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: GhostWriter is a novel attack that poisons the long-term memory of personal AI agents, achieving ~98% injection and ~60% activation rates. The authors propose AM-Sentry, a dual mitigation combining memory-saving policies and retrieval screening, to defend against this vulnerability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Personal AI agents that combine conversational and action-planning capabilities with long-term memory introduce unexamined security vulnerabilities, as they handle sensitive information while interacting with untrusted sources. Existing memory subsystems lack security-focused governance.

Method: GhostWriter operates in two phases: (1) Injection — an adversary sends a hidden attack payload to the target agent's memory store; (2) Activation — the poisoned memory is later retrieved during task execution. The defense, Agentic Memory Sentry (AM-Sentry), employs a memory-saving policy to filter what enters memory and a memory-retrieval screen to inspect retrieved memories before use.

Result: GhostWriter achieves near-universal injection rates (~98%) and a high average activation rate (~60%) against state-of-the-art personal agents. AM-Sentry dramatically reduces GhostWriter's success rate while preserving agent utility.

Conclusion: Current long-term memory subsystems in tool-using personal AI agents are critically vulnerable to memory poisoning attacks. AM-Sentry provides an effective defense by introducing structured memory governance without degrading agent performance.

Abstract: Personal AI agents powered by large language models can reason and act using available tools to access emails, manage calendars, and push code to remote repositories, all with minimal oversight. When augmented with long-term memory, an agent can recall specific details relevant to the current task, reducing the need for large context windows. Currently, long-term memory agents tend to fall into two distinct domains: conversational and action-planning agents. Personal assistant agents sit at the convergence of these two domains and handle sensitive information while interacting with untrusted information sources, creating previously unaccounted security vulnerabilities. In this work, we introduce the novel attack vector, GhostWriter, which exploits current memory subsystems in tool-using personal agents to poison their memory store. GhostWriter operates in two phases: injection, where an adversary sends a hidden attack payload to the target agent; and activation, in which the poisoned memory is retrieved. We show that GhostWriter achieves near-universal injection rates of approximately 98% and a high average activation rate of approximately 60% against state-of-the-art agents. This attack is possible due to the lack of security-focused memory governance. In response, we propose Agentic Memory Sentry (AM-Sentry), which leverages two mitigation techniques: a memory-saving policy and a memory-retrieval screen. Our experiments show that AM-Sentry dramatically reduces GhostWriter's success rate while preserving agent utility.

</details>


### [95] [Calibration-Family Overfit: Why Trusted Sabotage Monitors Don't Transfer Across Lineages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06596)
*Lucas Pinto*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: Trusted monitoring in AI control exhibits a significant cross-family performance gap: monitors calibrated on one model lineage detect sabotage substantially worse on another lineage. This "interaction effect" (~+0.17 AUROC) is geometric in nature — each family's sabotage occupies a distinct scoring direction — meaning single-pairing evaluations systematically overstate real-world monitor safety. The authors propose a four-step protocol and recommend reporting cross-family transfer matrices instead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI control evaluations treat trusted monitoring accuracy as an intrinsic property of the monitor itself, testing against only one or two untrusted models. The authors hypothesize that detection accuracy is partly a property of the specific monitor–untrusted model *pairing*, and that single-pairing results overstate safety against uncalibrated lineages. This matters for realistic threat models where defenders cannot assume the untrusted model will match the lineage used during calibration.

Method: The authors make the untrusted policy family the controlled independent variable. They fit a monitor on transcripts from family A, test on family B, and decompose the cross-family AUROC into three components: (1) how obvious each family's sabotage is, (2) how capable each monitor is, and (3) the residual own-family advantage (the interaction). Experiments span four open-weight families on a strict leak-free basis, expand to an eight-family matrix across four frontier vendors, use blatancy-matched controls, a transcript-reading monitor up to 34B parameters, and a second code benchmark. They further analyze the geometric structure by rotating the monitor's scoring direction toward the target lineage and comparing against matched random rotations.

Result: A positive interaction of +0.172 AUROC (95% CI [+0.158, +0.185]) survives dominant confounds. The gap is geometric: each lineage's sabotage occupies a different scoring direction; rotating toward the target recovers most lost detection, while random rotation recovers nothing. At a 1% audit budget with defer-to-trusted, an off-lineage monitor catches 19% of attacks vs. 41% for an in-lineage monitor — a 21.7 percentage-point gap. Results hold across model scales, vendors, and benchmarks.

Conclusion: Control evaluations must report cross-family transfer matrices rather than single-pairing AUROCs. The proposed four-step protocol — measuring the interaction, rotating scoring directions, calibrating to the gap, and reporting transfer matrices — provides a principled way to characterize and mitigate the lineage-specificity of trusted monitoring, yielding more honest safety estimates for deployment.

Abstract: Trusted monitoring is a central defense in AI control: a cheaper trusted model scores an untrusted model's actions for sabotage, and the most suspicious are audited or deferred. Such monitors are evaluated against one or two untrusted models, and the accuracy is reported as a property of the monitor. We ask whether it is partly a property of the pairing. We make the untrusted policy family the controlled axis: we fit a monitor on family A's transcripts, apply it to family B, and decompose the cross-family AUROC into how obvious each family's sabotage is, how capable each monitor is, and the residual own-family advantage after both are removed: the interaction. On code-backdoor transcripts the interaction is positive and survives the dominant confounds: +0.172 (95% CI [+0.158, +0.185]) on four open-weight families on a strict leak-free basis, holding across an eight-family matrix spanning four frontier vendors, blatancy-matched controls, a trained transcript-reading monitor up to 34B parameters, and a second code benchmark. The gap is geometric: each lineage's sabotage occupies a different scoring direction, and rotating the monitor's scoring direction toward the target lineage recovers most of the lost detection while a matched random rotation recovers nothing. At a 1% audit budget under defer-to-trusted, an off-lineage monitor catches 19% of attack attempts where an in-lineage monitor catches 41% at the same audit cost, a +21.7-point gap. Control evaluations should report cross-family transfer matrices: a single-pairing accuracy overstates the safety a monitor delivers against a lineage it was not calibrated on. We give a four-step protocol that acts on the gap, with each step a measured result.

</details>


### [96] [Security and Privacy in Agentic AI: Grand Challenges and Future Directions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06608)
*Adam Jenkins,Agnieszka Kitkowska,Caterina Maidhof,Diego Paracuellos,Francesco Sovrano,Gonzalo Gabriel Mendez,Guillermo Suarez-Tangil,Hana Kopecka,Isabel Wagner,Isabel Barbera,Javier Carnerero-Cano,Jide Edu,Jose Luis Martin-Navarro,Jose Such,Josep Domingo-Ferrer,Juan Carlos Carrillo,Kopo Marvin Ramokapane,Mark Cote,Pablo Vellosillo,Ramon Ruiz-Dolz,Rongjun Ma,Ruba Abu-Salma,Sameer Patil,William Seymour,Xiao Zhan*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: A horizon-scanning exercise involving 30 international experts identifies key challenges and future research directions for security and privacy in agentic AI systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The increasing agency of AI systems—their ability to act autonomously, make decisions, and interact with environments—introduces novel and poorly understood security and privacy risks that demand systematic investigation and forward-looking research agendas.

Method: A horizon-scanning exercise convening thirty leading experts from academia, industry, and government for structured discussions and collaborative exercises to surface, map, and prioritize emerging risks.

Result: The paper reports a set of key challenges and future research directions, though the abstract does not enumerate specific findings.

Conclusion: The abstract implies that agentic AI poses distinct security and privacy threats warranting a coordinated, cross-sector research effort to address them proactively.

Abstract: We present key challenges and future research directions in the security and privacy of agentic AI, based on a horizon-scanning exercise that brought together thirty leading international experts from academia, industry, and government to engage in focused discussions and collaborative exercises on the emerging risks associated with the growing agency of AI.

</details>


### [97] [PRoVeFL: Private Robust and Verifiable Aggregation in Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06612)
*Harsh Kasyap,Anil Kumar Pradhan,Ugur Ilker Atmaca,Graham Cormode,Carsten Maple*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: PRoVeFL is a modular federated learning framework that combines multi-key fully homomorphic encryption with a multi-server architecture to simultaneously achieve privacy preservation, Byzantine robustness, and verifiable aggregation, improving runtime by up to 100x over Prio and 10x over ELSA.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional FL faces a trilemma: centralized servers enable inference attacks, Byzantine clients can poison models, and existing solutions that address both privacy and integrity rely on heavy cryptography with prohibitive computational/communication overhead. There is no framework that simultaneously delivers privacy, Byzantine resilience, and verifiability without excessive cost.

Method: PRoVeFL uses multiple servers and multi-key fully homomorphic encryption. Clients encrypt local model updates and distribute encrypted shares across servers. A hybrid computation model offloads ciphertext operations to the plaintext domain under strict privacy constraints, enabling efficient evaluation of complex statistical aggregation rules (Krum, Trimmed Mean, FLTrust, norm clipping, MESAS, etc.). Verifiability is achieved with minimal trust in at least one honest server.

Result: PRoVeFL demonstrates scalability across varying parameter counts and participant numbers. It achieves up to 100x runtime improvement over Prio and up to 10x over ELSA under comparable security guarantees.

Conclusion: PRoVeFL resolves the privacy–integrity–verifiability trade-off in FL through a modular multi-server, hybrid plaintext-ciphertext design that supports diverse Byzantine-robust aggregation rules while dramatically reducing computational overhead compared to prior distributed-trust approaches.

Abstract: Federated Learning (FL) enables multiple clients to collaboratively train machine learning models while retaining data locality, thereby enhancing user privacy. However, traditional FL frameworks rely on a centralized aggregation server and assume honest-but-curious clients, making them susceptible to both server-side inference and client-side poisoning attacks. Although recent work has explored secure and Byzantine-resilient FL protocols, they face a fundamental trade-off among privacy, integrity, and verifiability, and incur substantial computational and communication overhead due to the heavy use of cryptographic primitives. In this work, we propose PRoVeFL-a novel, modular FL framework that is Privacy-preserving, Byzantine-Robust, and ensures Verifiable aggregation. PRoVeFL employs multiple servers leveraging multi-key fully homomorphic encryption. Each client encrypts its local model updates and distributes encrypted shares to all servers. This design enables a hybrid computation model in which ciphertext operations are carefully offloaded to the plaintext domain under strict privacy constraints to efficiently evaluate complex statistical aggregation rules. PRoVeFL is compatible with a wide range of state-of-the-art Byzantine-robust aggregation algorithms (e.g., Krum, Trimmed Mean, FLTrust, norm clipping, MESAS, and more) and further enhances them with verifiability mechanisms that require minimal trust in at least one honest server. We evaluate it across different settings and demonstrate its scalability with varying numbers of parameters and participants. PRoVeFL improves runtime over the prior works, Prio and ELSA, based on distributed trust with comparable security guarantees, up to 100x and 10x, respectively.

</details>


### [98] [ECO/CPO-DAG: A Contradiction-Based Accountability Layer for Adversarial Supply Chains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06804)
*Sebastian Cochinescu*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: ECO/CPO-DAG is a domain-specific accountability protocol for adversarial supply chains that uses signed Event Claim Objects in a DAG to detect contradictions. When two claims violate a domain constraint, any observer can compile a Contradiction Proof Object that triggers economic slashing of the blamed party. It targets provable contradictions (not consistent lies), uses EPCIS 2.0 semantics, and provides analytical detection/deterrence models with a reference implementation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Adversarial supply chains lack a lightweight, domain-specific accountability mechanism that can detect and economically punish provable contradictions without relying on consensus or global truth establishment. Existing approaches either require full consensus, trusted oracles, or cannot deterministically assign blame when inconsistencies arise.

Method: The protocol uses a DAG of signed Event Claim Objects (ECOs) with causal ordering (happened-before edges). Domain constraints are mapped to GS1 EPCIS 2.0 semantics (spatial uniqueness, temporal monotonicity, quantity conservation, quality monotonicity, regulatory validity). When two signed claims violate a constraint, observers compile a Contradiction Proof Object (CPO) — a self-verifying bundle of the two claims and the violated rule. Public verification triggers economic slashing of the determinately blamed party. Selective disclosure via commitment schemes (and optional zero-knowledge proofs) limits content exposure until challenge. Analytical models cover independent-observer detection probability, collusion deterrence conditions, and storage estimates (~1 GB/participant/year).

Result: Analytical results include: detection probability model 1-(1-p_min)^h, deterrence condition S > g(1-p)/(kp) under k-party collusion, and ~1 GB/participant/year storage estimate. The reference implementation corroborated the detection model — predicted coverage band overlapped the measured 95% CI at every observer count — with zero false accusations. Fully ZK CPOs, multi-party propagation, and adaptive-adversary evasion remain analytical (unimplemented).

Conclusion: ECO/CPO-DAG provides a practical supplemental validation layer that detects provable contradictions rather than establishing truth. It complements (not replaces) source verification and oracle aggregation. The protocol's explicit boundary — invisibility to parties that never contradict themselves — is a design feature, not a limitation, positioning it as a modular accountability primitive for adversarial supply chain settings.

Abstract: We present ECO/CPO-DAG, a domain-specific accountability protocol for adversarial supply chains that formalizes contradiction detection as a supplemental validation layer rather than a consensus or truth-establishing mechanism. Participants publish signed Event Claim Objects (ECOs) into a causally ordered, append-only directed acyclic graph (DAG) whose edges encode happened-before relations. When two claims about the same subject violate a domain constraint, any observer can compile a Contradiction Proof Object (CPO), a self-verifying object binding the two signed claims and the violated rule, which, on public verification, triggers economic slashing of a determinately blamed party. We map constraints to GS1 EPCIS 2.0 event semantics (spatial uniqueness, temporal monotonicity, quantity conservation, quality monotonicity, regulatory validity), so detection targets inconsistencies that are meaningful in practice. Selective disclosure via commitment schemes and, optionally, zero-knowledge contradiction proofs lets parties withhold claim contents until a challenge forces the minimal opening. We give an analytical treatment: an independent-observer detection model $1-(1-p_{\min})^h$, a deterrence condition $S>g(1-p)/(kp)$ under $k$-party collusion, and a storage estimate of order 1 GB per participant per year under stated assumptions. The protocol's boundary is explicit: it detects provable contradictions, not consistent lies; a party that never contradicts itself is invisible to it, so the layer complements, and does not replace, source verification and oracle aggregation. A single-machine reference implementation corroborates the detection model, with the predicted coverage band overlapping the measured 95% confidence interval at every observer count, and records zero false accusations; the fully zero-knowledge CPO, multi-party propagation, and adaptive-adversary evasion remain analytical.

</details>


### [99] [When Agents Go Rogue: Activation-Based Detection of Malicious Behaviors in Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06807)
*Haowen Xu,Xue Tan,Lei Ma,Zhihao Zhang,Chao Wang,Qingze Wang,Ping Chen,Jun Dai,Xiaoyan Sun*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: AcMAS is an activation-based detection framework for LLM multi-agent systems that identifies stealthy malicious behaviors by analyzing internal reasoning states in activation space, without relying on explicit interaction graphs, and restores compromised agents rather than isolating them.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing MAS security defenses assume semantically-explicit attacks and explicit graph-based interaction modeling. However, real-world attacks are becoming semantically stealthy, and MAS execution is asynchronous without temporal alignment assumed by graph propagation models, creating a gap that current methods cannot address.

Method: AcMAS analyzes internal reasoning states in the activation space of local agents to detect malicious behavior. It operates in a synchronization-robust manner without explicit interaction graphs. The activation analysis also provides signals to guide functional restoration of compromised agents, avoiding disruptive isolation.

Result: AcMAS outperforms graph-based baselines: +0.22 F1 in synchronous settings (0.94 vs. 0.72) and +0.55 F1 in asynchronous settings (0.93 vs. 0.38). It generalizes across diverse open-source LLM backbones, attack intensities, and MAS scales.

Conclusion: Activation-space analysis offers a robust, generalizable approach to MAS security that handles both synchronous and asynchronous settings while enabling agent restoration rather than isolation, significantly advancing the state of the art against stealthy attacks.

Abstract: While enabling effective collaboration on complex tasks, LLM-based Multi-Agent Systems (MAS) face critical security challenges due to vulnerabilities at the agent and interaction levels. Most existing MAS security defenses are built upon two core assumptions: semantically-explicit malicious attacks and explicit graph-based modeling of the MAS topology and agent-level interactions. In practice, real-world attacks are becoming more semantically stealthy, while MAS execution is typically asynchronous without the temporal alignment assumed by graph-based propagation models. To address these limitations, we propose AcMAS, an activation-based framework for malicious-behavior detection in MAS. By analyzing internal reasoning states in the activation space of local agents, AcMAS detects even stealthy attacks in a synchronization-robust fashion, without relying on explicit interaction graphs. Moreover, our activation analysis provides critical signals to guide AcMAS in restoring the functionality of compromised agents, rather than the disruptive agent isolation commonly used by the state-of-the-art methods. Comprehensive evaluation demonstrates that AcMAS significantly outperforms graph-based baselines against stealthy attacks, by +0.22 F1 in synchronous settings (0.94 vs. 0.72) and by +0.55 F1 in asynchronous settings (0.93 vs. 0.38), with generalization across diverse open-source LLM backbones, attack intensity, and MAS scale.

</details>


### [100] [Thinking More, Harnessing Better: State Machine Guided Harness Automatic Generation with Project Digestion and Workflow Decomposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07007)
*Xing Zhang,Zikang Huang,Gang Yang,CongChong Wang,Lu Liu,Bin Yin,Mingyi Wang,Ziquan Zhao,Min Li,Zhenyu Chen,Bo Wu,Lingyun Ying*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: SynapseFlow is an automatic fuzz harness generator that improves over one-turn LLM methods by using dataflow-aware function aggregation and a staged, rollback-enabled generation workflow, achieving superior branch coverage and bug detection, including 7 previously unreported bugs (5 CVEs).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based fuzz harness generation methods rely on one-turn generation, which suffers from hallucinations and inadequate coverage due to coarse-grained function targeting and misaligned generation workflows. A more structured, fine-grained approach is needed to improve harness quality and correctness.

Method: SynapseFlow proposes two innovations: (1) dataflow-aware function aggregation that constructs Structural Flow Graphs and extracts coherent Function Triplets from source code; (2) a decomposed four-stage harness synthesis process with a staged rollback algorithm to ensure correctness at each stage.

Result: On 25 real-world open-source projects, SynapseFlow outperforms OSS-Fuzz-Gen, CKGFuzzer, and PromeFuzz with 3.07×, 1.71×, and 4.26× higher branch coverage, and 1.77×, 1.51×, and 1.36× higher bug detection rates. It discovered 7 previously unreported bugs, 5 of which received CVE assignments.

Conclusion: SynapseFlow demonstrates that structured, dataflow-aware function aggregation combined with staged rollback-enabled generation significantly improves fuzz harness quality over existing LLM-based methods, enabling effective real-world bug discovery.

Abstract: High-quality fuzz harnesses are essential for effective gray-box fuzzing. While Large Language Models (LLMs) offer promise for automating this task, existing one-turn generation methods suffer from hallucinations and inadequate coverage due to coarse-grained function targeting and misaligned generation workflows. We present SynapseFlow, an automatic harness generator that addresses these limitations through two key innovations: dataflow-aware function aggregation and a staged, rollback-enabled generation workflow decomposition. SynapseFlow first analyzes source code to construct Structural Flow Graphs and extract coherent Function Triplets. It then synthesizes harnesses via a decomposed four-stage process governed by a staged rollback algorithm to ensure correctness. We evaluated SynapseFlow on 25 real-world open-source software projects. The experimental results indicate that SynapseFlow outperforms state-of-the-art tools (OSS-Fuzz-Gen, CKGFuzzer, PromeFuzz), achieving 3.07$\times$, 1.71$\times$, and 4.26$\times$ higher branch coverage, and 1.77$\times$, 1.51$\times$, and 1.36$\times$ higher bug detection rates, respectively. Most importantly, SynapseFlow discovered 7 previously unreported bugs (5 assigned CVEs), demonstrating its practical effectiveness in real-world bug discovery.

</details>


### [101] [Modeling Failure Dynamics in Time-Constrained Authentication Systems: Evidence of a Success Cliff in USSD Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07650)
*Aklile Seyoum Mamo,Amanuel Kebede,Anny Christelle Irakoze,Jema Ndibwile*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper introduces the "Success Cliff" — a non-linear phenomenon where session success rates in time-constrained interactive systems (like USSD financial services) collapse sharply once authentication complexity crosses a critical threshold. Using a simulation framework, the authors quantify the security-usability trade-off under constraints of user response time and network round-trip time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Time-constrained interactive systems (e.g., USSD-based financial services) face a fundamental tension: stronger authentication improves security but increases interaction time, which under strict session limits can cause transactions to fail. The authors aim to formally model and quantify this failure dynamic to identify when security mechanisms become counterproductive.

Method: The authors develop a simulation-based framework that models failure dynamics in time-constrained interactive systems. They incorporate three key variables — authentication complexity, user response time, and network round-trip time — and conduct controlled experiments to characterize how these factors interact to affect overall session success rates.

Result: The study formally defines and demonstrates a non-linear failure phenomenon called the "Success Cliff": beyond a critical complexity threshold, session success rates drop sharply rather than degrading gradually. The experiments quantify the precise trade-off between security and usability, revealing conditions under which secure authentication workflows become operationally unreliable.

Conclusion: There exists a critical authentication complexity boundary beyond which time-constrained interactive systems suffer catastrophic failure in session completion. System designers must account for this non-linear threshold to avoid deploying authentication mechanisms that inadvertently render services unusable.

Abstract: Time-constrained interactive systems such as USSD (Unstructured Supplementary Service Data)-based financial services operate under strict session limits and sequential user interaction. While stronger authentication mechanisms improve security, they also increase interaction complexity and time burden, potentially reducing transaction completion. In this work, we model the failure dynamics of such systems and investigate how authentication complexity interacts with user response time and network round-trip time to influence session success rate. We propose and implement a simulation-based framework to investigate these failure dynamics and formally define a non-linear failure phenomenon, termed the \textit{Success Cliff}, where session success rates sharply decline beyond a critical complexity threshold. Through controlled experiments, we quantify the trade-off between security and usability and identify conditions under which secure authentication workflows become operationally unreliable.

</details>


### [102] [Cyber Dynamics I: Finite Macrostates for Behavioral Anomaly Detection in Network Telemetry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07075)
*Abdul Rahman,Eranga Bandara,Sachin Shetty*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: The paper proposes a finite-dimensional macrostate framework for network telemetry that coarse-grains over persistent entities, relations, and temporal state, capturing multiple behavioral dimensions beyond scalar entropy. It models macrostate transitions to distinguish benign drift from adversarial reorganization, outperforming traditional entropy-based and standard anomaly detectors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing entropy-based network anomaly detection methods treat entropy as a scalar statistic on narrow observables, failing to capture the broader behavioral state-space of cyber systems. This limits their ability to distinguish between benign workload drift and genuine adversarial reorganization.

Method: A finite-dimensional macrostate framework is instantiated over the Canonical Security Telemetry Substrate (CSTS), performing coarse-graining over persistent entities, typed relations, and temporal state. The macrostate captures activity, distributional disorder, structural organization, temporal volatility, persistence, and deviation from benign baselines. Window-to-window macrostate transitions are modeled to define regime structure, stability, and anomalous change.

Result: The proposed representation improves anomaly discrimination and supports more interpretable behavioral analysis compared to Shannon-, Rényi-, and Tsallis-style entropy baselines and standard anomaly detectors, as evaluated on benchmark network telemetry datasets.

Conclusion: Moving from scalar entropy to a structured macrostate representation over the CSTS enables richer behavioral characterization of network telemetry, yielding better anomaly detection and interpretability, particularly in differentiating benign drift from adversarial activity.

Abstract: Entropy-based methods have long been used for network anomaly detection, but most existing approaches treat entropy as a scalar statistic on narrow observables rather than as part of a broader behavioral state-space for cyber systems. We propose a finite-dimensional macrostate framework for network telemetry, instantiated over the Canonical Security Telemetry Substrate (CSTS), so that coarse-graining is performed over persistent entities, typed relations, and temporal state rather than isolated event records. The resulting macrostate captures activity, distributional disorder, structural organization, temporal volatility, persistence, and deviation from benign baselines. Rather than scoring only unusual states, we model window-to-window macrostate transitions and define regime structure, stability, and anomalous change. This supports discrimination between benign workload drift and adversarial reorganization. We evaluate the framework on benchmark network telemetry datasets and compare it against Shannon-, Rényi-, and Tsallis-style entropy baselines, as well as standard anomaly detectors. The proposed representation improves anomaly discrimination and supports more interpretable behavioral analysis of cyber telemetry.

</details>


### [103] [Is Randomness Necessary for Adaptive Data Analysis?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07085)
*Edith Cohen,Haim Kaplan,Yishay Mansour,Shay Sapir,Uri Stemmer*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper resolves a long-standing open question in Adaptive Data Analysis (ADA): Is randomness necessary? The authors prove that, against computationally unbounded analysts, deterministic mechanisms can only answer ~O(n) queries before failing, whereas randomized mechanisms achieve ~n² queries. This establishes a strict separation, showing randomness is information-theoretically necessary for ADA.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite a decade of work on ADA, the necessity of randomness remained unresolved. A folklore observation held that randomness is unnecessary against computationally bounded analysts, but the question against unbounded analysts was open. The paper aims to close this fundamental gap in understanding the role of randomization in preventing overfitting under adaptive data reuse.

Method: The authors work in the information-theoretic Random Oracle model. They construct an adversarial strategy (as the unbounded analyst) that forces any deterministic mechanism to fail after k = Õ(n) adaptively chosen statistical queries, leveraging the ability to simulate and exploit the deterministic mechanism's behavior through the random oracle.

Result: The main result is a lower bound: any deterministic mechanism can be forced to fail after answering only Õ(n) adaptive queries. This contrasts sharply with randomized mechanisms that support ≈n² queries, thereby proving that randomness is strictly (i.e., information-theoretically) necessary for non-trivial adaptive data analysis.

Conclusion: Randomness is not merely a computational convenience but an information-theoretic necessity in ADA. The work resolves the open question by establishing a provable separation between deterministic and randomized mechanisms against unbounded analysts, with a quadratic gap (Õ(n) vs. ~n²) in the number of supportable queries.

Abstract: The Adaptive Data Analysis (ADA) problem formalizes the challenge of preventing false discovery and overfitting when a dataset is repeatedly reused. Formally, our input is a dataset containing $n$ i.i.d. samples from an unknown distribution $\mathcal{P}$ over a domain $\mathcal{X}$, and our goal is to answer a sequence of $k$ adaptively chosen statistical queries with respect to $\mathcal{P}$. The main question is how many queries we can support (i.e., how large $k$ can be), primarily as a function of the number of samples $n$. This question has been intensively studied and is relatively well-understood for randomized mechanisms: there are computationally efficient mechanisms that support $k \approx n^2$ queries, and no computationally efficient mechanism can answer $k \gg n^2$ queries. In this paper, we address a fundamental question: is randomness necessary for ADA?
  Despite a decade of work on ADA, this question remains open. A folklore observation dating back to the initial works on ADA is that randomness is not necessary when the analyst is computationally bounded. Yet, the necessity of randomness against computationally unbounded analysts has remained elusive. Our main contribution resolves this gap in the information-theoretic Random Oracle model. Perhaps surprisingly, we show that randomness is strictly necessary to answer a non-trivial number of adaptive queries: when the analyst is unbounded, any deterministic mechanism can be forced to fail after just $k = \tilde{O} (n)$ queries.

</details>


### [104] [Mitigating Taint-Style Vulnerabilities in MCP Servers via Security-Aware Tool Descriptions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07461)
*Yang Shi,Jiaheng Fu,Yihe Huang,Ruixiang Wu,Chengyao Sun,Kaifeng Huang*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: SPELLSMITH is a framework that mitigates taint-style vulnerabilities in MCP servers by embedding behavioral guidance into tool descriptions and leveraging LLM self-reflection, rather than relying on code-level patches. A systematic analysis reveals these vulnerabilities are prevalent, hard to fix, and slow to be addressed by the community.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MCP enables LLMs to invoke external tools dynamically, but this expands the attack surface. Prior work has focused on malicious MCP servers, yet the broader vulnerability landscape—especially taint-style vulnerabilities that allow untrusted data to flow into sensitive operations—remains underexplored. Code-level fixes are context-specific, require significant modifications, and face slow community adoption, motivating a unified, protocol-level mitigation.

Method: The authors first systematically analyze MCP server vulnerabilities across three dimensions: metadata characteristics, vulnerable code patterns, and community response times. Based on findings that taint-style vulnerabilities dominate, they propose SPELLSMITH, which operates in two modules: (1) a Description Enhancement Module that analyzes high-risk capabilities, tool descriptions, and parameter semantics to build a tool-level risk profile and embed behavioral constraints into the MCP Description property; and (2) a Self-Reflection Module that exploits LLM self-reflection to iteratively evaluate and refine outputs against those constraints, blocking taint-style exploitation at the decision-making level rather than the code level.

Result: The systematic study shows taint-style vulnerabilities constitute a substantial fraction of MCP server vulnerabilities, require significant code modifications to remediate, and receive slow community responses. SPELLSMITH effectively mitigates taint-style vulnerability exploitation across multiple MCP servers, demonstrating practical applicability and advantages over traditional code-level mitigations.

Conclusion: SPELLSMITH provides an active, unified, protocol-level mitigation for taint-style vulnerabilities in MCP servers that generalizes across diverse vulnerabilities, reducing dependency on slow, context-specific code fixes. The approach highlights the potential of leveraging LLM self-reflection and protocol-level behavioral guidance as a scalable security strategy for LLM-agent ecosystems.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed as autonomous agents that interact with external tools and services via the Model Context Protocol (MCP), a standardized interface for dynamic tool invocation. While MCP simplifies integration, it also expands the attack surface and enables generic exploits across multiple servers. Despite prior work on malicious MCP servers, the vulnerability landscape of MCP servers remains underexplored. In this work, we systematically analyze MCP server vulnerabilities, focusing on metadata characteristics, vulnerable code patterns, and community responses. Our study reveals that taint-style vulnerabilities constitute a substantial fraction of MCP server vulnerabilities, require significant code modifications to remediate, and are met with slow community responses. Motivated by these findings, we propose SPELLSMITH, presenting a novel textbased avenue for shielding taint-style vulnerabilities in MCP servers. In particular, SPELLSMITH analyzes the high-risk capabilities exposed by an MCP server and combines them with tool descriptions and parameter semantics to identify potential taint-style vulnerability risks, thereby constructing a tool-level risk profile. Then, SPELLSMITH leverages the Description property of the protocol to embed behavioral guidance (Description Enhancement Module) and exploits LLMs' self-reflection capabilities (Self-Reflection Module) to iteratively evaluate and refine outputs. By strengthening LLM internal decision-making, SPELLSMITH provides an active and unified mitigation strategy that generalizes across multiple vulnerabilities, reducing reliance on context-specific code-level fixes. Our experiments demonstrate that SPELLSMITH effectively mitigates taint-style vulnerability exploitation in MCP servers, highlighting its practical applicability and advantages over traditional code-level mitigations.

</details>


### [105] [Certifying Ghosts: How Cybersecurity AI Agents Break the EU Cyber Resilience Act](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07109)
*Víctor Mayoral-Vilches*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: The EU Cyber Resilience Act (CRA) relies on four assumptions about vulnerability management. Cybersecurity AI agents falsify all four. While the regulation bends but holds against assumption P1 (volume), it breaks against P2–P4 because AI-driven exploitation renders static, point-in-time certification meaningless. The paper argues that only continuous, agent-operated security regimes can survive in an AI-transformed threat landscape, and demonstrates this with robotic systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The CRA mandates vulnerability management processes rather than vulnerability-free products, betting that four conditions hold: slow human-driven discovery, knowable flaws at ship time, noticeable exploitation, and fixes outpacing discovery. The authors argue that Cybersecurity AI agents fundamentally invalidate these premises, threatening the regulation's efficacy before it reaches full force in December 2027.

Method: The paper systematically maps each of the four CRA premises (P1–P4) against the capabilities of cybersecurity AI agents, analyzing which regulatory mechanisms bend versus break. It then proposes and implements a remedy — agentic, continuous defense — on two CRA-scope physical systems (a humanoid robot and a lawn mower), demonstrating that an AI defender sustains security where static approaches fail.

Result: Against P1, the CRA bends: it can shift from scarce-attention compliance to defensible, documented prioritization. Against P2–P4, it breaks: AI agents enable post-certification exploitability without product modification, rendering market-entry tests, reporting triggers, and one-time certificates obsolete. The empirical demonstration shows agentic defenders successfully holding a security posture on both robots where undefended configurations cannot.

Conclusion: Static, human-paced security certification is obsolete. Since attackers and defenders wield the same AI tools, only continuous, agent-operated conformity regimes survive. The CRA, reaching full force in December 2027, is poised to certify products against an already-changed world; the transition to continuous, agentic security is no longer optional.

Abstract: The EU Cyber Resilience Act (CRA) makes a smart bet. It does not demand that products be free of vulnerabilities, but only that manufacturers run a process: assess risk, handle flaws, ship updates. The bet pays off if four things about the world stay true: (P1) finding vulnerabilities is slow, skilled, human work; (P2) a product's exploitable flaws are knowable the day it ships; (P3) exploitation is rare enough to notice; and (P4) fixes keep pace with discovery. Cybersecurity AI (CAI) agents, AI put to work finding and exploiting flaws in other products, falsify all four. The regime answers in two opposite ways. Against the sheer volume of flaws that agents surface it bends (P1): built for scarce attention, it re-centres compliance on defensible, documented prioritisation, and holds. But agents also collapse the speed and economics of the vulnerability lifecycle, and here it breaks (P2, P3, P4): a product that passed every check becomes exploitable without anyone touching it, so its market-entry test, its reporting trigger, and its one-and-done certificate vouch for a security that has quietly expired. The fault is in the landscape, not the product, so running the process more diligently cannot repair it. We map each mechanism to the force that strains or snaps it, and find the cure and the disease cut from the same cloth: because defenders and attackers wield the same AI, the only conformity that survives is one that never stops running. We also carry the remedy from proposal to proof on two CRA-scope robots, a humanoid and a lawn mower, where an agentic defender holds a line their undefended selves cannot. On the evidence already in hand, the CRA reaches full force in December 2027 certifying products against a world that has already changed. Static, human-paced security is finished; what replaces it must be continuous and agent-operated, and that is no longer a matter of taste.

</details>


### [106] [Evaluating Endpoint Detection Robustness Against Genetic Algorithm Driven Code Transformations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07191)
*Alvina Rwaichi Minja,Jema David Ndibwile*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: ShellForge is a genetic algorithm framework that evolves functionally equivalent post-compromise command variants to systematically evaluate AV/EDR detection robustness, revealing gaps in signature- and behavior-based pipelines under adaptive variation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern AV and EDR systems blend signature- and behavior-based detection, yet conventional evaluation pipelines struggle to reliably assess detection robustness when adversaries use adaptive variation. There is a need for systematic, reproducible benchmarks that stress-test endpoint detection under controlled variant generation.

Method: ShellForge employs a genetic algorithm with multi-objective fitness informed by AV/EDR detection feedback. It applies syntactic transformations, encoding schemes, and structural permutations to evolve post-compromise remote command execution variants while preserving functional equivalence. The framework is compared against baseline transformation tools under identical sandbox conditions.

Result: Measurable robustness gaps were identified in baseline signature- and behavior-oriented detection pipelines when subjected to ShellForge-generated variants. The controlled variant generation exposed limitations in both static signature matching and behavioral correlation mechanisms.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates the inadequacy of current detection pipelines against adaptively varied threats and proposes a reproducible benchmark for endpoint detection robustness evaluation. It advocates for robustness-aware defensive monitoring and improved behavioral correlation strategies.

Abstract: Post-compromise test variants are widely used in controlled security evaluation and endpoint robustness benchmarking. However, modern Antivirus (AV) and Endpoint Detection and Response (EDR) systems increasingly combine signature- and behavior-based detection, challenging the reliability of conventional detection pipelines under adaptive variation. This study introduces ShellForge, a Genetic Algorithm (GA)-driven framework that evolves post-compromise variants representative of remote command execution to generate functionally equivalent variants for systematic detection evaluation. ShellForge applies syntactic transformations, encoding schemes, and structural permutations guided by a multi-objective fitness function informed by AV and EDR detection feedback. We compare ShellForge against representative baseline transformation frameworks under identical sandbox configurations. Our findings highlight measurable robustness gaps in baseline signature- and behavior-oriented detection pipelines under controlled variant generation. In addition, we propose a reproducible benchmark for endpoint detection robustness evaluation, motivating the need for robustness-aware defensive monitoring and behavioral correlation.

</details>


### [107] [Continual Learning With Participation Privacy: An Auditable Buffering-Aggregation Recipe](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07209)
*T-H. Hubert Chan,Elaine Shi,Mengshi Zhao,Mingxun Zhou*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: Proposes a buffering wrapper that converts single-edit user streams (where one insertion/deletion shifts all subsequent updates) into Hamming-style per-bin update streams, coupled with a certification theorem for lifting non-adaptive DP proofs to adaptive inputs, achieving trajectory-level differential privacy with an explicit privacy-latency tradeoff via buffer size U.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Federated and streaming systems release intermediate models, requiring trajectory-level privacy under adaptive interaction. Standard Hamming-neighbor continual-release analyses fail under single-edit neighboring streams because one insertion/deletion shifts all subsequent updates. The authors aim to provide a modular, auditable framework for achieving DP guarantees in this challenging single-edit setting.

Method: Two-part approach: (1) A randomized buffering wrapper that emits bins of size [U, 2U], reducing single-edit streams to Hamming-style per-bin update streams with explicit backlog/delay guarantees, where U is calibrated by (ε,δ). (2) A certification theorem identifying when a non-adaptive Hamming-neighbor DP proof lifts to adaptive inputs, requiring fresh per-round randomness and a stable one-round privacy profile under common adaptive context. Standard primitives like tree prefix sums are used for the continual mechanism.

Result: Achieves trajectory-level (ε,δ)-DP for single-edit streams using standard primitives. Establishes an explicit privacy-latency link via the buffer size parameter U, providing a quantifiable tradeoff between privacy guarantees and delay.

Conclusion: The modular recipe of buffering plus certification provides an auditable, practical solution for trajectory-level DP in streaming/federated settings under the more realistic single-edit neighbor definition, bridging a gap left by standard Hamming-based analyses.

Abstract: Modern federated and streaming learning systems often release intermediate models, so privacy must hold for the full trajectory under adaptive interaction. Motivated by participation privacy, we study single-edit neighboring user streams, where one insertion/deletion shifts all subsequent updates and defeats standard Hamming-neighbor continual-release analyses. We give an auditable modular recipe. A randomized buffering wrapper emits bins of size $[U,2U]$, reducing single-edit streams to a Hamming-style per-bin update stream with explicit backlog/delay guarantees, where $U$ is calibrated by the privacy parameters $(\varepsilon,δ)$. We then prove a certification theorem identifying when a non-adaptive Hamming-neighbor DP proof for a continual primitive lifts to adaptive inputs: the primitive must use fresh per-round randomness and have a stable one-round privacy profile under common adaptive context. Together, these ingredients yield trajectory-level $(\varepsilon,δ)$-DP for single-edit streams using standard primitives (e.g., tree prefix sums), with an explicit privacy--latency link via $U$.

</details>


### [108] [Monitoring Vulnerabilities in Next-Generation Automotive Operating Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07226)
*Dimitri Simon,Badis Hammi,Joaquin Garcia-Alfaro,Hervé Debar*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: Presents a vulnerability assessment solution for software-defined vehicles (SDVs) that combines systematic CVE-based vulnerability discovery with a dockerized environment to evaluate exploitability risks, revealing both the breadth of threats and practical exploitation constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing vulnerability assessment tools inadequately address operating system vulnerabilities in SDVs, particularly failing to efficiently analyze diverse software stacks in realistic environments. This gap motivates a comprehensive security analysis focused on software vulnerabilities.

Method: The approach integrates systematic vulnerability discovery using public CVE databases with a dockerized development environment to simulate realistic software stacks and evaluate exploitability risks. The solution is publicly released.

Result: The analysis reveals a broad spectrum of potential threats across SDV software stacks, while also highlighting practical constraints encountered during exploitation. These findings expose the gap between theoretical vulnerability existence and real-world exploitability.

Conclusion: The paper discusses implications for both industry and research communities, and proposes future directions for building more resilient SDVs, emphasizing the need for practical, environment-aware vulnerability assessment approaches.

Abstract: Software-defined vehicles (SDVs) are revolutionizing transportation by integrating complex, interconnected hardware, and software systems. This evolution introduces significant security challenges. We present a comprehensive security analysis for SDVs, focusing on software vulnerabilities. We note that existing vulnerability assessment tools fall short in addressing operating systems vulnerabilities, particularly when it comes to efficiently analyzing diverse software stacks in realistic environments. We present and release a vulnerability assessment solution that efficiently addresses these limitations. Our approach combines systematic vulnerability discovery, leveraging public Common Vulnerabilities and Exposures (CVE) databases, within a dockerized development environment that evaluates exploitability risks. The results reveal both breadth of potential threats and the practical constraints we faced during exploitation. We discuss the implications for industry and research, and propose directions for building more resilient SDVs.

</details>


### [109] [MoLIFE: Methodology, Technologies, and Challenges for Mobile Live Intelligent Forensics Examination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07269)
*Silvia Lucia Sanna,Cristina Alcaraz,Alessandro Sanna,Giorgio Giacinto,Javier Lopez*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: MoLIFE is a novel mobile forensic methodology leveraging emerging technologies (AI, blockchain, quantum computing) for live data acquisition and analysis on mobile devices, designed to address increasing data protection barriers while supporting both real-time threat prevention and post-mortem analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mobile forensics faces growing challenges due to heightened data protection mechanisms (e.g., Android super-user restrictions, iOS security) that make physical acquisition risky —privilege escalation or custom ROM installation can alter device state and corrupt evidence. Simultaneously, mobile technology adoption and associated security threats are expanding rapidly, creating a gap that existing forensic approaches struggle to fill.

Method: The paper proposes MoLIFE (Mobile Live Intelligent Forensics Examination), a methodology grounded in the NIST SP800-101 framework for digital forensics. It is designed to integrate with emerging technologies such as AI, blockchain, and quantum computing. A proof-of-concept case study demonstrates Android data acquisition using the mDT (mobile Diagnostic Tool).

Result: The methodology is shown to be technically feasible through an Android case study using mDT-based acquisition. MoLIFE supports both proactive cyber threat prevention and reactive post-mortem forensic analysis. Its dependence on emerging technologies means current limitations are expected to diminish over time.

Conclusion: MoLIFE offers a forward-looking forensic framework that circumvents current mobile acquisition barriers by harnessing next-generation technologies. While presently constrained by the maturity of those technologies, the approach establishes good practices and is positioned to become more effective as AI, blockchain, and quantum computing evolve.

Abstract: Nowadays, mobile forensics is less explored in Digital Forensics case analysis due to the increase in data protection mechanisms implemented by tech companies (i.e., Google for Android and Apple for iOS). For example, the physical acquisition or analysis of specific directories under super-user protection would corrupt the evidence; access to such data is protected, and bypassing this protection requires either privilege escalation or custom ROM installation, leading to the modification of the device state. At the same time, the demand for mobile technologies and their respective communication systems is increasing exponentially, exposing numerous security threats and risks. For that reason, this paper presents a Mobile Live Intelligent Forensics Examination (MoLIFE), a novel Digital Forensics (DF) methodology for data acquisition and analysis of mobile devices. The proposed methodology is based on NIST SP800-101 for the DF process. MoLIFE can be integrated with new and emerging technologies by exploiting their power (e.g., AI, blockchain, quantum computing). MoLIFE can also be used to prevent cyber threats and incidents, as well as DF post-mortem analysis, offering examples of applying the MoLIFE methodology and good practices for the future. To prove the technical feasibility of the methodology, a small case study on Android devices data acquisition via the mDT will be presented. As the methodology is based on new and emerging technologies, it depends on their limitations that would be overcome in a few years.

</details>


### [110] [The AI Resilience Gap: Bringing Artificial Intelligence Inside the Operational Resilience Perimeter](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07359)
*Jonathan Shelby*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: Current AI governance frameworks (EU AI Act, NIST RMF, ISO 42001) focus on trustworthiness (safety, fairness, transparency) but neglect operational resilience. This paper identifies a structural gap between trustworthy AI and operational resilience, argues that UK financial regulators are already addressing this gap, and proposes an AI Resilience Framework that extends operational resilience practices—dependency mapping, impact tolerances, fallback doctrines, and concentration management—to AI systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The rapid adoption of AI in regulated firms has spurred governance focused on trustworthiness, yet this overlooks operational resilience: continuity of critical business services under disruption, substitutability of AI components, and concentration risk from reliance on a few frontier model providers. The authors argue a distinct resilience obligation exists that the trustworthy AI stack does not cover.

Method: The paper maps the two regulatory logics (trustworthy AI vs. operational resilience), identifies the structural gap between them, and analyzes how UK financial authorities (FPC, Critical Third Parties regime, joint statement on frontier AI and cyber resilience) are already closing this gap. It then proposes a regime-agnostic AI Resilience Framework with five components: dependency mapping, criticality-substitutability tiering, extension of impact tolerances to AI-specific failure modes, an explicit fallback doctrine, and provider-level concentration management.

Result: The analysis reveals a structural gap: trustworthy AI governance addresses model-level properties (fairness, safety, transparency) but does not ensure business service continuity when AI components fail. UK financial regulators are already bridging this gap through systemic analysis, third-party oversight, and resilience mandates. The proposed AI Resilience Framework provides an actionable route from governance policy to demonstrable resilience, usable by CISOs, security architects, and boards.

Conclusion: AI adoption creates a resilience obligation distinct from trustworthiness. The proposed AI Resilience Framework brings AI dependencies inside the operational resilience perimeter, complementing existing governance measures and extending cyber resilience practices into the AI domain. This work extends the authors' companion analysis of the UK cyber resilience regulatory stack.

Abstract: The rapid adoption of artificial intelligence across regulated firms has produced an extensive governance response oriented around trustworthiness: the EU AI Act, ISO IEC 42001, the NIST AI Risk Management Framework, and the United Kingdom's principles-based approach all address safety, fairness, transparency, and model risk. That response is necessary but incomplete. It does not, on its own, address operational resilience: the continuity of important business services under severe but plausible disruption, the substitutability of AI components, and the concentration of dependency on the small number of firms that supply frontier models. This paper argues that AI adoption creates a resilience obligation that is distinct from, and inadequately covered by, the trustworthy AI stack, and that United Kingdom financial authorities are already closing this gap through the Financial Policy Committee's systemic analysis, the Critical Third Parties regime, and the May 2026 joint statement on frontier AI and cyber resilience. We map the two regulatory logics, identify the structural gap between them, and propose the AI Resilience Framework: a regime-agnostic method for bringing AI dependencies inside the operational resilience perimeter through dependency mapping, a criticality-substitutability tiering, the extension of impact tolerances to AI-specific failure modes, an explicit fallback doctrine, and provider level concentration management. The framework gives chief information security officers, security architects, and boards an actionable route from AI governance policy to demonstrable resilience. This work extends a companion analysis of the United Kingdom cyber resilience regulatory stack into the artificial intelligence dimension.

</details>


### [111] [Unlearning to Protect: A Distilled Reinforcement Learning Framework with Privacy-Preserving Feature Unlearning and XAI for IoT Security](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07635)
*Md. Nahid Hasan,Golam Rabiul Alam*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: DiRLU is a lightweight reinforcement learning (A2C) framework for IoT botnet detection that uses knowledge distillation to create an efficient student model, incorporates post-hoc feature unlearning for GDPR compliance, and achieves 99.60% accuracy with only 2,370 FLOPS — 3.87× more efficient than state-of-the-art.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing AI-based botnet detection solutions are (i) too computationally heavy for resource-constrained IoT devices, and (ii) lack unlearning capabilities to remove sensitive or outdated features without full retraining. IoT devices also typically lack built-in botnet traffic filtering, leaving them highly exposed to DDoS, data theft, and service disruptions.

Method: The DiRLU framework employs: (1) Knowledge distillation from a strong teacher model to a lightweight student model, both trained using the Advantage Actor-Critic (A2C) reinforcement learning algorithm; (2) A post-hoc unlearning mechanism that modifies weights to remove targeted features while maintaining reversibility; (3) Training on 25% of the BoT-IoT dataset (vs. the typical 5%); (4) LIME-based Explainable AI integration for decision interpretability.

Result: The student model achieves 99.60% accuracy and 99.80% F1 score on the BoT-IoT dataset. DiRLU requires only 2,370 FLOPS, making it approximately 3.87× more efficient than the state-of-the-art model. The post-hoc unlearning mechanism successfully removes targeted features with negligible performance loss, and restored features show reversibility.

Conclusion: DiRLU demonstrates that lightweight RL-driven architectures with knowledge distillation can achieve state-of-the-art botnet detection accuracy while being efficient enough for edge/IoT deployment. The integrated unlearning mechanism provides GDPR-compliant privacy (right to be forgotten) without sacrificing performance, offering a practical and scalable IoT security solution.

Abstract: Botnets pose a significant cybersecurity threat, enabling attacks such as DDoS, data theft, and service disruptions on IoT devices. These devices often lack built-in botnet traffic filtering, leaving them highly exposed. Existing AI-based solutions improve detection capabilities but have limitations: (i) they are too heavy for IoT deployment, and (ii) they lack unlearning capabilities to forget sensitive or outdated features without retraining. To address these challenges, we propose DiRLU, a lightweight, reinforcement learning driven framework, while ensuring privacy by selectively unlearning sensitive or outdated features without requiring retraining. The framework leverages knowledge distillation to transfer knowledge from a teacher model into a lightweight student model, with both models trained using A2C. A post-hoc unlearning mechanism modifies weights to remove targeted features, while restored features show negligible performance loss, confirming reversibility.
