Unlike many benchmark models that used only 5% of the BoT-IoT dataset, this research leverages 25%, allowing us to develop a strong teacher model. Both the teacher and student models were trained using the A2C reinforcement learning algorithm, achieving impressive results, with the student model achieving 99.60% accuracy and a 99.80% F1 score. To enhance transparency, we integrated Explainable AI (XAI), particularly LIME, which helps interpret the model's decisions and identify the key features influencing its predictions. Moreover, DiRLU requires only 2,370 FLOPS, approximately 3.87x more efficient than the state-of-the-art model, highlighting its efficiency for edge deployment. DiRLU combines efficiency with privacy, aligning with GDPR standards (right to be forgotten) to provide practical and scalable IoT security solution.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [112] [AgentLens: Production-Assessed Trajectory Reviews for Coding Agent Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06624)
*Andrey Podivilov,Vadim Lomshakov,Sergey Savin,Matvei Startsev,Roman Pozharskiy,Maksim Parshin,Sergey Nikolenko*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AgentLens is a production-oriented benchmark for evaluating interactive code agents that goes beyond binary pass/fail metrics, instead assessing the full agent trajectory — including instruction following, tool use, self-verification, error recovery, and communication — using formal verification combined with LLM-written trajectory reviews and side-by-side comparisons.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing code-agent benchmarks reduce evaluation to a single bit (pass/fail), ignoring the rich, multi-dimensional quality of the agent's entire interaction trajectory. Real users experience how the agent follows instructions, uses tools, verifies work, recovers from errors, and communicates. There is a need for evaluation that captures this full experience to enable diagnosis, version comparison, and regression detection in production settings.

Method: AgentLens combines three evaluation approaches: (1) formal verification for tasks with objective correctness criteria, (2) LLM-written trajectory reviews that produce human-readable assessments of the agent's behavior throughout the run, and (3) side-by-side comparisons to rank or contrast different agent versions. This yields a scored trajectory with an interpretable explanation rather than a single bit.

Result: The benchmark proves useful beyond model ranking: it enables diagnosis of model behavior, comparison of successive agent versions, and catching product regressions in a nightly evaluation pipeline. The authors release the benchmark as open source.

Conclusion: AgentLens demonstrates that trajectory-level evaluation with interpretable feedback is both feasible and valuable for production code-agent development, offering a practical alternative to binary benchmarks for iterative improvement and regression monitoring.

Abstract: We present AgentLens, a production-assessed benchmark for interactive code agents. Most code-agent benchmarks reduce a run to a single bit -- did the task pass? -- but the people who actually use these agents experience the entire trajectory: how the agent follows instructions, uses its tools, verifies its own work, recovers from mistakes, and talks to them along the way. AgentLens evaluates that whole trajectory. It pairs formal verification, where an objective check exists, with LLM-written trajectory reviews and side-by-side comparisons, so that each run yields a readable explanation of why the score is what it is. This makes AgentLens useful for more than ranking models: we use it to diagnose model behavior, compare successive versions of our own agent, and catch product regressions in a nightly evaluation pipeline. We release the benchmark as open source at https://github.com/agent-lens/agent-lens-bench.

</details>


### [113] [LLM-powered reasoning in agent-based modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06757)
*Sifat Afroj Moon,Dakotah Maguire,Adam Spannaus,Joe Tuccillo,Maksudul Alam,Sudip K. Seal,John Gounley,Heidi Hanson*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HALE integrates large language models (LLMs) into agent-based modeling (ABM) to dynamically predict human decision-making, replacing static priors. The framework is demonstrated via a COVID-19 simulation in Salt Lake County, UT.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional ABMs rely on static priors for agent behavior, making them unable to adapt to real-time changes. LLMs offer a way to generate more realistic, adaptive human decision-making within simulations, addressing this information gap.

Method: The authors propose HALE (Hybrid Agent-based and Language-driven Epidemic), a scalable modeling framework that embeds LLMs into an ABM to predict agent decisions dynamically during simulation. Proof-of-concept is conducted by simulating COVID-19 spread and behavioral responses in Salt Lake County, Utah.

Result: The paper presents HALE as a proof-of-concept for COVID-19 simulation in Salt Lake County, demonstrating the feasibility of LLM-driven agent decision-making within an epidemiological ABM.

Conclusion: LLM-augmented ABMs like HALE represent a promising direction for adaptive, real-time policy modeling by enabling agents to exhibit more nuanced, context-sensitive decision-making grounded in natural language reasoning.

Abstract: Agent-based modeling (ABM) has the capability to model millions of individuals and their interactions, which is useful for policy making. However, ABMs have traditionally relied on static prior, which prevents the models from adapting to real-time changes. Our research provides a novel approach to addressing this information gap. Large language models (LLMs) offer new opportunities to predict human decision-making. Here, we introduce a scalable Hybrid Agent-based and Language-driven Epidemic (HALE) modeling framework that leverages LLMs to predict human decision-making in an ABM simulation. As a proof-of-concept, we use HALE to simulate COVID-19 and its effects in Salt Lake County, UT.

</details>


### [114] [When Does In-Context Search Help? A Sampling-Complexity Theory of Reflection-Driven Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06720)
*Yotam Wolf,Noam Wies,Amnon Shashua*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper provides a theoretical framework modeling in-context search in LLMs as approximate Bayesian inference over reasoning traces, showing that when reflections reliably localize early mistakes, exponential sample-complexity gains over parallel sampling are achievable; otherwise, no asymptotic benefit exists.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To formally understand when and why in-context search (iterative generate-critique-revise) in LLMs yields substantial improvements over naive parallel sampling, and to characterize the conditions under which such gains are attainable.

Method: The authors model in-context search as approximate posterior inference over reasoning traces, with the base LLM as a prior and self-reflection as feedback for posterior updates. They analyze inference-time sampling complexity, prove exponential gains when reflections reliably localize early mistakes (and no asymptotic benefit otherwise), show robustness to approximate posteriors, establish learnability via cross-entropy training on search rollouts with polynomial sample complexity, and connect the optimal policy to a posterior reweighting rule under a stagewise RL abstraction with verifiable rewards.

Result: Key results: (1) In-context search yields exponential improvements (polynomial attempts for problems with exponentially small zero-shot pass rates) iff reflections reliably localize early mistakes; (2) approximate posterior updates are sufficient; (3) cross-entropy training on search rollouts recovers the required behavior with polynomial sample complexity; (4) the optimal policy in stagewise RL with verifiable rewards implements the same posterior reweighting; (5) qualitative predictions are validated on real large reasoning models.

Conclusion: In-context search provides structured, theoretically grounded benefits over parallel sampling, but its efficacy critically depends on the reliability of self-reflection in localizing errors. The framework unifies inference-time search, training, and RL-based optimization under a common posterior-reweighting principle.

Abstract: Training large language models (LLMs) with extended reasoning has enabled in-context search, in which models iteratively generate, critique, and revise solution attempts. We provide a theoretical analysis of in-context search by modeling it as approximate inference over reasoning traces, where the base model defines a prior and self-reflection provides feedback for posterior updates, and study the resulting inference-time sampling complexity - the number of sequential attempts needed to achieve high success probability. We show that when reflections reliably localize early mistakes, in-context search can yield exponential improvements over the base model, solving problems with exponentially small zero-shot pass rates using only a polynomial number of sequential attempts, whereas when this property fails, conditioning on past attempts offers no asymptotic benefit over parallel sampling. We further show that these gains are robust and learnable: approximate posterior updates suffice, and cross-entropy training on search rollouts recovers the required behavior with polynomial sample complexity. Finally, we show that under a stagewise abstraction of reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards, the optimal policy extension implements the same posterior reweighting rule. We validate key qualitative predictions of the theory on real large reasoning models.

</details>


### [115] [Operational Reframing and Approval-Framed Delegation in Multi-Agent LLM Safety](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07097)
*Lifei Liu,Haoran Yu,Xiaochong Jiang,Su Wang,Pin Qian,Yihang Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Multi-agent LLM safety should not be assessed by a single "pipeline effect." The authors decompose this aggregate into three mechanisms — operational reframing, planner behavior, and delegation framing — using a five-condition controlled design, showing that each factor operates independently and that raw model rankings mispredict deployed behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing safety evaluations of multi-agent LLM systems report an aggregate "pipeline effect" (direct prompt vs. planner-executor), which conflates distinct mechanisms and is difficult to interpret. The authors argue this conflation prevents understanding which component actually drives safety failures.

Method: A five-condition controlled contrast design that isolates three mechanisms: (1) operational reframing — harmful intent disguised as legitimate tasks; (2) planner behavior — refusal or transformation of requests; and (3) approval-framed delegation — executor prompts implying prior approval. Evaluated on 30 synthetic harmful scenarios plus an external validation set from four agent-safety benchmarks, using LLM-judged compliance.

Result: Operational reframing consistently increases compliance across GPT, Gemini, and DeepSeek, while Claude resists it. Planner refusal can offset this risk, but when the planner produces executable steps, executor compliance may exceed the direct operational baseline. Approval-framed delegation is sensitive to prompt design, model pairing, and scenario source; a skeptical executor prompt sharply reduces compliance. Raw-direct rankings mispredict pipeline behavior: Gemini appears safest under direct prompts (8.9% compliance) yet rises to 38.9% with a Claude planner, while GPT's near-zero aggregate pipeline effect hides reframing-driven increases canceled by planner refusal.

Conclusion: Multi-agent safety evaluations should separately report reframing effects, planner behavior, delegation framing, and model pairing before attributing safety outcomes to architecture. Aggregate pipeline safety is not a stable architectural property.

Abstract: Safety evaluations of multi-agent LLM systems often compare a direct prompt with a planner-executor pipeline and report the difference as a single "pipeline effect." We argue that this aggregate is difficult to interpret because it conflates three mechanisms: harmful intent may be reframed as plausible operational work, the planner may refuse or transform the request, and the executor may act under delegation prompts implying prior approval. To separate these factors, we introduce a five-condition controlled contrast design, evaluated on 30 synthetic harmful scenarios and an exploratory external validation set from four agent-safety benchmarks using LLM-judged compliance.
  Our results show that aggregate pipeline safety is not a stable architectural property. Operational reframing is the most portable risk signal, increasing compliance for GPT, Gemini, and DeepSeek across both scenario sets, while Claude is comparatively resistant. Planner behavior can offset this risk mainly through refusal; however, when the planner produces executable steps, the executor may become more compliant than under the direct operational baseline. Approval-framed delegation is sensitive to prompt design, model pairing, and scenario source, and a skeptical executor prompt sharply reduces compliance.
  Raw-direct model rankings can also mispredict deployed planner-executor behavior. Gemini is safest under raw direct prompts in the primary set yet shows the largest amplification with a Claude planner, rising from 8.9 percent to 38.9 percent compliance. GPTs near-zero aggregate pipeline effect instead hides a reframing increase canceled by planner refusal. These findings suggest that multi-agent safety evaluations should report reframing, planner behavior, delegation framing, and model pairing separately before attributing failures to architecture itself.

</details>


### [116] [QANTIS: Hardware-Calibrated Sequential POMDP Belief Updates on IBM Heron](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06760)
*Bayram Yuksel Eker,Suayb S. Arslan,Ozgur Nazli,Mustafa Serhat Demirgil,Furkan Deligoz*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: QANTIS treats a quantum processor as a calibrated belief-update service for POMDPs, returning posteriors to a classical planner. A hardware case study on IBM Heron shows that all-step fixed-point amplitude amplification (FPAA) preserves the Tiger POMDP posterior across up to 32-step horizons, with hardware posteriors and exact Bayes posteriors selecting the same immediate action in every reported decision check. Boundary-aware BIQAE stabilizes amplitude estimation near 0/1, and a rare-event sweep maps the sample-complexity envelope for one-in-a-million evidence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous systems under partial observability must maintain beliefs over hidden states, and computing exact Bayesian posteriors becomes intractable for rare-event evidence. The authors investigate whether a quantum processor can serve as a reusable belief-update primitive across sequential POMDP horizons without corrupting the posterior, targeting a practical operating envelope rather than claiming quantum advantage.

Method: The study uses the Tiger POMDP as a benchmark, running on IBM Heron hardware. It compares three amplitude estimation strategies: no amplification, guarded Grover amplification (applied only when evidence is not rare), and all-step fixed-point amplitude amplification (FPAA). Boundary-aware BIQAE handles estimation near probability boundaries (0 and 1). A rare-event sweep characterizes the logical sample-complexity envelope. The key metric is whether the hardware posterior changes the downstream action compared to exact Bayes.

Result: All-step FPAA preserves the Tiger posterior across 8-step, 12-step (primary runs) and 20-step, 32-step (controls) horizons, remaining within the same operating band. In every reported decision check, the hardware posterior and the exact Bayes posterior select the same immediate action. Boundary-aware BIQAE stabilizes amplitude estimation near 0 and 1. The rare-event sweep maps the sample-complexity envelope for evidence as rare as one-in-a-million.

Conclusion: The work establishes an operating envelope for a hardware-calibrated quantum belief-update primitive rather than making a standalone hardware-advantage claim. The results demonstrate that quantum processors can serve as reliable belief-update services within classical POMDP planning loops on current NISQ hardware, provided appropriate amplitude estimation strategies are used.

Abstract: Autonomous systems under partial observability act on beliefs, not raw sensor events. QANTIS treats the quantum processor as a calibrated belief-update service in that loop: it receives a prior and an observation model, estimates the rare-event evidence term, and returns an ordinary posterior to a classical planner. This paper asks whether that service can be reused across a sequential Tiger POMDP horizon on present IBM Heron hardware without corrupting the planner-facing posterior. We answer with a controlled hardware case study rather than an end-to-end autonomy or wall-clock speedup claim. The study compares no amplification, guarded Grover amplification, and all-step fixed-point amplification on the same trajectory, then checks whether the returned posterior would change the downstream action. All-step FPAA preserves the Tiger posterior across the reported 8-step and 12-step primary runs, and the 20-step and 32-step controls remain inside the same operating band. In every reported decision check, the hardware posterior and the exact Bayes posterior select the same immediate action. Boundary-aware BIQAE stabilizes amplitude estimation near zero and near one, while a rare-event sweep maps the logical sample-complexity envelope for one-in-a-million evidence. The result is an operating envelope for a hardware-calibrated belief-update primitive, not a standalone hardware-advantage claim.

</details>


### [117] [From Atomic Actions to Standard Operating Procedures: Iterative Tool Optimization for Self-Evolving LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07321)
*Haipeng Ding,Yuexiang Xie,Zhewei Wei,Yaliang Li,Bolin Ding*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EvoSOP enables LLM agents to self-evolve by synthesizing atomic actions into reusable Standard Operating Procedures (SOPs) — higher-order callable tools — and iteratively optimizes them through construction, merging, evaluation, and pruning, significantly boosting task success rates while reducing interaction rounds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing agent frameworks rely on static toolsets of granular atomic actions (e.g., basic file I/O, single-turn search), forcing agents to repeatedly reinvent low-level logic for recurring workflows. This leads to excessive reasoning overhead and elevated failure rates. The authors propose that agents need a mechanism for self-evolution through tool abstraction.

Method: EvoSOP, a framework that extracts Standard Operating Procedures (SOPs) — multi-step, callable higher-order tools — from execution trajectories. The framework implements a systematic lifecycle: (1) Construction of SOPs from successful trajectories, (2) Merging of redundant or overlapping SOPs, (3) Evaluation of SOP utility and effectiveness, and (4) Pruning of low-quality or obsolete SOPs, enabling iterative toolset optimization.

Result: Extensive experiments show EvoSOP significantly boosts task success rates while substantially reducing the number of interaction rounds compared to baselines. The iterative tool optimization process fosters reliable and efficient tool-use patterns.

Conclusion: Self-evolution through SOP synthesis and iterative toolset refinement provides a scalable pathway for developing more capable, efficient, and reliable LLM agents, moving beyond static atomic tool paradigms.

Abstract: Tool utilization enables Large Language Model (LLM) agents to interact with the real world and resolve complex tasks. However, existing agent frameworks predominantly rely on static toolsets composed of granular atomic actions (e.g., basic file I/O or single-turn search), which forces agents to reinvent low-level logic for every recurring workflow, leading to increased reasoning overhead and failure rates. In this study, we propose that agents can achieve self-evolution by synthesizing these atomic actions into reusable Standard Operating Procedures (SOPs), which function as callable higher-order tools that encapsulate multi-step logic. We further introduce EvoSOP, a framework that empowers agents to extract SOPs from execution trajectories and iteratively optimize the toolset through a systematic lifecycle of construction, merging, evaluation, and pruning. Extensive experiments demonstrate that EvoSOP significantly boosts task success rates while substantially reducing the number of interaction rounds compared to baselines. Our analysis also reveals that iterative tool optimization fosters reliable and efficient tool-use patterns, providing a scalable pathway for the development of self-evolving agents.

</details>


### [118] [InductWave: Inductive Multi-Hop Logical Query Answering on Knowledge Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07422)
*Mayank Kharbanda,Michael Cochez,Rajiv Ratn Shah,Raghava Mutharaju*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: InductWave is a wavelet-based inductive embedding method for logical multi-hop query answering on large knowledge graphs, capable of reasoning over unseen entities with fewer message-passing layers while outperforming state-of-the-art models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing logical query answering methods rely on transductive reasoning, which fails on unseen entities. Real-world KGs are massive, making it infeasible to train on all nodes. An inductive method that generalizes to unseen entities with lower resource requirements is needed.

Method: Proposes InductWave, a wavelet-based inductive embedding approach. Uses fewer message-passing layers (50-75% of baseline layers) to learn entity representations that generalize from a smaller training graph to a larger test graph with unseen nodes.

Result: InductWave performs on par with baselines using half the message-passing layers and outperforms all baselines in most cases using 75% of the layers. Evaluated extensively on FB15k-237 with varying train-test graph proportions and on massive graphs like Wiki-KG.

Conclusion: InductWave enables efficient and scalable logical query answering over large KGs with strong inductive generalization, reducing computational overhead while maintaining or exceeding state-of-the-art performance.

Abstract: Logical Multi-Hop Query Answering over Knowledge Graphs (KGs) can be formulated as querying, with an implicit completeness assumption. Current works mainly focus on Existential First Order Logic (EFO) queries. These EFO queries contain conjunction, disjunction, and negation operators. Most existing works employ transductive reasoning, meaning they are not capable of reasoning over entities unseen during training. In the real world, there is a resource scarcity, and we cannot train a model with all the nodes of a large KG. Hence, we propose InductWave, a wavelet-based inductive embedding method for logical query answering on large KGs. Here, the training graph consists of fewer nodes than the test graph. Our model performs on par with the baseline models while having half the number of message-passing layers. It outperforms all of them in most cases, with 75% of the layers. These fewer resource requirements enable us to evaluate InductWave on massive graphs, such as Wiki-KG. We test our model using extensive experiments across varying train-test graph proportions of the FB15k-(237) dataset, comparing it with the state-of-the-art models. The code and datasets for the model are available at https://github.com/kracr/inductwave/.

</details>


### [119] [Cost-Effective Agent Harnesses for Abstract Reasoning and Generalization on ARC-AGI-1](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06764)
*Kabir Moghe,Peter Chin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A two-stage agent pipeline (Explorer-Definer) and a Reflective Orchestrator are built atop DeepSeek V3.2 (non-thinking, no ARC fine-tuning) for ARC-AGI-1. The pipeline achieves 57.50% pass@2 at $0.25/task; the orchestrator reaches 67.25% pass@2 at $0.62/task, lifting a 15.50% one-shot baseline by ~52 points. Analysis shows generation-bound (not selection-bound) performance and the think tool is critical.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing ARC-AGI-1 approaches rely on either heavy test-time compute (evolutionary search, exhaustive sampling, long CoT) or benchmark-specific fine-tuning. The authors explore a third regime: what performance can be recovered through agentic architecture alone, using an open-weight non-thinking model under a strict budget with no ARC-specific training.

Method: Two architectures are introduced: (1) Explorer-Definer Pipeline — a two-stage agent pipeline that explicitly separates pattern discovery from executable transformation synthesis; (2) Reflective Orchestrator — augments the pipeline with autonomous re-exploration of new transformations when prior hypotheses fail on training pairs. Analysis includes unbiased pass@k diagnostics to distinguish generation-bound vs. selection-bound bottlenecks, plus an ablation on the think tool.

Result: On the ARC-AGI-1 public 400-task set: Pipeline reaches 57.50% pass@2 ($0.25/task); Orchestrator reaches 67.25% pass@2 ($0.62/task). Combined lift over one-shot baseline (15.50%) is ~52 points. Unbiased pass@k analysis reveals ~95% of candidate ceiling is captured by training-pair accuracy selection (generation-bound, not selection-bound). Orchestrator's adaptive re-exploration confirms this: unbiased pass@1 lift +9.81pp, matching selection-mediated pass@2 lift. Think tool ablation: removal reduces pass@2 by 5.75pp.

Conclusion: Architecture alone — decomposing pattern discovery and program synthesis into explicit agentic stages with reflective orchestration — can recover substantial ARC-AGI-1 performance without benchmark-specific training or heavy compute. The system is generation-bound, meaning future gains require broader hypothesis generation rather than better ranking. The Reflective Orchestrator validates this by implementing adaptive re-exploration.

Abstract: Recent progress on ARC-AGI-1 from disclosed architectures has come broadly from two regimes: heavy test-time compute over frontier models (evolutionary search, exhaustive sampling, extended chain-of-thought), or benchmark-specific training in which small models are fine-tuned on ARC data, often with task-specialized architectures. We study a third regime: an open-weight model in non-thinking mode (DeepSeek V3.2) under a strict budget, with no ARC-specific fine-tuning. We study what is recoverable through architecture alone, building agentic harnesses that decompose pattern-discovery and program-synthesis stages explicitly. First, we introduce an Explorer-Definer Pipeline that separates pattern discovery from executable transformation synthesis, implemented as a two-stage agent pipeline. Next, we present the Reflective Orchestrator, which augments the pipeline with autonomous exploration of new transformations when previous hypotheses fail on training pairs. On the ARC-AGI-1 public 400-task evaluation set, the pipeline reaches 57.50% pass@2 at \$0.25 per task, and the orchestrator reaches 67.25% pass@2 at \$0.62 per task. Together these architectures lift a 15.50% one-shot baseline by ~52 points without benchmark-specific training or heavy test-time compute. Furthermore, the orchestrator-driven lift tests a falsifiable diagnostic the pipeline produces; unbiased pass@k analysis suggests the pipeline is generation-bound, not selection-bound (selection via training-pair accuracy captures ~95% of the candidate ceiling) and predicts that significant improvement requires broader generation, not better ranking. The orchestrator implements this prediction via adaptive re-exploration and confirms it (unbiased pass@1 lift +9.81 pp, matching selection-mediated pass@2 lift). An additional pipeline ablation identifies its think tool as a significant component, with removal reducing pass@2 by 5.75 pp.

</details>


### [120] [Measuring Intelligence Beyond Human Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07040)
*Jerry Han,Rafael Moschopoulos,Ella Colby,Vishrut Goyal,Andrew Tu,Kia Ghods,Mark Braverman,Elad Hazan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes a relative, adversarial evaluation paradigm where models generate challenges to differentiate other systems, replacing saturated absolute benchmarks. This yields a scalable psychometric rating system with protocols to resist gaming, enable judge-free adjudication, and measure capabilities beyond the human frontier.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Human-authored benchmarks inevitably saturate as AI capabilities advance beyond the human frontier. Beyond that point, human examiners cannot reliably identify tasks that are both hard and verifiable, creating a fundamental limitation in absolute-scale evaluation. A new paradigm is needed that scales with the systems being measured.

Method: The authors propose an adversarial psychometric rating framework based on relative measurement: models generate public challenges designed to separate other systems, and outcomes are aggregated into ratings (e.g., Elo-style). This is instantiated across both verifiable domains (where correctness is automatically checkable) and open-ended, non-verifiable domains. Practical protocols are introduced to mitigate private-information attacks, support judge-free adjudication, and ensure the system scales naturally with advancing agent capabilities.

Result: The framework yields a self-scaling evaluation system where model-generated challenges can continue measuring capabilities beyond the human frontier, without requiring human examiners to design tasks at superhuman difficulty levels.

Conclusion: Relative, adversarial measurement via model-generated challenges offers a principled solution to the saturation problem in AI evaluation, enabling ongoing assessment of systems that exceed human capabilities.

Abstract: How can we measure intelligence beyond human capability?
  Human-authored benchmarks saturate, and above human capability, examiners may not know which tasks are both hard and verifiable. We argue that this difficulty is inherent to absolute-scale evaluation and propose a new paradigm based on relative measurement in which models generate public challenges that separate other systems. Aggregating these outcomes yields an adversarial psychometric rating system that can scale with the systems being measured. We describe practical protocols that reduce incentives for private-information attacks, support judge-free adjudication, and naturally scale with agent capabilities. We instantiate the framework across verifiable and open-ended, non-verifiable domains, illustrating how model-generated evaluation can continue to measure systems beyond the human frontier.

</details>


### [121] [Physics-Audited Agentic Discovery in Scientific Machine Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07379)
*Diab W. Abueidda,Bilal Ahmed,Panos Pantidis,Mostafa E. Mobasher*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PA-SciML introduces a verification-first workflow for agentic scientific ML that audits candidate surrogate models against physics requirements (boundary conditions, superposition, causality, etc.) before selection, rather than relying solely on error metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Low validation error does not guarantee that predicted fields satisfy essential physics constraints (e.g., boundary conditions, superposition, stiffness scaling, causality). Existing agentic SciML pipelines lack physics auditing, risking selection of surrogates that violate mechanics principles.

Method: PA-SciML fixes a scoring evaluator before search, derives machine-checkable physics requirements, audits each trained candidate on its outputs, searches input ranges for high-violation cases without reference solutions, and reports a surrogate as verified only under stated checks. Optional features include advisory numerical probes before training and isolating modeling changes to attribute score gains.

Result: In static elasticity, PA-SciML selects a surrogate with lower validation error than the error-only baseline while both pass linear-elastic checks. In transient elastodynamics, the error-only baseline violates causality (responding to future loading history) despite similar mean error, while the PA-SciML surrogate passes all stated checks.

Conclusion: The key differentiator is per-candidate physics evidence on predicted fields rather than a richer aggregate score. Physics auditing catches failures invisible to error metrics alone, particularly causality violations in dynamics problems.

Abstract: In agentic scientific machine learning (SciML), large language model (LLM) agents can discover surrogate models and select one by an automated score, typically an error metric. A low error, however, does not establish that the predicted fields satisfy the physics that matter for mechanics, such as boundary conditions, superposition, stiffness scaling, or causality. We introduce Physics-Audited Agentic SciML (PA-SciML), a verification-first workflow for agentic SciML discovery. The workflow fixes a scoring evaluator before search, derives reviewable machine-checkable physics requirements, checks each trained candidate on its outputs, and separately searches prescribed input ranges or measured load-history spans for high-violation cases without reference solution fields. A surrogate is reported as verified only under the stated checks. When enabled, the workflow also adds advisory numerical probes before training and tests one modeling change at a time to record which isolated edits are associated with score gains before reuse. In the reported computational-solid-mechanics numerical examples, the static elasticity run selects a surrogate with lower validation error than the error-only baseline while both selected models pass the common linear-elastic checks. In the transient elastodynamics run, an error-only baseline with similar mean error fails a stricter causality check by responding to future parts of the loading history, while the selected surrogate passes the stated checks. The main distinction is per-candidate physics evidence on predicted fields, not a richer aggregate score.

</details>


### [122] [MIRA-Math: A Benchmark for Minimal Information Requesting and Mathematical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07391)
*Charbel Al Bateh,Samer Saab*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MIRA-Math is a benchmark for testing whether LLMs can identify and request exactly one missing atomic fact needed to solve a math problem, then integrate the returned fact to produce the correct answer. It features 2,310 instances across 22 mathematical families with deterministic validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing math benchmarks either provide all necessary facts or conflate reasoning with tool use and long-horizon dialogue. MIRA-Math isolates a narrow diagnostic capability: recognizing precisely which single piece of information is missing, requesting it efficiently, and using it correctly.

Method: The benchmark generates problems where exactly one atomic fact is withheld from the solver. A solver LLM must request that fact in natural language under a budget constraint. A fixed constrained responder LLM checks whether the request matches the canonical fact and either returns it verbatim or declines. Instance generation, validation, and answer verification are fully deterministic. The dataset spans 22 mathematical families (algebra, probability, linear systems, discrete structures, signal processing, Markov chains, circuits, interpolation, numerical boundary-value problems).

Result: Experiments across frontier and small models show that request success and final-answer accuracy are separable phenomena: models may request the right fact but fail downstream computation, or fail to obtain the canonical hint entirely, revealing distinct failure modes.

Conclusion: MIRA-Math provides a clean, reproducible testbed for minimal information requesting in mathematical reasoning. The authors release generators, verifiers, prompts, run metadata, and documentation to support further research.

Abstract: Mathematical reasoning benchmarks typically provide all facts needed to solve each problem, while interactive benchmarks often mix reasoning with tools, retrieval, and long-horizon dialogue. We introduce MIRA-Math, a benchmark for a narrower diagnostic capability: solving mathematical problems whose full latent state has a unique answer, but whose solver-facing view is missing exactly one necessary atomic fact. The solver must request the missing information in natural language under a strict budget and then integrate the returned fact into an exact final answer. A fixed constrained LLM responder sees only the dataset-provided atomic fact and must either offer the quoted fact when the request matches it, or decline otherwise. Thus, instance generation, typed hint specifications, validation, and final-answer verification are deterministic, while request metrics are measured under a fixed LLM-mediated responder channel. MIRA-Math contains 2{,}310 generated instances from 22 typed mathematical families spanning algebra, probability, linear systems, discrete structures, signal processing, Markov chains, circuits, interpolation, and numerical boundary-value problems. Experiments across frontier and small models show that request success and final-answer accuracy are separable: models may ask for the right fact yet fail the downstream computation, or fail before obtaining the canonical hint. We release generators, verifiers, prompts, run metadata, and dataset documentation to support reproducible evaluation of minimal information requesting in mathematical reasoning.

</details>


### [123] [Agentic Data Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07397)
*Elaine Ang,Chenxi Huang,Georgios Liargkovas,Jerry Liu,Jinhui Liu,Nikos Pagonas,Charlie Summers,Haonan Wang,Jiakai Xu,Tianle Zhou,Yusen Zhang,Zhou Yu,Zhuo Zhang,Tianyi Peng,Kostis Kaffes,Eugene Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This talk abstract argues that to safely harness autonomous agents, we must redesign the data environments they operate in — termed "Agentic Data Environments" — shifting databases from passive state stores into active execution substrates that both augment agent capabilities and enforce safety bounds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous agents offer major gains in speed, scale, and labor efficiency, but their failures can cause abrupt and often irreversible harm. The core tension is maximizing automation benefits while containing failure costs. Current data infrastructure (databases, files, APIs) is passive and wasn't designed for agentic execution, creating a safety gap.

Method: Proposes "Agentic Data Environments" — a reframed execution substrate for agents that spans databases, files, APIs, applications, and system state. These environments are designed to both amplify agent capabilities and enforce safety guarantees at the infrastructure level, rather than treating safety as an afterthought.

Result: This is a talk abstract outlining early-stage work; specific empirical results are not presented. The contribution is a conceptual reframing of data systems from passive state stores into active, safety-enforcing execution substrates for autonomous agents.

Conclusion: The path to safe agentic automation lies in re-architecting the data layer itself. By making data systems active participants in safe execution rather than passive repositories, we can simultaneously unlock greater agent capabilities while providing hard safety guarantees against irreversible failure modes.

Abstract: Autonomous agents promise substantial gains in speed, scale, and labor efficiency, but their failures can impose abrupt and often irreversible costs. The central challenge for agentic automation is therefore to increase the benefits of automation while bounding the consequences of failure.
  While databases remain central to modern computing, agents operate over a broader data environment spanning files, APIs, applications, and system state. In this talk, I will outline early work on Agentic Data Environments -- the execution substrate in which agents operate -- that both amplify agent capabilities and enforce safety guarantees. This perspective reframes data systems from passive stores of state into active substrates for safe, reliable execution.

</details>


### [124] [Reason Less, Verify More: Deterministic Gates Recover a Silent Policy-Violation Failure Mode in Tool-Using LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07405)
*Vikas Reddy,Sumanth Reddy Challaram,Abhishek Basu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM agents in policy-permissive environments produce silent policy violations that go undetected. The authors propose deterministic pre-execution gates that inspect tool calls against state before allowing writes, raising task success by ~12pp on the τ²-bench airline domain, with gains concentrated on tasks where gates actually fire.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents deployed with tool access can silently violate domain policies while appearing to succeed, because tools in policy-permissive environments execute well-formed calls regardless of whether the resulting state transition is legal. These "silent wrong-state failures" are invisible to both tool error reporting and agent self-assessment, creating a dangerous failure mode for deployed agents.

Method: The authors characterize silent wrong-state failures in the τ²-bench airline domain. They then introduce deterministic, read-only pre-execution gates: lightweight modules that inspect the proposed tool call and current state before permitting a write operation. A suite of four such gates is evaluated on gpt-4o-mini, with paired task-level bootstrap significance testing. Two negative controls (a self-enforcing retail domain and BFCL) bound the mechanism's applicability. A small-scale frontier test with gpt-5.2 is also conducted.

Result: On a budget agent (gpt-4o-mini), 78% of failures are silent wrong-state failures. The four-gate suite improves full-benchmark success from 29.6% to 42.0% (+12.4pp; P=0.0012), replicated on a disjoint 15-seed set (+12.3pp; P=0.0008). Gains concentrate on the 26/50 tasks where gates fire (+19.2pp), with no significant movement on non-firing tasks. Negative controls confirm gates help only when tools are policy-permissive. At the frontier (gpt-5.2), the same pattern holds: 61.2% → 71.6% (+10.4pp; P=0.020).

Conclusion: Deterministic pre-execution gates do not guarantee task success, but they can deterministically prevent a known class of silent policy-violating writes at the action boundary. The failure mode persists even at the frontier, and the intervention is bounded — effective where tools are policy-permissive, adding little where tools already self-enforce.

Abstract: Tool-using LLM agents can violate the very policies they are deployed to enforce while appearing to complete the task successfully. In policy-permissive environments, a tool may execute any well-formed call even when the corresponding state transition is forbidden by domain policy. The result is a silent wrong state (a booking cancelled, a passenger count changed, a claim acted on without verification) that neither the tool nor the agent's self-report exposes.
  We study this failure mode in the $τ^2$-bench airline domain. On a budget agent, 78% of observed failures are silent wrong-state failures with no tool error, and the aggregate failure rate is reproducible across disjoint seeds, not sampling noise. We then evaluate a lightweight intervention: deterministic, read-only pre-execution gates that inspect the proposed call and current state before allowing a write. A four-gate suite raises full-benchmark success from 29.6% to 42.0% on gpt-4o-mini (+12.4pp; paired task-level bootstrap P=0.0012), and the lift reproduces on a disjoint 15-seed set (+12.3pp; P=0.0008).
  The effect is concentrated where the gates fire: on the 26/50 firing tasks, success rises by +19.2pp, while movement on the 24 non-firing tasks does not exclude zero. Two negative controls (a self-enforcing retail domain and BFCL) bound the mechanism: gates help when tools are policy-permissive and add little where tools already self-enforce. As suggestive evidence, not a central claim, the same failure mode persists at the frontier: gpt-5.2 at default reasoning still attempts policy-violating writes, and the same suite improves success from 61.2% to 71.6% (+10.4pp; P=0.020; n=5, no replication). The contribution is a bounded evaluation and reliability result: deterministic gates do not guarantee task success, but they can deterministically prevent a known class of silent policy-violating writes at the action boundary.

</details>


### [125] [The Blind Curator: How a Biased Judge Silently Disables Skill Retirement in Self-Evolving Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07436)
*Xing Zhang,Yanwei Cui,Guanghui Wang,Ziyuan Li,Wei Qiu,Bing Zhu,Peiyang He*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: When LLM judges used for reference-free evaluation have false-pass bias (failures misclassified as passes), a self-evolving agent's skill-retirement mechanism silently breaks past a sharp threshold, yet this failure remains invisible in aggregate metrics. The authors provide a corrupted-reward analysis, behavioral evidence across domains, and a cheap pre-deployment audit to detect the vulnerability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Self-evolving agents rely on skill retirement — discarding underperforming skills — to prevent their growing skill library from degrading below baseline. Prior guarantees assume unbiased reward signals, but reference-free tasks increasingly depend on LLM judges whose biases are unknown and potentially systematic. The paper investigates what happens when the judge evaluating skill failures is itself fallible in a biased way.

Method: The authors combine (1) a corrupted-reward theoretical analysis to characterize how bias interacts with retirement mechanisms, (2) a causal behavioral study that injects controlled corruption atop a deterministic reward signal on a reference-free report-writing testbed with code-generation cross-validation, and (3) a defect-injection audit procedure that operators can run pre-deployment to determine which side of the failure threshold their judge occupies.

Result: Symmetric noise leaves the retirement mechanism intact. However, false-pass bias — failures erroneously judged as passes — disables contribution-based retirement beyond a sharp threshold that cannot be overcome by additional data. This mechanism failure is universal across domains and failure rates, sparing only near-zero-false-pass graders (e.g., verifiers). Downstream eval quality degrades only when the same corruption also starves skill synthesis; otherwise, the disabled curator remains silent in all aggregate metrics. The separation of genuine retirement from cap-eviction churn confirms the mechanism, not just the outcome, is broken.

Conclusion: The work constitutes a behavioral safety result rather than a performance claim: biased judges can silently disable skill retirement without detection through standard metrics. The proposed defect-injection audit offers a practical, low-cost pre-deployment check to identify unsafe judge bias before operational use.

Abstract: A self-evolving agent retires its bad skills by watching them fail, so what happens when the judge cannot see the failures? Skill retirement is the structural constraint that keeps a growing library from drifting below the no-skill baseline, but its guarantee assumes an unbiased reward, which is false for the LLM judges that reference-free tasks force upon us. We show that a biased judge does not merely add noise; it \emph{silently switches off the curator}. We make this precise with a corrupted-reward analysis and, isolating the causal channel by injecting corruption on top of a deterministic reward, a behavioral study on a reference-free report-writing testbed with a code-generation cross-check. Symmetric noise leaves retirement intact, but \emph{false-pass} bias (failures slipping through as passes) disables contribution-based retirement past a sharp threshold that no amount of data can cross. Separating genuine retirement from cap-eviction churn shows this \emph{mechanism} failure is universal, holding across domains and failure rates and sparing only near-zero-false-pass, verifier-like graders. The downstream \emph{outcome}, though, is regime-dependent: eval quality degrades only where the same corruption also starves skill synthesis, and otherwise holds steady, so the disabled curator is \emph{silent}, surfacing in no aggregate metric. The contribution is a behavioral safety result, not a performance one. A cheap defect-injection audit then tells an operator, before deployment, which side of the threshold their judge occupies.

</details>


### [126] [SpaCellAgent: A Self-Evolving LLM-Based Multi-Agent Framework for Trajectory Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07467)
*Songhan Wang,Haoang Chi,He Li,Zhiheng Zhang,Jiayan Yuan,Cheems Wang,Hao Peng,Xinwang Liu,Wenjing Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SpaCellAgent is an LLM-based multi-agent framework that automates end-to-end trajectory inference analysis for spatial and single-cell transcriptomics, achieving over 40% efficiency improvement while maintaining expert-level performance across six heterogeneous datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing trajectory inference methods for spatial and single-cell transcriptomics require extensive manual intervention and proficiency in heterogeneous tools, creating a significant barrier to efficient analysis. There is a need for an automated, accessible solution that democratizes these advanced analyses.

Method: SpaCellAgent employs a multi-agent LLM architecture with three core components: (1) a strategic workflow planning module that decomposes natural language specifications into analytical steps, (2) a dynamic tool-orchestration engine for adaptive algorithm selection across heterogeneous tools, and (3) a self-evolution module that iteratively refines performance through feedback loops.

Result: Evaluated on six heterogeneous datasets spanning complex temporal developmental trajectories, diverse sequencing platforms, and spatially-resolved tissue architectures, SpaCellAgent demonstrated over 40% improvement in analytical efficiency while maintaining performance aligned with human experts.

Conclusion: SpaCellAgent democratizes advanced spatiotemporal modeling by converting natural language into optimized analytical workflows, establishing a scalable, agent-driven paradigm for computational biology. Code is publicly available on GitHub.

Abstract: Spatial and Single-cell transcriptomics are transformative in deciphering cellular dynamics. As the fundamental paradigm for reconstructing cell developmental paths, trajectory inference (TI) is critical. However, existing methods require extensive manual intervention and proficiency in heterogeneous tools, posing a significant barrier to efficient TI analysis. To bridge this gap, we propose SpaCellAgent, an autonomous large language model (LLM) multi-agent framework that automates end-to-end spatiotemporal analysis and narrative generation. SpaCellAgent utilizes a multi-agent architecture for strategic workflow planning, a dynamic tool-orchestration engine for adaptive algorithm selection, and a self-evolution module that iteratively refines performance through feedback. We evaluate SpaCellAgent on six heterogeneous datasets encompassing complex temporal developmental trajectories, diverse sequencing platforms, and spatially-resolved tissue architectures. SpaCellAgent consistently demonstrates over 40\% improvement in analytical efficiency while maintaining expert-aligned performance. By converting natural language specifications into optimized analytical workflows and fully automating the pipeline, SpaCellAgent democratizes advanced spatiotemporal modeling and establishes a scalable, agent-driven paradigm for computational biology. The code and materials are available at https://github.com/LittleXH-shw/SpaCellAgent.

</details>


### [127] [Search, Fail, Recover: A Training Framework for Correction-Aware Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07492)
*Dmitry Beresnev,Vladimir Makharev,Roman Khalikov,Ivan Oseledets,Petr Anokhin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Pyligent frames reasoning as validated search over partial solution chains, training models to continue, finish, or backtrack (with failure summaries). By converting failed search branches into supervised targets, it substantially outperforms gold-only SFT on tasks requiring delayed-failure recovery, including hidden graphs, Sudoku, and Blocksworld.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many reasoning tasks cannot be adequately solved by a single left-to-right chain. Solvers need to explore plausible branches, recognize delayed failure, and backtrack to the last viable prefix. Standard supervised fine-tuning on polished gold solutions fails to teach this recovery behavior, motivating a framework that explicitly supervises failed-branch exploration and backtracking.

Method: Pyligent is a training and inference framework built on the Diligent Learner formulation. It uses a task validator to label generated continuations and failures, producing search trees that are converted into supervised targets for three actions: **continue**, **finish**, and **backtrack** (with optional traces summarizing abandoned branches). During inference, the model performs validated search guided by these learned behaviors.

Result: Compared to gold-only SFT, Pyligent improves solve rates by: **+72.7 pp** on hidden directed graph tasks; **+17/18 pp** on mixed/expert Sudoku; **+27/14 pp** on mixed/expert Sudoku with reasoning traces; and **+13 pp** on Blocksworld.

Conclusion: Explicit supervision on failed branches teaches useful recovery behavior that imitation of polished solution chains cannot capture. This is particularly impactful in domains where delayed-failure signals are critical, and the approach generalizes across multiple structured reasoning tasks with exact validators.

Abstract: Many reasoning tasks are not well described by a single left-to-right chain: a solver may need to pursue a plausible branch, observe delayed failure, and return to the latest prefix that can still be completed. We introduce Pyligent, a training and inference framework inspired by the Diligent Learner formulation that represents reasoning as validated search over partial solution chains. A task validator labels generated continuations and failures, and the resulting search trees are converted into supervised targets for three actions: continue, finish, and backtrack, with optional traces that summarize abandoned branches. We evaluate Pyligent on a hidden directed graph task designed to isolate delayed-failure recovery, and on structured reasoning domains with exact validators, including $4{\times}4$ Sudoku, Sudoku with reasoning traces, and Blocksworld. Compared with gold-only supervised fine-tuning, Pyligent improves solve rate by $72.7$ percentage points on hidden graphs, by $17$ and $18$ points on mixed and expert Sudoku, by $27$ and $14$ points on mixed and expert Sudoku with reasoning traces, and by $13$ points on Blocksworld. These results suggest that explicit failed-branch supervision can teach useful recovery behavior beyond imitation of polished solution chains.

</details>


### [128] [Do LLM-Generated Skills Make Better AI Data Scientists? A Component Ablation Across Data-Science Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07504)
*Wei-Jung Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether LLM-generated reusable skill files improve performance over simple task-only prompting across four data-science lifecycle stages. Through extensive ablation experiments (7,560 runs), the authors find no statistically significant improvement from any full or ablated generated skill variant, cautioning against using LLM-generated skills as a default single-shot prompting strategy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Expert-written reusable skill files encode high-quality guidance for recurring data-science tasks but create a manual curation bottleneck. The authors ask whether LLM-generated skills offer a low-curation alternative that reliably improves task performance over prompting with the task alone, potentially eliminating the need for expert authorship.

Method: The study tests LLM-generated skills across four data-science lifecycle stages: data preparation, data extraction, statistical analysis, and reporting. The main experiment ablates different skill components across 56 tasks, nine model configurations, and three providers (7,560 total runs). A supplemental token-matched control experiment (1,512 runs) compares full generated skills against task-irrelevant but skill-formatted content to isolate the effect of formatting vs. meaningful guidance.

Result: No full generated skill or ablated skill variant yields statistically significant performance improvement over No-Skill prompting. All p-values are at least 0.396, with a total performance spread across variants of only 1.2 percentage points. The token-matched control further reveals that full generated skills perform similarly to task-irrelevant content presented in skill format.

Conclusion: The results strongly caution against adopting one LLM-generated skill per data-science workflow as a default single-shot prompting strategy, as generated skills provide no reliable benefit and may simply consume context-window tokens without improving outcomes.

Abstract: Product data scientists often ask LLM-based agents to help with recurring execution tasks such as cleaning data, writing SQL, choosing statistical tests, and formatting results. Reusable skill files are meant to avoid prompting from scratch by packaging guidance for a task family. Expert-written skills can encode high-quality guidance, but writing and maintaining them across many data-science task families creates a manual bottleneck. We ask whether LLM-generated skills offer a useful low-curation alternative: do they improve performance over the task prompt alone? We test this question across four lifecycle stages: data preparation, data extraction, statistical analysis, and reporting, using one generated skill per stage. We find no reliable improvement from full generated skills over No-Skill prompting. We then ask whether any part of the skill is useful by ablating different skill components. The main ablation covers 56 tasks, nine model configurations, and three providers, yielding 7,560 runs. Compared with prompting using the task alone, neither the full generated skill nor any ablated skill variant significantly improves performance; all p-values are at least 0.396, and the total spread across variants is only 1.2 pp. A supplemental token-matched control adds 1,512 runs and finds that Full skills perform similarly to task-irrelevant skill-formatted content. The results caution against using one LLM-generated skill per data-science workflow as a default single-shot prompting strategy.

</details>


### [129] [Recursive Self-Improvement in AI: From Bounded Self-Refinement to Autonomous Research Loops](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07663)
*Mingguang Chen,Licheng Wang,Bo Qu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A taxonomy of AI self-improvement systems surveyed across 1,250 arXiv papers, distinguishing bounded self-refinement from open-ended recursive self-improvement (RSI). The framework organizes systems by what they improve (behavior, policy, evaluator, research) and degree of loop closure, with a dedicated focus on self-evaluation as the core claim of any improvement loop. A verification hierarchy from formal verifiers to intrinsic self-assessment predicts self-improvement strength and failure modes, with governance-grade measurement identified as a critical gap.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The vocabulary of AI self-improvement ("self-refine," "self-reward," "self-play," "self-evolve") conflates fundamentally different ambitions, obscuring critical distinctions between safe, convergent industrial practices and speculative open-ended recursive self-improvement. The field lacks a shared taxonomy to organize work, identify failure modes, and inform safety/governance discussions.

Method: A systematic survey of 1,250 arXiv papers (2024-2026) organized along two primary axes: (1) the target of improvement (deployment behavior, training policy, evaluator, research process) and (2) degree of loop closure (human-in-the-loop to fully closed). The authors introduce a dedicated evaluator design space taxonomy covering judges, process reward models, verifiers, rubrics, and meta-evaluation, and construct a verification hierarchy ordering signals from formal verifiers (strongest) to intrinsic self-assessment (weakest).

Result: Demonstrated self-improvement strength tracks the verification hierarchy: systems relying on stronger verification signals (formal verifiers, ground-truth checks) achieve more reliable improvement, while weaker signals (intrinsic self-assessment) exhibit self-confirming loops, model collapse, and diversity collapse. The "research direction-setting" bottleneck — the point where humans remain essential — sits at the top of this hierarchy. No surveyed system achieves fully closed recursive self-improvement; all remain bounded by grounding requirements, collapse dynamics, and compute constraints.

Conclusion: Bounded self-refinement is convergent, evaluable, and already industrial practice, while open-ended RSI remains speculative and bounded on every measured axis. The verification hierarchy both predicts improvement reliability and explains failure modes. Governance-grade measurement of self-improvement capabilities is identified as the field's most underpopulated niche, with implications for safety monitoring and regulatory frameworks.

Abstract: AI systems increasingly participate in their own improvement: revising their outputs, adapting their own harnesses during deployment, training on data they generate, and, increasingly, conducting AI research itself. This literature is described under a vocabulary ("self-refine," "self-reward," "self-play," "self-evolve") that conflates fundamentally different ambitions. We survey 1,250 arXiv papers (2024-2026) along two axes: what the system improves -- its behavior in deployment, its policy through training, its evaluator, or the research process itself -- and the degree of loop closure (human-in-the-loop to fully closed). The taxonomy separates bounded self-refinement -- convergent, evaluable, and already industrial practice -- from open-ended recursive self-improvement (RSI), which remains bounded by grounding requirements, collapse dynamics, and compute constraints on every measured axis. Its distinctive feature is a dedicated category for self-evaluation: every improvement loop is a claim that some signal can substitute for human judgment. We survey the evaluator design space -- judges, process reward models, verifiers, rubrics, meta-evaluation -- order the signals into a verification hierarchy from formal verifiers (strongest) to intrinsic self-assessment (weakest), and observe that demonstrated self-improvement strength tracks this hierarchy, that its failure modes (self-confirming loops, model collapse, diversity collapse) follow from its violations, and that the "research direction-setting" bottleneck keeping humans in the loop sits at the top of that hierarchy. We connect the technical literature to the theory of RSI limits and to the safety and governance questions raised by frontier-lab accounts of closing the loop, and identify governance-grade measurement of self-improvement as the field's most underpopulated niche.

</details>
